## F: flying-camp–fuzzweed

### flying-camp
 
 <i>n.</i> a fly-camp, a temporary camp or bivouac

>  <b>PASCOE</b> I had a mental debate last night as to whether to shift all my Camp up as far as the foot of the Saddle, if there is one - or leave most of it here and take a flying camp and see what was ahead ... it just becomes a matter of site [sic] up all night keeping the fire warm, when a fellow has only a Flying Camp, a Fly and a single blanket. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,153</i>) [NOTES: 1891 May 27 Douglas Report to Roberts in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 153/4]

>  <b>WILMOT</b> From this main line I made several branch lines, taking out flying-camps, and ascending a good many hill-tops, so as to obtain the best views of the country. <i>(1897,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Wilmot's Journal in 1957 Reed and Reed Farthest West 179]

### flying-fish weather
 
 <i>phr.</i> ? cf. soldiers' weather/wind [(sailing) ca1933]

### flyline
 
 <i>n.</i> a narrow road

>  Narrow roads (called `Flylines') are cleared at intervals of 3 chains in the swamp, for access and removal of the leaves. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,550</i>)

### Flymo toe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  National Party presidency hopeful Geoff Thompson has suffered the indignity of a "Flymo toe"... [He] slipped while mowing a bank.., chopped off a big toe and damaged another toe next to it... Mr Thompson said the injury is quite common, hence the name, Flymo toe, given it by hospital staff. <i>(Mar 22, 1994,Evening Post,2</i>)

### flystrike
 
 <i>n.</i> a blowfly egg or maggot infestation of sheep. [Check NZ Dept Ag Bulletin 12 1907]

>  From a study of a mild season's fly-strike amongst the Merinos, half-breds, and crossbreds of Marlborough, the following results were obtained ... <i>(1953,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/212A</i>)

>  fly-strike NZ & Austral. infestation of sheep by blowfly maggots; fly-struck (of sheep) so infested. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,290</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Fly strike this season was the worst in 30 years ... Dipping is effective against flies and larvae, but the majority of sheep affected ... had not been dipped ... Fly-struck sheep do not eat properly, and die if they are not treated. <i>(May 26, 1986,Manawatu Evening Standard,8</i>)

>  Birdsfoot trefoil.. is to be used in a project to fight flystrike and prevent dagginess in sheep. <i>(May 23, 1997,Dominion,8</i>)

>  The lack of wool means no flystrike or dags. <i>(Oct 30, 2002,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  Marlborough may be classified into four regions of fly strike. <i>(Nov 1938,NZ Journal of Agriculture,388</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an infestation of sheep by flies.

>  Goats are susceptible to lice but not fly strike. <i>(Mar 9, 1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar</i>) [NOTES: 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

>  Poor flystrike control can arise for many reasons including incorrect dosage, OP resistance or dirty sheep to name a few. <i>(Dec 21, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

>  Flystrike is an affliction you would not wish on any creature ... <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 50</i>)

### flystruck
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  fly-strike NZ & Austral. infestation of sheep by blowfly maggots; fly-struck (of sheep) so infested <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,290</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was surprised by the number of maggot-ridden sheep in Australia. The majority of wethers were fly struck around the pizzle. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,117</i>)

>  Fly strike this season was the worst in 30 years ... Dipping is effective against flies and larvae, but the majority of sheep affected ... had not been dipped ... Fly-struck sheep do not eat properly, and die if they are not treated. <i>(May 26, 1986,Manawatu Evening Standard,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] afflicted with fly eggs and maggots on their dungy rear ends, or in lacerated areas.

>  Maggo was named in the article because it is the best-known treatment for flystruck sheep, not because it poses unusual risks. <i>(2000,Wool Grower,Spring 5</i>)

>  Only 3% of lambs with no dags at all got flystruck. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

### FMC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Federated Mountain Clubs of New Zealand

### foal-getter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Well-known Powerful Draught Stallion ATHELSTANE, WILL SERVE MARES THIS SEASON. ... He has proved in Otago what he proved in Nelson, that he is a sure foal-getter. <i>(Mar 30, 1861,Otago Witness,8</i>)

### foaling alarm
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a transmitter fixed to a mare's collar that triggers an alarm if the mare lies down to foal.

>  <b>GORDON</b> A recent invention can, however, save a lot of bleary eyes and night shifts - the foaling alarm. It's a battery-powered transmitter that's fixed to a collar round the mare's neck so that when she lies down to foal, it triggers an alarm beside Nick's bed. The alarm not only eliminates night staff, but puts a lot less stress on the mare. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,164</i>)

### foaling paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock close to a studmaster's home on a stud horse-breeding property.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Once they start to 'bag up' (make milk) they come into foaling paddocks beside the house, and since 90 per cent of all mares foal at night, there's a fair bit of midnight oil burnt in looking after them. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,164</i>)

### foamer
 
 <i>n.</i> a trainspotter (See [also foamist](../A#also-foamist))

>  Enthusiasts are also aware that the locomotive drivers call them Foamers - because they are often seen alongside the track foaming at the mouth. <i>(Apr 10, 2005,Sunday Star Times,3 Escape</i>)

### foamist
 
 <i>n.</i> a trainspotter

>  They're called foamists because rumour has it they foam at the mouth at the sight of a train coming down the track. <i>(Jun 26, 2005,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Marcus Lush]

### fob
 
 <i>n.</i> acronym for "fresh off the boat", used especially of Pacific Islanders in New Zealand [Harry Orsman has picked this up referring to Caribbean immigrants in an English thriller]

>  <b>McGILL</b> fob Samoan; street talk. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)

>  A white boy and a brown boy, palagi and fob (Fresh Off the Boat) meet and become firm friends... There are third-generation kids who still think they are fobs... I mix my fob accent with Serbian. <i>(Mar 31, 2004,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  The family immigrated to New Zealand in 1974... "We looked so fresh, wearing our lavalavas. I remember feeling alien and being called 'Fob' and 'Bunga'. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,11</i>)

>  They just assume I'm one of the FOBs [fresh off the boats]. <i>(Nov 1991,Metro,116</i>)

>  Then with FOB shirt on and my lavalava wrapped, I headed with my crew to the Hawaiian night. <i>(May 1, 2000,Salient,7 : 24</i>)

>  Like a lot of fobs - fresh-off-the-boat Islanders - we had problems adjusting. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 15</i>)

>  The design for Fab washing powder has been changed to Fob - the derogatory term for Pacific Island immigrants Fresh Off The Boat. <i>(Aug 16, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Sigeyuki Kihara started playing around with well known logos in 1996, when she corrupted a Fab 2 logo on a t-shirt to read Ultra FOB Total (fresh off the boat, geddit?)... Witness a display of t-shirts bearing the words.. Barbie, repackaged as a full-bodied brown babe called FOBIE. <i>(Aug 16, 2000,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  So it's brave and cheeky that Kihara has printed T-shirts showing the Colonel sporting the initials "KKK" rather than "KFC". Tip-Top becomes "Hip-Hop" and Fab, the washing powder, becomes "Fob." <i>(Apr 15, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

### fobby
 
 <i>adj.</i> [also fob-y] (See [fob](fly-fuz.md#fob))

>  <b>Maepu</b> She had a fobby accent because she was born and raised in Tonga and English was her second language. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,19</i>)

>  They break all the "fobby" stereotypes of what Pacific people in this country are supposed to be like. They don't wear mumus or tupenu to their ankles. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.victoria.ac.nz/modernletters/bnzp/2005/mila-note]

>  And if fobby translation or poor spelling is funny to you too, check out Engrish.com ... <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.shotthen.com/category/the-halfcast-broadcast]

>  We were very fobby - fresh off the boat. <i>(1998,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Used by Samoan-born NZer. From FOB for "Fresh off the Boat" used off newly-arrived Islanders.]

>  For example, you see a European couple tallking to each other, and it doesn't bother anyone, but when you see an Asian couple talking in 'FOB-y English', it's all of a sudden a joy to laugh at. <i>(Jul 2005,Tearaway,4</i>)

### focus farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm property where production and progress are monitored by a group of farmers and agribusiness interest groups.

>  The Hocken's 460ha Richmond Downs property at Tarata is one of 60 focus farms being used to develop the specifications of a new quality assurance programme for the meat industry. As part of the process a number of field days are being held on the focus farms. <i>(Nov 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  .. the property is a focus farm in the Westland dairy company's monitoring series. <i>(Jun 1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,98</i>)

>  As a presenter at focus farm field days, Mary-Anne wants farmers to learn well. <i>(1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,102</i>)

>  The O'Briens only began the drainage technique during their time as a focus farm. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

>  .. the Wilsons have allowed their property to become a focus farm for a group of King Country farmers and other professionals associated with the rural community. <i>(Sep 16, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  'Being the focus farm keeps you on the ball,' says Alan. <i>(1999,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)

### focus farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer whose property is a focus farm for agribusiness monitoring.

>  .. he admits to being sceptical when he was initially invited to be a focus farmer. <i>(Nov 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### fodder pay/fodder wage
 
 <i>n.</i> a subsistence wage [(waterfront obs.)]

>  While the work is very patchy/ On the waterfront today,/ You've not much to come and go on/ When you draw your `fodder pay.'/ <i>Ibid.</i> 27 You've got to tighten up your belt/ While you're on the `fodder' wage. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,9</i>)

### FOG
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Formation of Government

>  Now we're into what groovy guys at Parliament like to call FOG: Formation of Government. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### fog
 
 <i>v.</i> euphemism for fuck

>  <b>HELMER</b> `Wull, fog my bloody days, eh? A joker no sooner gets comfortable than I looks up ... at the rear end of a dirty big stag ...' <i>(1964,Stag Party,81</i>)

### fog belt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and hunting] the layer of fog that settles in high altitude valleys and peaks.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Fog belts are seldom deeper than a thousand feet but they can easily blanket out the whole area. <i>(1964,Deer Shooting Days,11</i>)

### foggy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> The South Island in an occasional `North Island' season still suffers from rank, foggy feed, and in such season hogget ill-thrift is rampant. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,336,1970 ed.</i>)

### fogue
 
 <i>v.</i> have a strong or objectionable odour

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fogue. To have a strong or objectionable odour: New Zealanders' (-1935). Perhaps ex fug, fuggy. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,294</i>)

### foil
 
 <i>n.</i> a small amount of cannabis wrapped in tinfoil, a tinny.

### FOL
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Federation of Labour

>  F.O.L. N.Z. abbrev. for Federation of Labour. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 291; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 191]]

>  Putting aside the two most difficult points for the unions, contestability under the guise of a provision for union coverage and demarcation ... the FOL has fared relatively well. <i>(Oct 3, 1986,National Business Review,14</i>)

>  FOL secretary Ken Douglas would not comment yesterday however, beyond confirming that the FOL was `aware of the development'. <i>(Aug 26, 1987,National Business Review,1</i>)

>  The FOL, the central union organisation for private sector unions from 1937, has been replaced by the new umbrella organisation, the New Zealand Council of Trade Unions, encompassing both private and State-sector unions and a number of previously unaffiliated unions. <i>(May 10, 1988,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  FOL (now CTU) policy did not operate for seamen at that time. <i>(Dec 3, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  In 1950, the watersiders had withdrawn from the Federation of Labour (FOL), along with the other militant unions who opposed arbitration, which the FOL supported. <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### fold
 
 <i>n.</i> a river wave

>  The precious "fold," which keeps the kayaker on the wave, is always flexing and surging, sometimes there, sometimes not. <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/81</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to roll fleecewool.

>  <b>Tripp</b> Commenced shearing at Ashburton. Abner, Harry, Ross, C Smith shearing. J Robinson folding fleeces. <i>(Feb 19, 1858,Diary,33</i>) [NOTES: in Harper (1967) The Kettle on the Fuchsia]

### folding koha
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka folded note koha]

>  <b>BAND</b>, Caro and Nga Hoa, local artists playing a diverse range of easy listening music, originals and covers. 7.30pm, Lembas Cafe, Poplar Ave, Raumati Soouth, folded note koha. <i>(May 13, 2010,Horowhenua Mail,37</i>)

>  <i>(May 13, 2010,Horowhenua Mail,37</i>) [NOTES: <b>PLAY</b>, The Threepenny Opera, 7pm, Te Ra School hall, Poplar Ave, Raumati South, folding koha, also Saturday and Sunday.]

### folding wedges
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Wedges, folding-</i> A pair of wedges driven in opposite directions and sliding on each other; used to firm up concrete formwork supports and the like. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,129</i>)

### follies
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> Marble games, with terminology far too extensive to go into, included follies, the simplest, or holes, in which one had to roll a marble into the last of a series of holes, called poison, before attacking an opponent's taw. <i>(1996,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

### follow the drag
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to chase the scent with hounds in a drag hunt.

>  <b>KERR</b> In 1876 the Christchurch Hunt Club first brought their hounds to South Canterbury and the public was invited to 'follow the drag' at Washdyke. Nearly 500 spectators and eighty or ninety riders accepted the invitation. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,397</i>)

### follower
 
 <i>n.</i> a dog

>  <b>STRONACH</b> One good dog and four `followers' will not see a man through, no matter how good he is. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,46</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [pasture management] a farm animal used to graze leftover pasture.

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Ewes and cattle can be used as 'followers' to ensure full feed utilisation. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,111</i>)

>  It might be inferred from the above that the sheep are there as followers in an intensive grazing system, but this is not so; they are killers, bought at a low price. <i>(Apr 19, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,36</i>)

### follows
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In the nineteenth century the ring game seems to have been the most popular marble game. (In more recent years marble games with holes have had the greater role.) There was Big Ring, in which a circle a yard or more in diameter was drawn on the ground; there was the mullibar variation, in which an outsize taw was catapulted amongst the marbles in this circle. There were Little Ring, Liney, Follows, Follow the Taw, Holey, and then, on a less important scale, Butcher's Board, in which the marbles were bowled through the holes in a board, Eye-Drop, Knock-Backs, Leggings-Out (or Leggin's-Out), Span-'Em, and Backits. The last was more usually a girls' game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### FoMA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Federation of Maori Authorities [ORIGIN: acronym, (from the website Feb 2009) FoMA in Brief - The Federation is easily New Zealand's largest Maori business network with a voluntary membership of Maori Incorporations, Maori Land Trusts, Maori Trusts Boards and Runanga, and emerging communally owned entities. The mission of the network is to progressively foster and promote the development, sound management and the economic advancement of Maori Authorities and in turn the wider Maori communities they benefit.] (See [www.foma.co.nz](../W#www.foma.co.nz))

### fondlepiece
 
 <i>n.</i> [National Radio 3/2/03]

### fong
 
 <i>n.</i> booze [f. fonged]; occas. as in 1974 cit. meths as a drink

>  <b>GEBBIE & MCGREGOR</b> ... a group of regular drinkers set up a kitty to pay for taxis home. ... One of the farsighted drinkers threw a dollar to the `fong [90] fund' and made the comment, `Tell me I'm not fit to drive and I'll use that to get a taxi home.' <i>(1979,Incredible 8-Ounce Dream,89</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> It was so much easier [in the 1930s] to pay sixpence for a bottle of methylated spirits, commonly referred to as fong, drown your sorrows and lose yourself in a world of fantasy. <i>(1974,Loner, The,39</i>)

>  ... on the odd occasion he wouldn't front up in the morning. I always assumed it was because he'd attacked the fong (booze) the night before. <i>(Sep 1985,Metro,47</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fong methylated spirits. [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Everybody as full as a bull on the bus back from the game and they stopped for a leak ... and Fong Needham fell down the bank ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,174</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> `Old Bell called them a liar.' Dinny suddenly chuckled. `'Course, the boys had been stretching things a bit, and the old boy was pretty fonged up. ...' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,34</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> The "fong" experts were not encouraged by the genuine talent and were usually avoided. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,177</i>)

>  "Fong" or "fong-eye" was popular about the same time, to mean booze in general, but since this was 1950s New Zealand, in practice it meant only beer... Fong and its derivatives are rather unusual in New Zealand slang in not being shared with Australia. <i>(Nov 17, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### fong-eye
 
 <i>n.</i> booze (esp. be on the fong-eye) [ca1945 D. Singe (used on the East Coast, North Island) (rare)] (See [whangaied](../W#whangaied))

### fonged
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk [ca1945 Wellington Students]

>  <b>WILSON</b> It occurred to him that Harry was fonged already. The man scarcely showed it but for his more florid speech and a slight blurring of his words. <i>(1965,Outcasts,50</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `I'm a bit fonged, actually. I wouldn't jabber away like this if I wasn't half cut. You sure it'll be jake with Ron?' <i>Ibid.</i> 48 `I like you when you're fonged, dear Martha! You always speak the truth then.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,43</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fonged drunk... fonged up drunk, cluttered, messed up, bewildered, stymied; combining fog and pong; eg `He's all fonged up about whether or not she like him.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)

>  When she fell off the wagon and got fonged while performing on stage.., she informed the entire audience that he'd done her wrong. <i>(Dec 5, 1998,Dominion,23</i>)

>  When Joe Niland came to New Zealand in 1950 he found people using the words "fonged" and "half-fonged" to describe men in complete or partial states of drunkenness. Now, he reports, he never hears it. <i>(Nov 17, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  I'd asssumed that once I hit 40, I would never get fonged. <i>(Jun 8, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

### fonged-up
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  "Fonged-up", for some reason, meant being in a mess, not necessarily because of beer. <i>(Nov 17, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### fono
 
 <i>n.</i> a meeting of village leaders in Samoa and other Pacific Islands; in NZ a conference or hui on Pacific issues [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  Capital and Coast District Health Board has organised 11 public meetings as well as two hui with Maori and two fono with Pacific Islands people. <i>(Jun 28, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  In Samoa, kava is ceremoniously dispensed at the beginning of a fono, or meeting of village leaders. <i>(Sep 21, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Health issues of special concern to older Pacific people are discussed at fono held twice a year in Christchurch. The last fono...attracted over 50 people, including 33 older Pacific people (matua). <i>(Feb 2008,Keeping On,57:18</i>)

### Fonterra
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the national dairy processing and marketing organisation, formed from the amalgamation of Kiwi Co-operative Dairies, New Zealand Dairy Group and the Dairy Board in 2001. [NZ's largest corporation].

>  Fonterra, it's not the name of some Italian brothel, but that of New Zealand's biggest corporate enitity. <i>(Sep 3, 2001,Rural News,3</i>)

>  RD1 is a wholly owned subsidiary of Fonterra co-operative group and operates 51 dairy specialist rural retail outlets. <i>(Jun 12, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

### foo
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [1984 14 F E126Wgton Girls C. 5 FOO [exclam. of annoyance] -->1984 14 F E126Wgton Girls C. 8 FOO [exclamation of approval] -->1984 14 F E126Wgton Girls C. 35 FOO [of approval]]

### Food for Britain
 
 <i>n.</i> a WWII campaign to send food to the UK

>  I was Mrs Eleanor Sievwright's hardresser and she organised a Miss New Zealand show in benefit of 'Food for Britain'. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/45</i>)

### food runner
 
 <i>n.</i> a restaurant assistant

>  Experienced front of house staff and food runners for busy daytime restaurant. <i>(Oct 28, 2005,NZ Herald,C9</i>) [NOTES: Ad.]

### foofoo valve
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED says foofoo (1826) is a kind of West African dough made from plantains. Partridge says a foofoo valve is 'a mythical "gadget" that's always blamed for any mechanical breakdown' but is RN slang dating back to around 1910. The phrase was common in the 1940s in NZ but was usually used in the figurative in the context of telling someone to take things steady - "Go easy or you'll bust your foofoo valve".]

>  He said that a good run cleared the foofoo valve and insisted whenever I fell or sprained my ankle that I got up and kept running. <i>(Sep 1998,North & South,61</i>)

### foofter
 
 <i>n.</i> a gentle fart; ? variant of more common fluff [ca1943, probably echoic or variant of poofter.]

### fooster
 
 <i>v.</i> to mess around, muck about [also @n foosterer; ca1950s at least : Society of Mary fide Dr F.M.McKay (15/7/86) e.g. stop foostering about (cf EDD Fooster to fuss (Irish) (? influenced by fuss) - a variant or elab.]

### foot
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [fencing] to place a piece of wood or other solid material at the foot of a post to ensure it remains vertical.

>  <b>McKenny</b> ... When wiring on stones or iron to 'foot' posts it is preferable to fasten them on by passing the wire just through the hole ... <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,43</i>)

>  A reader from Warkworth, Northland, is seeking information on footing posts in a fence across swampy ground. Excepting in really steep dips, driven posts are seldom footed, though usually an extra long post is used in place of a footed post. <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  <b>Crump</b> Then for three weeks they dug post - and strainer - holes, ran out wires, footed posts, blocked and stayed the angles, ... <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,126</i>)

>  <b>Mowat</b> This is a very slow fence to erect as there are so many posts to be footed. <i>(Oct 1, 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  The stay is also constructed of concrete "footed" by the concrete block shown in the right-hand bottom corner of Fig.3. <i>(Aug 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,101</i>)

>  <b>Gascoigne</b> Now I maintain that your strainers should be footed so there's no chance of lift. Put in an eight-foot stay, block it firm and nothing will shift. Half the posts in the hollows here were never footed and they've lifted out and the sheep were walkin' through. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,105</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a block of wood attached by wire to a post and buried with the post in the posthole; or some other device to stop the post being pulled out when the fence is strained

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> Northland Posts in a dip are called `foot-posts' because they require `footing' to prevent their lifting as wires are strained. A `foot' is a buried block of wood fastened either to the post or to the wires. <i>(1951,Letter</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> In the gullies you have to attach a `foot' to a post - a piece of timber or rock firmly slotted and wired into the bottom end. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,157</i>)

>  Ross prepared a `foot' to wedge into the base of the post. This was done with his well-used rammer. The `foot' was a solid piece of totara with a length of No8 wire stapled to it. <i>(Mar 21, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  Foot: a block of wood attached by wire to a post and buried with the post in the posthole, or some other device to stop the post being pulled out when the fence is strained. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> super foot; a way of measuring timber

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> He paid ten shillings per hundred feet for it, the best of heart rimu timber! <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,16</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [fencing] a piece of wood or other solid material buried at the foot of a post to ensure that it remains vertical.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> foot: attachment on the bottom of a post to prevent it from lifting. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> I put in a foot under the fence between Killing Paddock and Front Face as sheep kept on getting through. <i>(1943,Alexander Mowat Diaries,8</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> In the gullies you have to attach a 'foot' to a post - a piece of timber or rock firmly slotted and wired into the bottom end. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,157</i>)

>  Ross prepared a "foot" to wedge into the base of the post ... The "foot" was a solid piece of totara with a length of No8 wire stapled to it. <i>(Mar 21, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Foot: a block of wood attached by wire to a post and buried with the post in a post-hole, or some other device to stop the post being pulled out when the fence is strained. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)

### foot candy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Fashion editor Stacy Gregg's choice of what's often an outrageously priced and impractical piece of foot candy has the power to get the tills ringing... <i>(Jun 26, 2005,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),8</i>)

### foot muster
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gathering of sheep on high country that is too steep for horses.

>  <b>MOMATUIK & EASTCOTT</b> Autumn means arduous foot musters, starting before dawn, often ending in pouring rain and a howling nor'wester. <i>(1980,High Country,22</i>)

### foot shooter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a deer culler who hunts on the ground rather than from the air.

>  <b>CURTIS</b> I think a fair example of animal populations and the difficulty of control by limited numbers of foot shooters is illustrated in the history of West Coast deer-culling. <i>(1997,Murphy's Law and the Hunter,124</i>)

### foot-flusher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "I'm a foot-flusher"... A foot-flusher is a person who, thinking that nasty germs might lurk on the thing you push to flush the toilet, uses a foot to push it instead of a hand. <i>(Mar 28, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),3</i>)

### foot-rot
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a disease of cow's feet [known elsewhere as ''foul-in-the-foot''.]

>  Three of my cows are badly affected with foot-rot. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,252</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to pare and treat the infected feet of sheep.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Over to shed & footrotted the balance of our old stud ewes. <i>(Jan 12, 1941,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Got in 32 sheep from reserve. One dead one. Plucked him; footrotted and bluestoned 8. <i>(Jul 1, 1893,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Footrotted rams and put them in Toi paddock. <i>(Nov 19, 1915,Alexander Mowat Diaries,.</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> .. he and Donald between them cut gorse with me joining them at times when I wasn't footrotting ewes. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,70</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> I killed sheep, did the dagging, footrotted sheep and branded cattle. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,148</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a disease of sheep's feet caused by a micro-organism Fuciformis nodosus, and often Spirochaeta penortha, resulting in lameness, and eventual starvation.

>  <b>EDGAR</b> Foot rot is a very common infection of sheep's feet, with as much as 30 per cent of a flock affected at a time. Two bacteria, Dichelobacter nodosus and Fusobacterium necrophorum, operating together cause foot rot. <i>(1959,NZ Farmers' Veterinary Guide,108</i>)

>  Romney Marsh are perhaps engaging more attention ... not liable to take foot-rot on the dampest bottoms. <i>(Nov 30, 1867,Weekly News,18</i>)

>  Foot-rot is a disease which has become exceedingly common and may cause considerable financial loss. <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,4</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> If any cases of foot-rot are found, the whole bought-in mob should be kept in isolation for a period sufficiently long to ensure that all have sound, uninfected feet. Not until the hill-country breeding farms eradicate foot-rot will its complete control on fat-lamb farms become a practical possibility. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,119</i>)

### foot-rot trough
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A branding-race might.. have a foot-rot trough installed in the bottom of it. <i>(Aug 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,140</i>)

>  At times foot-rot troughs are at the bottom of the drafting race and covered over.. when not in use. <i>(Sep 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,229</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a foot bath in which sheep are treated with bluestone or a proprietary product against foot-rot.

>  Those farmers who had tried the cross [Corriedale-Romney] had to instal foot-rot troughs ... <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1311</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The yards may also incorporate a foot-rot bath or trough. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,335</i>)

### foot-rotter
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheephandler who pares the infected feet of sheep.

>  <b>FISHER</b> Ralph's attitude towards the 'drifters' gangs of scrubcutters, shearers, crutchers, footrotters, dippers, fencers and dockers was quite different. <i>(1978,Dolphins and Killer Whales,45</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep suffering from footrot.

>  B.H mustered rams and got in 89 long wools and 30 Merino footrotters. <i>(Apr 21, 1889,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  THS and Blair mustered Natural paddock and drafted off hoggetts [sic] and some footrotters. <i>(May 1, 1889,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

### foot-rotting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Jack was constantly on the farm, `mouthing ewes in the [136] yards', stooking with the children in the oats paddock, `foot-rotting the cull half-bred ewes', or `shifting sheep'. <i>(1985,Coldstream,135,1985 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1913 diary in Studholme Coldstream (1985) 135-136]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Foot-rotting was a horribly monotonous job! <i>Ibid.</i> 116 116 Foot-rotting was a horribly monotonous job after one had been at it for weeks, and it was a great relief when the trouble disappeared for the time being. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,100,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> We did quite a lot of foot-rotting, which is a job I dislike as much as dagging. All lame sheep were driven into the woolshed for treatment ... their hoofs pared down and the rot cut out. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,195</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> A little stock work, the drafting and picking of lambs for despatch to the works or sale yards, some help if foot-rotting or dipping was in progress. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,11</i>)

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> `Do? What do you do? ... You know the range of things: footrotting to ocean racer crewing.' <i>(1986,O.E.,22</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the pruning and treating of decaying sheep's hooves.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Foot-rotting was a horribly monotonous job after one had been at it for weeks. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,116</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> They involve the use of secateurs for what is commonly known as footrotting, and either shears or machines for dagging. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,80</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Another unpleasant task is foot-rotting - that is, pruning decaying flesh off a sheep's hooves. The foot-rotting job is a tiring one, with kicks to avoid and every hoof requiring close inspection. [footrotting] <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,83</i>)

### foot-rotty
 
 <i>adj.</i> [(of sheep)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> .. the infection is carried by foot-rotty sheep. <i>Ibid.</i>116 A lot of the infection is carried by foot-rotty sheep being put into saleyards and leaving the germ there ready to be carried away by the next mob that is put in the pen. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,100</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> It's not skittles and beer when you strike a wet year/ Following some foot-rotty flock. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,75</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] suffering from footrot.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> A lot of infection is carried by foot-rotty sheep being put into saleyards and leaving the germ there ready to be carried away by the next mob that is put in the pen. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,116</i>)

>  Culling a footrotty sheep each year won't help a jot. <i>(Nov 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

### foot-scald
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep] a bacterial disease of the feet.

>  <b>MAF</b> Foot rot and foot scald - bacterial diseases of the feet. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,50</i>)

>  The lucerne-fed ewes wintered well so far as feeding of dry lucerne was concerned, but foot-scald caused a great deal of trouble, and the ewes had to be moved to other pastures from time to time. On the grass-only area no difficulties were encountered, either from foot-scald or at lambing. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,123</i>)

### foot-scalded
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] with damaged hooves.

>  <b>COWAN</b> About fifty of the rest were foot-scalded from standing in their own urine for so long. <i>(1998,Goodness Gracious Me,146</i>)

### foot-track
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a walking path for pedestrians or trampers, a Maori trail or hobnail trail.

>  The road from the Lower to the Upper Anatoki is now so far complete that the foot-track is passable all the way. <i>(Apr 10, 1858,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  He has also prepared a plan and specification for what is familiarly known as "Jacob's Ladder," or the steep foot-track leading from the head of Gabriels to the Blue Spur. <i>(Dec 11, 1878,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

### football
 
 <i>n.</i> rugby union, in most parts of NZ (except possibly the West Coast where it often = league) until, perhaps, the late 1970s or early 80s, when soccer came into public notice

>  <b>MER EDITH</b> [Rugby Union & attrib] Speaking of football, I have joined the football club. ...He was chasing the smallest member of the club, who was running with the ball; ... <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,59,1935 ed.</i>)

>  [A `Football Column' written by `Pakeha'] <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/7</i>)

>  <b>INGRAM</b> ... the afternoon was spent in watching the 1st. and 2nd. Batalion football reps. in action against each other. <i>(Sep 16, 1917,Anzac Diary,44,1987 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> He ... talked football to Tommy at supper, after which he addressed his sisters. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,27</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> If he had been an English boy, he would have said `Rugby' or `Rugger', according to his status. In New Zealand you talk of `Football' and you mean `Rugby Football.' If you mean anything else you say `association' or whatnot. <i>(1936,Poor Scholar,65</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Football was in the ascendancy, for New Zealand players to the old Rugby Game, and hard training in spare time was the order of the day. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,66</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> There was a time when no end-of-year function of a local body, or A & P dinner or football club `smoko' was complete without a toast to `the press' ... <i>(1981,Straight from the Shoulder,23</i>)

>  football: when used alone denotes Rugby football; otherwise the terms `soccer' or `Rugby league' are used; e.g., `Do you play football?' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  In the country districts, more interest is this year being taken in the game of football than has ever been the case before. <i>(Jul 3, 1890,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Football had not really come into its own in those early days but in July 1885 a match was played between Hawke's Bay and Poverty Bay: Hawke's Bay 9, Poverty Bay 0. <i>(1958,Story of Hawke's Bay, The,256</i>)

>  A meeting of gentlemen interested in getting up a Football Club was held last night, at the Branch Hotel. <i>(May 13, 1871,Evening Post,00</i>) [NOTES: Check pagination]



 
 <i>n.</i> i.e. rugby union football [attrib. (football match esp.)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Yes, but it was Wilkinson who showed the football brains today. ...' `Yeah, he's got the footie brains all right. He would have been an All Black if he'd played in a North Island union.' <i>Ibid.</i> 166 `Damned dirty play I call it. ... ' `Aw no, he was just using his head. Football brains, that's what it was.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,99</i>)

>  <b>GILKISON</b> For some years my natural [climbing] mediocrity has been intensified by the particular handicap of a `football ankle.' <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,14</i>) [NOTES: formed on tennis elbow, house maid's knee, etc.]

>  <b>MANDER</b> The bay was now a township. It had its own post office, its little public school, its town hall, its football field. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,198,1960 ed.</i>)

>  A football match of some interest will be played at the Park Oval.. tomorrow. <i>(Mar 8, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  The football match, New Zealand against the World, which was postponed from the 29th ult., was played on Saturday at the Barracks. <i>(May 10, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,4</i>)

>  Someone asked Mrs Baldon who was going to win the interprovincial football match. <i>(May 27, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  FOOTBALL MATCH - The College v. Town. <i>(May 17, 1870,Colonist (Nelson),00</i>) [NOTES: Check pagination]

>  <i>(1884,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: Check issue & pagination]

>  It has long been felt that Masterton and Palmerston North.. are too far distant as footballing centres, and the Clubs mentioned.. now have an institution closer at hand to foster football interests in the district. <i>(Apr 23, 1890,Woodville Examiner,00</i>) [NOTES: Check pagination]

>  The first football match of the season was played last Saturday afternoon on the Government ground in rear of the barracks, and was well attended by both spectators and players. <i>(May 22, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the drug Temazepam

### Football Ferns
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ international women's football team (new in 2007, replacing Swanz q.v.)

New Zealand Football's head of women's football, former international Michelle Cox, said what was sought was a name that was "sassy and upbeat". <i>(Sep 2007,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.yellowfever.co.nz]

 <i>n.</i> a chronic knee injury from playing rugby; cf. football ankle

>  <b>SLATTER</b> .... chaps with cauliflower ears from the scrum or football-knee from the heavy tackle. The duelling scars of the New Zealander. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,74</i>)

### footballer
 
 <i>n.</i> a rugby player

>  <b>SEDDON</b> I had become a footballer. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,26</i>)

### footboard
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> FOOTBOARD: Wide board at schooner driver's feet. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,186</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 186]

### footed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fenceposts and battens] stengthened with a foot or deadman.

>  Excepting in really steep dips, driven posts are seldom footed, though usually an extra long post is used in place of a footed post. <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Put in a few footed battens and a few posts. <i>(Oct 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries,5</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> The use of chains on tie-downs, where footed posts are normally used, has become popular and effective. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,193</i>)

### footer
 
 <i>n.</i> rugby [-->1951 14-15 M 33 Wellington H.S. 20 FOOTER (2) -->1953 15 M A28 Thames DHS 20 FOOTER -->1953 16 M A32 Thames DHS 21 FOOTER]

>  In his leisure hours he was an enthusiastic "footer" man. <i>(Jan 18, 1918,Press, The,8</i>)

### foothill country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) down lands of high country [particularly close to the base of the Southern Alps.]

>  An important feature of the hill country is the abrupt commencement of the high hills and the small amount of foothill country... Between Mt Somers and the Rangitata River a little more foothill country is present. The total number of sheep grazing the whole area, including the foothill mountain country mentioned above is about 173,000. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,99</i>)

>  <b>ELLMERS</b> In this foothill country .. were gracious well-cared-for farm homesteads. <i>(1993,Schoolhouse Under the Sky,8</i>)

### foothills farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a downcountry farmer [usually on the foothills of the South Island high country.]

>  One foothills farmer had lost 200 acres of young grass. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

### footing
 
 <i>n.</i> (fencing) a foot or the material used for a foot

>  Footing: Same as foot. It may describe the material used for the foot. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a piece of wood or rock used to support a fence-post.

>  Strainers, angle posts, footings and so on need to be put in as carefully as for any other fences. <i>(Dec 1970,NZ Dairy Exporter,15</i>)

### footpad
 
 <i>n.</i> one who tramps the roads; a swagman

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> FOOTPAD: One who tramps the roads a swagman. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,186</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 186]

### footpath
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A foot-path Kape[acute]kka <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/334</i>) [NOTES: (English-NZ Vocabulary)]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> At this point a native foot-path winds up the western hills towards Porirua, ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,100</i>) [NOTES: [=track]]

>  Among the distinctively New Zealand [and Australian] ... words are domain, dome (UK press-stud, lamington, (tea-) shower; probably footpath (UK pavement, US sidewalk) should have a similar marking. <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Forrest Scott)]

>  Estimate for forming footpath from the corner of Sydney-st. to Willis-st. with stone curb, and totara posts, and thence to Ghuznee-st...GBP150.0.0 <i>(Oct 11, 1848,Independent (Wellington),[iii]</i>)

>  <b>WARD</b> Information had been laid against a resident of Karori, for a breach of the Municipal Bye-Law, by tying his horse up to as to interrupt traffic on the footpath. An apology induced the Magistrate to dismiss the defendant wth a caution, and a reminder that the streets of Wellington were not now so unfrequented that the ancient practice could be continued with impunity. <i>(1928,Early Wellington,180</i>) [NOTES: Dates from 1876]

### footpost
 
 <i>n.</i> [(fencing)] (See [foot](fly-fuz.md#foot))

### Footrot Flats
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand farming cartoon series by cartoonist Murray Ball.

>  The Footrot Flats annuals are compiled from a selection of strip cartoons that appear regularly in 19 newspapers throughout New Zealand and 81 in Australia. <i>(Jan 20, 1982,Manawatu Farmer,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Murray Ball's comic strip, later made into a film (c.1987)

>  "I mean, <i>Footrot Flats</i> did really well in Australia, but they thought that was theirs, because it plays in 120 newspapers a day." <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <i>Footrot Flats</i> is the first feature I've seen that has used the moving line and the painted background to give convincing texture to Country and country... That may be because the script has sacrificed tightness in order to include the whole gallery of Footrot Flats characters. <i>(Jan 31, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  For many New Zealanders the closest they get to farm life is their daily dose of <i>Footrot Flats</i>, courtesy of cartoonist and spare-time farmer Murray Ball... Footrot Flats Leisure Park will offer such rural delights as flying foxes, trail bikes, sheepdog trials and farmyard golf. <i>(Jun 22, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  Murray Ball's relationship with the characters of <i>Footrot Flats</i> has a pastel shade of regret......<i>Footrot Flats</i> has been a pretty good thing for the <i>Edmonds Cookery Book</i> too... <i>Footrot Flats</i> originated on his uncle and aunt's farm near Feilding. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Whangarei, is architypal New Zealand heartland, populated by a Footrot Flats cast of farmers ... <i>(May 28, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

### footsack
 
 <i>v.</i> [(Boer War)]

### footwall
 
 <i>n.</i> the underside wall of an inclined reef. [mining]

>  <b>McARA</b> Footwall: The underside wall of an inclined reef. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),322</i>)

### footy/footie
 
 <i>n.</i> (often as 'the footy') rugby [It is a book certain to arouse argument. Every public bar or tavern in New Zealand should have one at the ready to settle the crazy sort of arguments rugby fans are inclined to have on the way home from the footy.]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Afterwards I cleaned my footy boots and packed my gear. <i>(1947,Big Game,14</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> As in the old days when the footie was the only entertainment of the week. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,171,1976 ed.</i>)

>  Cripes, ... I'll go up ... and have some beers with the boys and talk about the footy and afterwards we'll have a bonzer feed at the Greek's on Seddon Street. <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,31</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The flicks and the footy ... <i>(1950,Our Street,26</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `I remember you on that trip to Nelson for the footie.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,42</i>)

>  After complaining of Scott-Base-like conditions at last week's Auckland club footie, old Loosehead has come down with a spot of the bot and his regular Sunday Star column will take a week's rest with the joker himself. <i>(Jul 20, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

>  Our oldest brother was a footy player and he believed that sports and politics shouldn't be mixed. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,23</i>)

>  Te Au never thought she would play test match footy. <i>(Sep 19, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B18</i>)

>  I'm still keen - if the drive is still there to play international footy. <i>(Sep 17, 1999,Evening Post,22</i>)

>  By an odd twist there's so much money in the game now the players don't have to hustle for a buck and can concentrate on what matters : playing footie. <i>(Sep 26, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  [Hdg] It's footy, but not as we know it. <i>(Jan 31, 2000,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  Realisation started sedately enough with cricket and Copperfield, trickled down through opera and oysters and finally flooded at footy and a fair go. <i>(Apr 12, 2000,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  We were a bunch of skinny white boys playing footie in a Wellington competition dominated by Pacific Islanders... <i>(Apr 6, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),17</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Welsh can sing but they can't play footy. <i>(Jan 13, 1998,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  It would mean sacrificing a bit of my footy. And you only get one shot at the footy. ... It's footy and flying, and a bit of fishing and diving, anything like that oudoors. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  Did you know footie is a great leveller? It's true! <i>(May 29, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  Some reasons why I got this job... Because footie's no more important than art... <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>) [NOTES: Brian Turner]

>  Even the smallest of small boys.. must have something to kick at, and, failing anything more suitable, has a kick at his own hat or the headgear of the urchin across the road who has challenged him to a game of "footy," as the youngsters call it. <i>(Jul 6, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 8</i>)

>  This joker still knows that when she comes to winnin' footie, the best direction is still a line as straight as a car salesman's hand headin' for your pocket. <i>(May 16, 1993,Sunday Star (Auckland),B-3</i>)

>  "John Mitchell has put a lot of faith in me and I've just for to pay that faith back the best way I know and that's to play the best footie I can." <i>(Jun 2, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  <b>LACEY</b> What's that got to do with footie? <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,12</i>)

>  Then there are more memory-stirring pictures of the company barber at work, the naked swimming (so much appreciated after weeks of no water), the games of "footie", and privates and officers on the same level in all sport. <i>(Apr 23, 2003,Capital Times,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rugby

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I stuck it up on top of the photograph of the Old Boys' footie team I played for in 1935. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,116</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> I cleaned my footy boots. <i>(1947,Big Game,14</i>)

>  Every Saturday afternoon Steerforth Smith steamed about the footy field while the crowd roared. <i>(Mar 17, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Yeah, he's got the footie brains all right. <i>Ibid.</i> 229 It's a good job we fight our wars on the footie field ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,99</i>)

>  <b>ADSETT</b> Last time I came to the footy dance a sheila told me to wipe the cow-muck out of me ears. <i>(1963,Magpie Sings, A,176</i>)

>  A man dressed in a striped footie jumper ... was calling ... `bloody Aussie prawn tossers'. <i>(1979,Loosehead Len's Gluepot Greats in $1</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> The footy field had lights around it now. <i>(1985,G'day Country,60</i>)

>  The only way I ever meet people is through work or sport. And you don't meet many women on the footie field do you?' <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,133</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> At first ... Elsdon thought it was an oddly shaped footy ball someone had forgotten to take home. <i>Ibid.</i> 1 She wore footy jerseys and sometimes boots, and he'd seen her clomping about in the back yard after it was dark. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,1988 ed.</i>)

>  Once when she couldn't have me Dad let me go to the flicks by myself. He gave me some extra money, so I went to the milkbar and bought a gobstopper and some other lollies. I stuffed my face, and felt crook all the way home. He bought new footy boots for my eldest brother too, and an Art Union ticket for Mum. He must have had a beaut time up the RSA that day, I reckon. <i>(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam,6</i>)

>  footy boots (rugby boots) <i>(1988,University Entrance English,3</i>)

>  (Goldie Brown Collection) footy <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> football, i.e. rugby [attrib. uses]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> He looked over the school before knocking at the nuns' door. The footy field got his approval and the six big trees along the boundary. <i>(1960,Tangahano,46</i>)

>  Footy Club Rugby Football Club <i>(1989,Chosen Place,104</i>)

>  That, of course, is the equivalent of a footie coach saying " there was a bit of argy bargy" when he means fists flew from the first whistle to the last. <i>(Nov 15, 1998,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  ...wherever you are a footy field is 100 metres long and 50 metres wide... <i>(May 11, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Boys of all sizes swirled and yelled and grinned, some dressed in swimming togs or footie jerseys. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,147</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I.. remembered.. Duncan had footy practice and I was meant to go to Miri's till Mum picked me up. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,45</i>)

>  <b>BALL</b> It is here, in the hard light of high noon on the footy paddock, where manly virtue is put to the test. <i>(1970,People Makers, The,89</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> He decided to go over to the Mad Woman's house and look at her balls and try to find a footy jersey. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,95</i>)

>  <b>LACEY</b> A footie trip.. can be roughly compared with bein' first off the landin' craft on D-Day. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a (rugby) football [ca1932 G.T. Diary (Dannevirke) -->ca1935 Havelock]

>  ... he just happened ... to be tenacious with a footy. <i>(Mar 17, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>EDMOND</b> Peter and Terry gobbled their porridge and went outside to kick the footy about; ... <i>(1984,High Country Weather,100</i>)

>  Women's teams like to give the footy "plenty of air". <i>(Sep 19, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B18</i>)

### force
 
 <i>v.</i> of a sheepdog, to move sheep

>  <b>LILICO</b> [The dogs] would head, lead, hunt away, force and back, though of course, they were best at rouseabout work. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,27</i>)

>  force ... NZ & Austral. ability of dog to move sheep ... NZ & Austral. (of a dog) move (sheep) in a certain direction <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,294</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the power of dogs to move sheep [(station)]

>  Force.. It is different from eye, the dog's control of sheep by staring them in the face, and from noise, in that there are plenty of dogs which yap, yap continually and yet seem to make little or no impression on their sheep. Huntaways are sometimes spoken of as forcing dogs; but the term force is also applied to the ability of a heading dog to pull sheep. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  force ... NZ & Austral. ability of dog to move sheep ... NZ & Austral. (of a dog) move (sheep) in a certain direction <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,294</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### force-pen
 
 <i>n.</i> [1933 check Acland and forcing-pen]

### forcer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LILICO</b> These dogs had any amount of eye and style and yet they were powerful forcers with plenty of noise ... <i>Ibid.</i> 26 A bitch pup I sold ... went direct to freezing works to pen up and developed into a first class forcer and backer ... The finest backing dogs I have ever seen are in the saleyards at Addington <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,1</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a forcing sheep-dog. (See [forcing dog](fly-fuz.md#forcing-dog))

>  <b>LILICO</b> A bitch pup I sold went straight to the freezing works to pen up and developed into a first class forcer and backer. <i>(1926,BLANK ENTRY,76</i>) [NOTES: in Gordon (1998) Three Sheep and a Dog]

### forces
 
 <i>n.</i> [(marble term)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Action terms which refer to the shooting of the marbles are: Knuckle down Stiff, Knee High, Funks [Flunks sic] or Funnicks, Slips, Firsts and Lasts and Forces. ... The term `Knuckle down Stiff' (Taranaki [18]80, Gisborne [18]85, Waitara [18]95) is mentioned by [Lady A.B. Gomme Vo. 1, p.113]. ... As Taranaki [18]80 says, `The term was called out in order to prevent too easy a success against a marble near the ring'. It was less advantageous to fire with the hand on the ground than with the hand elevated, perhaps at `Knee High'. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>Ibid.</i> 269 [marble term] Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### forcing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LILICO</b> [The dog] was no use in forcing but at shedding and control of a small lot, he was perfect. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,5</i>)

### forcing dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a huntaway sheep dog that can double as a yard dog and that will persevere in moving individual sheep.

>  Sheep dogs are of three distinct classes - the show dog, or fancy-worker, the mustering or paddock dog, and the yard or forcing dog. <i>(1907,NZ Farmer,209</i>)

>  The utility dog is a different class of animal. He is of two kinds - the mustering dog and the yard dog. His training too - unlike that of the forcing dog...is a matter of great care. <i>(1907,NZ Farmer,210</i>)

>  Huntaways are sometimes spoken of as forcing dogs, but the term is also applied to the ability of a heading dog to pull sheep. <i>(Oct 20, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  The only test of a forcing dog is the huntaway course. [forcer] <i>(May 21, 1942,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

### forcing pen
 
 <i>n.</i> a small pen constructed to force stock in one direction. [Farming: 1923 Perry et al Sheepfarming in NZ 16, 1950 NZJ Ag July 5/2, 1965 Guam] small pen next the race in a drafting or dip yard =crush pen]

>  <b>PERRY</b> [The sheep yards] should consist of receiving yards at each end, forcing pen, and drafting race. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  Small pen next the race in a drafting or dip yard. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> [=CRUSH PEN] Receiving yards. These must have the capacity to take incoming mobs. They communicate with the forwarding yards, or diamond pens, which are much smaller, and lead up to the crush pens or forcing pens. ... These last [crush pens or forcing pens] are usually one or two long pens that communicate by a two-way gate with the mouth of the drafting race. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

>  forcing pen NZ & Austral. = crush-pen <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,294</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen through which stock are forced into a drafting race. (See [crush](con-cyp.md#crush))

>  <b>PERRY</b> They [sheep yards] should consist of receiving yards at each end, forcing pen and drafting race. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Forcing pen: pen in the yards which narrows down to the drafting race entrance. It differs from a crush pen in as much as one of these is needed only where physical force may be required eg in the dipping yards. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)

>  If the forcing pens..and check pens run up a slight incline, it is quite satisfactory to have the rest of the yards on level ground. <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,5</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> They [receiving yards] communicate with the forwarding yards, or diamond pens, which are much smaller, and lead up to the crush pens or forcing pens. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

Some obsolete terms (e.g. entail, foreclosure, probation)are not noted as historical only. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2(1) : 1</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the first milk drawn from the udder prior to milking [(farming)]

### forepoling
 
 <i>n.</i>

### foreshore
 
 <i>n.</i> that area of the beach between the high-tide mark and the low-tide mark

>  The debate is, in its legal sense, strictly limited. "The foreshore" includes only that area of the beach between the high-tide mark and the low-tide mark. <i>(Aug 19, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### foreshore and seabed
 
 <i>phr.</i> popular name of legislation relating to ownership of and access to New Zealand beaches and ocean. (See [seabed and foreshore](sa-sho.md#seabed-and-foreshore))

>  If you say oysters? And I say yes, / and give back the foreshore and seabed, / Then we'd better call the whole thing off / Let's call the whole thing off. <i>(Oct 15, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  Even if Tamihere trots off to the backbench meek as a lamb, there is still the foreshore and seabed ordeal to get through. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  The Brash debate flowed on into the foreshore and seabed debate, which was even less amenable to the facts, because it seemed almost impossible to know what the facts were. <i>(Dec 25, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

### forest
 
 <i>n.</i> usually replaces, or is used as an alternative to, bush in technical, scientific, and official usage: state forest is the official term; most `exotic' plantations are referred to as forests [[AND see open forest; 2. also in flora and fauna]]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The brow on the larboard side of the Ship, which a few days ago was impenetrable forest, is now clear & airy, & contains an Observatory, a forge, a green hut for the woodcutters & a pen for our Sheep; and more than an acre of ground is cleared of the woods; ... <i>(Apr 24, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/266</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The forests of New Zealand are truly magni- [16] ficent, and are totally different in their appearance from those of New South Wales and Van Dieman's Land. ... It is impossible to force your way through the underwood ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),15</i>) [NOTES: [main use in Yate (also underwood 16)]]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> [first use : not bush] ... the deep black vegetable mould ... produces the most splendid growth of forest, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),2</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The spur was covered with black birch forest, ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,4</i>)

>  On Forest Culture <i>(1874,TrNZI,184</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  Forest is, of course, still in use, but it does not come as naturally to the tongue as bush, and there are no words derived from it in this part of the world. It is stiffer, more formal, more technical. We may speak of kauri forest or beech forest, but we get lost in the bush, fight bush fires, and climb above the bushline. The kauri forest, like the mixed podocarp forest, the sub-tropical rain forest, ... and such-like are terms that come from the botanist and forester, not from the layman - or from the bushman. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,23</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand, forest usually refers to large tracts of introduced trees, usually pines grown for the paper industry. ... Officially the term `indigenous forests' is used for the native bush, at least in yearbooks and government reports, but bush is the standard term. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,52</i>)

>  forest ... 3. N.Z. an area planted with pines or similar trees, not native trees. Cf. bush (sense 4). <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,436</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 295]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants see daisy-tree; groundsel; grass, oat-grass; grass, rice-grass; snowberry; fern, umbrella fern; houri, libertia, nettle; tree-groundsel, willow-herb

### Forest Accord
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On several occasions recently, private Forestry companies have consulted with Forest and Bird branches about logging access protected under the Forest Accord... The Accord was built upon the Tasman Accord, negotiated in 1988 between Forest and Bird and Tasman (now Fletcher) Forests, following lively protest against the company's clear-felling of native forest to plant pine trees. Both accords saw conservationists agree to champion sustainable pine forests, and the forestry companies agree not to plant pine trees at the expense of native forest. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/40</i>)

### Forest and Bird
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Farmers and Forest and Bird forged an unlikely coalition to support the Department of Conservation's aerial 1080 drop in Waitutu yesterday. <i>(Oct 5, 2010,Southland Times,14</i>)

>  Innovation comes from rearranging the surface text, foregrounding it, setting it against a counter-text - a Forest and Bird newsletter, a survival manual, a proofreading styles sheet. <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

### Forest and Birder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In keeping with the tradition that June had enthusiastically supported, 13 Forest and Birders came together on Saturday June 26th to create a memorable roast dinner with pavlova and mulled wine. <i>(Aug 2004,Internet - see Notes,6</i>) [NOTES: http://www.environmentnetwork.org.nz/downloads/Twig%20&%20Tweet%20Newsletter%20August%2004.pdf]

>  A "sleeping" Forest and Birder, I've only been to a couple of meetings and field trips in my thirty-plus years of membership... <i>(Mar 2006,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,4</i>)

>  It was a few years later that Wellington-based Forest-and-Birder Jim Lynch came up with the idea of using the same techniques on a 252 ha decommissioned dam site two kilometres from the centre of the capital city. <i>(Jul 2007,NZ Geographic,86/85</i>)

>  The island first caught the eye of Lower Hutt Forest and Birders from the window of the western hills kitchen of Ron and Angela Freeston in 1979, as they pondered new venues for their weekend tramping trips. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,33</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a specific section of forest allocated to a recreational or professional hunter.

### forest cabbage
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cordyline banksii</i>

### forest conservancy
 
 <i>n.</i> forest conservation

### forest farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm land cut from native bush.

The first operation consists of cutting down the underscrub (that is the supplejack, saplings, ferns and creepers; in fact all the cutting that can be done with a bill-hook. It is better to throw all the trees, big and small. <i>(Oct 1, 1877,NZ Country Journal,I iv  243</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Arthropodium candidum

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> 'It's the forest floor lily. Arthro .. Arthro ...' He was already leafing through a reference book, and found the photograph. 'Arthropodium candidum.' <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,40</i>)

### forest gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> a gecko species that is common in the north of each island but highly camouflaged and able to make rapid colour changes, so that it is difficult to sight. [<i>Hoplodactylus granulatus</i>]

>  On the Open Bay Islands, four kilometres off the southwest coast of New Zealand, there is an unusual-looking forest gecko that has long been known to be different from the adjacent mainland forest gecko. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/17</i>)

>  .. catch a glimpse of reintroduced tuatara, kakariki, giant weta and forest gecko; and see the results of a long-term native forest replanting programme by Lower Hutt Forest and Bird. <i>(Jun 30, 2005,Wellingtonian,6</i>)

>  A scientist's survey of Mr Hewitt's land reported "This site is home to the greatest diversity of lizard species known from any one site in the Wairarapa. Species present are common skink, spotted skink, common gecko, forest gecko, and Wellington green gecko. .." <i>(Aug 5, 2009,Wairarapa News,2</i>)

>  <b>Forest gecko</b>: Hoplodactylus granualtus, 89mm long, has also been seen by using a spotlight at night. Like the green gecko, its population appears to be healthy. <i>(Aug 5, 2009,Wairarapa News,2</i>)

>  Forest geckos were released onto Somes Matiu Island on Saturday... [He] says forest gecko numbers have been steadily declining nationally and they are now rare in the Wellington area. <i>(Apr 19, 2005,Independent (Auckland),11</i>)

>  Their reward is seeing the return of native animals to the island, including kakariki, North Island robin, tuatara, giant weta, and forest geckos. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes</i>)

### forest park
 
 <i>n.</i> former name for conservation park q.v.

>  It may be remarked that National Park is American in origin, dating from 1872, about the time that Yosemite was set aside for recreational purposes. In England, park, like forest, may have a definite legal meaning when it applies to a tract of land held by royal grant for keeping beasts of the chase. Unlike a forest, a park is enclosed, and [31] it does not have special laws or offices. In all the uses to which park has been put in England, until the recent adoption of National Park, there has been the idea of enclosure, as there is in the cognate paddock. The term forest park, as in the Tararua Forest Park, would once have seemed a contradiction in terms. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,54</i>)

### Forest Ranger
 
 <i>n.</i> [Forestry official]

>  It was proposed by some members to unite the duties of Surveyor with that of Forest Ranger, and that the Council would then be enabled to afford something like a remunerating salary. <i>(Oct 19, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  On Friday aftenoon a fire broke out in the office of the local State Forest Ranger, Mr Janson, adjacent to his residence in Eton Street and owned by Messrs John and Allan A. Fisher. <i>(Mar 3, 1931,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  R. B. Collett, State Forest Ranger, Otautau, had worked with Mr Macpherson when he surveyed Butler's road, the boundary of the Reserve. <i>(Jul 19, 1921,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  [A]n inquest was held at Harapipi .. on the body of Robert Napier, one of the Forest Rangers. <i>(May 23, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The whole question of the duties of the Inspector of Forests and the Forest Ranger was discussed conversationally in detail, and the matter was left in the chairman's hands with instructions to report at next meeting. <i>(Dec 29, 1876,Southland Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(hist.) volunteer militia during Maori Wars, sometimes abbreviated to 'ranger/s']

>  In August 1863 an appeal for `Active Young Men, having some experience of New Zealand Forests' to join `a Corps of Forest Volunteers'; <i>(Aug 1863,Daily Southern Cross</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Aug Daily Southern Cross [Auckland] in 1974 Bartlett the Emigrants 72]

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> The Forest Rangers, in command of Capt. Jackson, returned from Pukekohe this morning ... <i>(Sep 2, 1863,Journal,74,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> Forest Rangers (a body of settlers). <i>(1873,Bush Fighting,77</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... Atkinson and his picked men became increasingly useful as scouts and forest rangers. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/225 His [Captain Harry Atkinson's] party of fifty men of No. 2 Company, Taranaki Rifles, was the first corps of forest rangers to take the field in New Zealand. The force, as the war went on, was increased to two companies, and was styled the Taranaki Bush Rangers. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/248 The inn was the headquarters of Jackson's Forest Rangers during the early part of the war ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/161</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> `He's off to join the Forest Rangers,'. ... `The Forest Rangers!' cried the Colonel. `Pah! You should try and join a pukka regiment, my boy.' <i>(1939,Rewi's Last Stand,82,1944 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> A number of actions were fought along the road between the Waikato and Auckland. The settlers countered these tactics by forming a force of Forest Rangers, whose most famous commander was a flamboyant soldier of fortune, Gustavus Von Tempsky. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,136</i>)

>  It is not the story of Von Tempsky's famous Forest Rangers who fought the white man's battles in the sixties, that I desire to tell in this paper, but rather the incidents connected with the cases of robbery under arms which occurred in the early days of our province. <i>(Oct 13, 1909,Otago Witness,87</i>)

>  He also wanted a number of men for representation of the 60th Regiment and famous Forest Rangers. <i>(Oct 6, 1937,King Country Chronicle,2</i>)

### Forty Fours
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Forty Thieves
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical, derogatory) a powerful group of (original) colonial landowners [The 1849 citation below shows that the term was used first in context of the Wellington (Britannia) settlement, and only later in Nelson (and then somewhat differently in Hawke's Bay (1873 citation)). See Orsman for another more recent use in Auckland.]

>  <b>McINTOSH</b> Though his assertions may be discounted the spirit of Saunders's utterances may be taken as a reflection of the attitude of the popular party. In commenting on the fact that the power acquired by `the Original Resident Land Purchasers' Association' was retained long after the Company had ceased to exist, Saunders states `It became a leading and bitter opponent, a dangerous and mischievous class combination, facetiously called `The Nelson Supper Party' and more viciously called also `The Forty Thieves'. This party was very ably led by Dr., afterwards Sir David, Monro, and retained all political power in their own hands until some time after the introduction of representative government into this country. <i>(1940,Marlborough,137</i>) [NOTES: [Saunders in ? Nelson Examiner Oct 1857]]

>  <b>ROLLESTON</b> The notoriety of the defendants [at the 1873 Native Lands Alienation Commission] gave rise to their being called, variously, the twelve apostles (actually only seven were involved in the ownership of twelve shares) or the forty thieves. <i>(1873,Master, The,96,1980 ed.</i>)

>  In the outset of the Company's colonisation scheme, there were certain individuals who, by their disinterestedness in forwarding the establishment of the Company's first and principal settlement, obtained the sobriquet of the Forty Thieves ; and it appears that some individuals forming a portion of the Company's second Colony were anxious to rival the above-named gentlemen in a similar line of conduct. The one set proposed, that no individual holding less than five original land-orders in the settlement, should be eligible to a seat in their self- elected Council for the republican settlement of Britannia, well knowing at the time that none but themselves were qualified under such [a regulation, seeing that they were the holders of ten gratuitous landorders from the Company. The Nelson resident original landpurchasers, on the other hand, propose to exclude all persons who are not, like themselves, resident original purchasers, but who still originally emigrated here, and have become holders of land by purchase from them and the absentee land proprietors, and have embarked their labour and capital for various purposes, thereby promoting the welfare of the settlement, from any right to runs for cattle and sheep upon the waste lands. <i>(Jan 6, 1849,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle</i>) [NOTES: Sourced from Papers Past]

### forty-acre farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an allotted portion of land for development by bush settlers [1800s.]

>  <b>WASWO</b> The crowning disappointment for many settlers in New Zealand was the discovery that the forty-acre farms allotted to them were, when cleared and grassed, hopelessly inadequate to support a family. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,8</i>)

### forty-acre farmer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In the eighteen-sixties the Auckland Provincial Council, for instance, promised each immigrant a `free farm' of forty acres ... As one of the `forty acre farmers' said in the House of Representatives thirty years later, he came out because he wanted `a bit of land of his own'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,100</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer developing an allotted forty-acre farm under the forty-acre system of the 1800s.

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> As one of the forty-acre farmers said in the House of Representatives thirty years later, he came out because he wanted 'a bit of land of his own'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,100</i>)

### forty-acre man
 
 <i>n.</i> one who receives a (free) forty-acre grant of land [(usu. pl.) (obs. or hist.)]
