>  <b>MAY</b> We were lately on the sections of two `forty-acre men.' <i>(1869,Guide to Farming in NZ,42</i>) [NOTES: [? orig. Auckland usage =Canterbury/Otago `cockatoo']]

### forty-acre section
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an allocated portion of land for development into farmland by bush settlers.

>  <b>SILK</b> There they received their two forty-acre sections, of which there was not one square yard of arable land. <i>(1923,History of Puhoi,43</i>)

>  <b>SILK</b> She arrived here with five little children and was given a forty-acre section on the west side of Puhoi. <i>(1923,History of Puhoi,82</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Edie had 200 acres adjoining this block and he and a number of others were keen to get this forty-acre section. <i>(1954,Beyond the Blue Mountains,71</i>)

### forty-acre system
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs or hist.)]

>  <b>MAY</b> .. the country was a dog-hole ... the forty-acre system a most villainous take-in. <i>(1869,Guide to Farming in NZ,42</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an allocation of land based on units of forty acres.

>  I am no friend to the special settlement system, not being able to see much good that has come of it, that is to say, beyond what would have come of the forty-acre system alone. <i>(Aug 20, 1864,Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> During the 1850s large areas of Crown Land were made available under the 40-acre system, which entitled an adult immigrant on payment of his own fare from Britain, to a grant of 40 acres, plus 40 for his wife, and 20 for each member of his family aged between five and 18 years. Under the 40-acre system the immigrant gave an undertaking to remain in the province and not sell his land for a period of five years, after which time he would be given the freehold title of the land. <i>(1977,To the Northward,128</i>)

>  In New Zealand the forty-acre system has been adopted - a system which has proved most injurious to the country ... <i>(Oct 1866,Weekly News,13</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> The country was a dog-hole .. the forty-acre system a most villainous take-in. <i>(1869,May's Guide to Farming in NZ,42</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> "The Britain of the South" with its vaunted climate and scenery attracted them, and especially the forty-acre system .. Disillusionment awaited them. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,33</i>)

### forty-fives
 
 <i>n.</i> a West Coast card-game [(plural)]

>  <b>WOOD</b> I [the Otago Goldfields Commissioner] cannot describe how welcome that `clay' [pipe] was to me, ... with Dub. [Dublin, an Irish miner], Mexican and Pat, for my companions; the others having gathered round the rough table to enjoy the Irish game of `Forty-fives'. <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,32</i>) [NOTES: (`Beside the Billy')]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Consequently larger numbers grouped themselves outside [the Reefton Courthouse], where they passed the time in playing cards - the favourite game in those days [c.late 1860s] being `forty-fives'. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,110</i>)

>  A background of the haze engendered by tobacco and coal [in Buller pubs] is cards. As staple as beer is, more so is the game of `forty-fives', impossible of definition, where the five of the trump suit beats all other cards and the table gets a thumping. It accumulates all the good-natured interest and noise of which pubs are capable. <i>(1948,NZ Geographer,166</i>) [NOTES: Pascoe `Coal from Buller']

>  In smoky little back parlours [in Westport] friendly little groups meet to play `forty-fives' with uproarious oaths and much heavy thumping of the table. For [100] the uninitiated it may be stated that in this game the five-fingers (the five of the trump suit) beats all other cards. The trump suit order is five, jack, ace, king queen - all other suits begin with the king. Non-trump suits win high in red; low in black. Obviously the ace of diamonds is the lowest card in the pack unless diamonds are trumps, in which case it is the fourth best. The word `obviously' in the preceding sentence is a little optimistic - as the ace of hearts (the Maggy) is always the third best trump. It is credibly reported (a) that the game is of Irish origin (b) that there are no written rules (c) that the game can only be learned by playing it, and (d) it is as characteristic of Westport as whitebait or coal. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,99</i>) [NOTES: Korero Our Own Country]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> [Gloss] Forty-fives: A peculiar card game played on the West Coast, imported originally from Ireland. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,420</i>)

>  <b>CRUMPTON</b> The game played was called Forty-fives, and he could make neither head nor tail of the method of play. There was no money on the table, but a record of games played and won was made on a cribbage board by shifting a match about in the holes. At the conclusion of each game and [sic] a score of forty-five was reached, the losers shouted drinks for the other players, and the cards were shuffled round again. ... The fourth man stood up and excitedly thumped the table with the five of hearts. `I've been waiting for that, twenty-five - one - five, now lead and keep leading.' Play and conversation went on in a similar strain. Dave endeavoured explain the rudiments of the game. `The sequence of the game is highest in red cards and lowest in black. The highest card in the pack is the five of whatever suit turns up, then comes the Jack, and the ace of hearts is always the third highest trump in the pack. But you'll never learn the game looking on, it's too hard to follow. They play four legs to a game which is called a `horse.' Jim's playing a hundred pounds a game, twenty pounds a leg, and twenty pounds at the completion of the game. Then they start again.' <i>(1979,Spencer: the Gold Seeker,41</i>) [NOTES: [West Coast]]

>  <b>BATTYE & EAKIN</b> 45's an Irish card game, scored on a cribbage board, still popular on the West Coast. <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,72</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Old Calamity was an early one-man band of fiddle, horn, cymbals and kettledrums. Gambling was poker of 45s, a diggers' card game from California involving legs, queer suits and Maggie, cries of `Get Jinx!' and `Go for the Doctor!' <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,127</i>)

>  Above the stage / in the Buffs hung a wicked pair of horns; all the Druids/ did was play Forty-Fives and Euchre. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: Jeffrey Paparoa Holman]

### Forty-Mile Bush
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush area settled by bush farmers in northern Wairarapa.

>  I dimly remember as a youngster in the days when the Forty-Mile Bush was still standing, such a drink as 'matai beer'... <i>(Dec 10, 1920,Quick March,11</i>)

>  There is plenty of good land in the Forty-Mile Bush. The bush settlers would be only too glad to avail themselves.. <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,289</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> We did not stay long .. returning by what is called, surely in irony, the 'Forty-Mile' Bush ... <i>(Feb 10, 1890,Diary,47</i>) [NOTES: in Wilson (1894) In the Land of the Tui]

### forty-rod
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs, ? from US in WW1)]

>  <b>ANZAC</b> Shebangs [in Cairo] where they sell you whisky that takes the lining of your throat down with it ... a soothing liquid that licks `forty-rod', `chained lightning' or `Cape Smoke' to the back of creation; the kind of lush that gives you a sixty-horse dose of the jim-jams while you wa <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,77</i>)

### forward
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock sales] to present stock for sale.

>  <b>TAIT</b> The farming practice has been to forward good drafts of fat and forward wether lambs and attractive lines of two-tooth ewes and five-year ewes. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,68</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] between store and prime condition, with some ''finishing'' yet to be done.

>  100 2-tooth ewes from up-river, 40 18-month steers, off rough country, 300 forward bush wethers. <i>(Jan 31, 1905,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  Indications, however, are that as a finisher for forward stores, lucerne is slower than good rape. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,387</i>)

>  The lambs are sold as fat and forward. <i>(Nov 25, 1999,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

### forward conditioned
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [farm stock] in near-top condition.

>  Forward conditioned cows and heifers were in demand. <i>(Apr 6, 1944,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  Forward-conditioned two-tooths off the shears have reached 63/4. <i>(Oct 25, 1951,NZ Farmer,34</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Mr Hull had purchased 230 acres of sandhill country on the coast west of Waiuku, where forward conditioned cattle are 'run off' during the wettest part of the winter. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,127</i>)

>  Forward conditioned 18 month heifers, some of which had failed to sell in the prime pens, made $400-$430. [forward-conditioned] <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

### forward selling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the selling of unseen stock at a distance.

>  <b>GORDON</b> One [method of doing business] which I knew about, but which had virtually ceased when I went on the road, was the common practise [sic] of 'forward selling.' <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,58</i>)

### forward store
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a store animal in near to top condition.

>  Indications, however, are that as a finisher for forward stores, lucerne is slower than good rape. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,387</i>)

>  Good forward stores $35 - $40. <i>(Aug 29, 1996,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> They were a good line of well-woolled forward stores. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,40</i>)

>  They [store cattle] can range from big, fat animals ready to kill, 'forward stores' that will be ready in a few weeks if feed is good, to walking toast racks. <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 22</i>)

### forward-store
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of store sheep or cattle] in near to top condition.

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Losses can be markedly reduced by ensuring that the ewes are held at 'forward-store' condition up till about four to five weeks from lambing. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,66</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> The aim is to equalise the condition of the ewes to a little better than 'forward-store' level. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,117</i>)

### forwarding yard/forwarding pen
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The forwarding yards or pens, which are considerably smaller and consequently are useful for dealing with small mobs of sheep, as well as their function of leading up to The crush or forcing pen(s): These are usually one or two long, small pens, frequently tapering at one end, and holding about 50 sheep. They in turn communicate by a two-way gate with the mouth of the Drafting race: This race is long and narrow just wide enough for the sheep to pass through in single file. Its purpose is to divide a mixed flock ... into any required number of smaller mobs, and for this purpose the race is fitted at its exit with one or more drafting gates, which guide the sheep into several <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Receiving yards. These must have the capacity to take incoming mobs. They communicate with the forwarding yards, or diamond pens, which are much smaller, and lead up to the crush pens or forcing pens. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>) [NOTES: [=DIAMOND PEN]]

### fossick
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PYKE</b> Do you think I can go fossicking in patience here, while you are running down the game? <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/ii/77,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  ~ v.i. to dig out crevices with a pick and knife; to work over waste heaps and abandoned claims in the hope of finding gold; to search about or rummage for profit.-foss'icker n. [E. dial.] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  v.int. to propect for gold, especially in abandoned claims and diggings; whence fossicker. fossicker n. searcher for gold or other minerals. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  fossick To dig or search for gold, especially on abandoned claims or `tailings'; also to hunt about or rummage for anything. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),171,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ..., and that was the last trace found of those prospectors, and probable reason for the creek where they had camped and fossicked, [sic] becoming known as Gold Creek. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,23</i>)

>  fossick vb. Austral. & N.Z. 1. (intr.) to search for gold or precious stones in abandoned workings, rivers, etc. 2. to rummage or search for (something): to fossick around for. [C19: Austral., prob. < E dialect fussock to bustle about, < E FUSS] - fossicker n. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,439</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 297; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 195]]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Peters had persuaded a man named Jenkins to accompany him in his gold-hunting expeditions, and for some time Jenkins `fossicked' about the Woolshed, whilst Peters went further ahead. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,37,1962 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fussy or querulous person; fusspot; troublesome person (often old fossick, young fossick, old (young) devil, etc.) you old fossick =fossie [cf tossle f. tassle] [ca1910 NZ use obsolesc. at least, and possible, influenced by fossil in a similar use (old fossil), and possibly by fuss(-pot).]



 
 <i>v.</i> 1. to search or hunt. 2. to search for gold in abandoned mines, etc. fossicker/

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Fossick... intrans. to dig, but with special meanings. Derived, like fosse, a ditch, and fossil, through French from Lat. fossus, perfect part. of fodere, to dig. Fossicking as pres. part., or as verbal noun, is commoner than the 3 other parts of the verb. ... (1) To pick out gold. -->1952. W. H. Hall, `Practical Experiences at the Diggings in Victoria,' p. 16 : -->(2) To dig for gold on abandoned claims or in waste-heaps. -->1865. F.H. Nixon, `Peter Perfume,' p. 59: -->(3) To search for gold generally, even by stealing. -->1861. T. McCombie, `Australian Sketches,' p. 60: -->(4) To search about for anything, to rummage. -->1870. S. Lemaitre, `Songs of Goldfilds,' p. 14:] -->A. v.t. -->1a. (goldminers') (i) to search out and find (alluvial gold) small amounts of gold <i>(1898,Austral English,152</i>)

>  see citation FOSSICKING ppl aj <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources</i>)

>  I've got a great geologist to look/ About for gold .../ And he's been fossicking in every nook,/ But Nothing found ... <i>(Oct 22, 1862,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Oct 22 Lyttelton Times in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 259]

>  Another man, in a few hours, fossicked with his knife 10ozs [of gold] <i>(1864,Saturday Review,X/62</i>) [NOTES: 1864-67 Grant Dunedin 1864-67 Saturday Review X/62]

>  <b>GRACE</b> I shouldn't have left till I'd fossicked that gorge. <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,85</i>)

>  ...I am quite sure that there is still gold and silver up there in 'them thar hills!' and no doubt modern machinery will be fossicking around in the not too distant future. <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No. 36/18</i>)

>  Men from New Zealand, who got a precarious living by fossicking about the islands, got into all sorts of trouble. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> "I'm getting tired of fossicking about here," said Jack next morning. <i>(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,266</i>)

>  She fossicked out two gin bottles, took them down to her bowser attendant, and had them filled. <i>(Aug 13, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/216 : 7</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Close we'd cling about him, an' fossick in his coat - / Lollies for the kiddies," always came in / Father's coat. [Note: Fossick : Australasian for "hunt about"]. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,74</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a search (cf. fossick verb)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> Ngaia [?o] only laughed, and picking up the axe followed her husband, not, however, until she had made a close fossick for any further gold there might be. <i>(1898,Maori Maid,332</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (sc. one's way, route) to pick (one's way); find, search out (a route) through trial and error'

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> I was [sent] to bring the old horse up ... We fossacked our way through that grim collection of rocks, known to us by the name of the Devil's lumber box. <i>(1888,Wakatipians,80</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> One of [the dog's] accomplishments was to `fossick' a route round a bluff. <i>(1946,Memories of Mountains,88</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> I was [sent] to bring the old horse up. ... we fossicked our way through that grim collection of rocks, known to us by the name of the Devil's lumber box. <i>(1988,Wakatipians,80</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (transf.) a. (sc. food, etc.) fossick (out), to forage; bring to light by searching

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> We caught some fish, and also fossicked out some potatoes. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,23</i>) [NOTES: [1866 ? Diary Williamson] 1870 Whitworth Martin's Bay Settlement 23]

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> All your grub you [sc. a horse] had to fossick/ Upon the dry and stringy tussock. <i>(1883,Castle Gay,72</i>)

>  ... a medium-sized footprint ... shows where a striped rail has been fossicking for tit-bits. <i>(Oct 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,39</i>)

>  <b>G.M.WILSON</b> We ... fossick out our heavy luggage which we left in charge of John to bring on. <i>(Feb 6, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 6 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 170]

>  <b>WILLIAMSON</b> We caught some fish, and also fossicked out some potatoes. <i>(1866,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Williamson Diary in Whitworth Martins Bay Settlement (1870) 23]



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> (absol.) to hunt, potter about [not specific to NZ, archive. JB]

>  The hind toes and claws help in maintaining the position of the [kiwi] when fossicking about the prostrate trunks. <i>(1872,TrNZI,V/189</i>) [NOTES: (Potts, West Coast)]

>  <b>BARR</b> He kept fossicking about, as scientific men are wont to do, stimulating enquiry and inducing others to enter with him upon researches ... <i>(1879,Old Islands,102</i>)

>  64. <i>Thinornis Novae-zealandiae</i>, Gml. Masked Plover, Sand Plover, Shore Plover, Tuturautu. - This pretty plover is ... seen in the southern part of this island fossicking about the sandy shores at the mouths of rivers. <i>(1885,NZ Journal of Science,508</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>SKEY</b> [transf] 'Tis the last of my rabbits, left ambling alone,/ ... /Not one of his [rabbit's] kindred is left to our whim/ To gamble [sic], to fossick, to hop out with him. <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,38</i>) [NOTES: (`The Last of My Rabbits')]

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> [Colonialisms] `To fossick about' meant, in the first instance, to search the chinks and crannies of a rocky watercourse for gold; it has now come to mean any searching at hap-hazard. This is no doubt akin to or derived from fosse (French) and fossa (Latin). <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,169</i>)

>  <b>PRICE</b> We were warned ... to be very careful how we fossicked about. <i>(1891,Through the Uriwera [sic] Country,40</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> When `fossicking' for this route the week previous, our gymnastic feats were most interesting and amusing, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,59</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> [fn] fossick; Australasian for `hunt about'. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,74</i>)

>  `While Lottie (meaning his wife) was at the baths I went fossicking about in her room and found the letter and bottles.' <i>(Jan 14, 1911,NZ Truth,290/5/5</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> Fossick: hunt about. <i>(1926,Treasury of NZ Verse,288</i>)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> Lamps were taken from the coach, and Miles ... began to fossick for traces of the right road, ... <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,35</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Fossick (about). To SEARCH for or look for something. `I'll go fossick about in that rubbish heap I call an office, and see if I can can find those papers for you'. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,42</i>)

>  In over 730 episodes [<i>Country Calendar</i>] has gently fossicked through every area of country life: harvesting, propagation of new horticulture, scrub clearance, and mad gadgets made out of bits of tin and No 8 wire. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  The flexibility of the upper mandible ... tends materially to assist the bird ... and thus aids it in searching or fossicking around or beneath the shingle for its food ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,3/93-97</i>) [NOTES: T.H. Potts 1870 TrNZI 3/93-97 in 1873 Buller Birds of NZ 218]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The latter [eggs] were chosen, and one of the cadets, who had a private fowlyard, fossicked around and returned with between two and three dozen eggs, ... <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,159,(1935) ed.</i>)

>  The unsuspecting traveller leaves a swell hotel and plenty to fossick round for accommodation. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> I've fossicked around the premises, and I must confess that they're the most trifling push I ever saw [a bank robber referring to bank he intends to rob]. <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,87</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> In April 1967 Mr Evans was fossicking around Mary Island when he spotted a cave a few feet above the lake. <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,33</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> ... I always felt a bit squeamish eating [wekas] as they were friendly, cheeky little fellows, often fossicking around in our garden. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,51</i>)

### fossicker
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who fossicks (for gold, or for other things)

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> The gilded loafer `does the block' and elevates his snout/ To see the rough-dressed fossicker, white haired and broken down/ Who strays from out the lonely wilds for his Christmas booze in town. <i>(1894,Voices of Wave and Tree,15</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> `Harry the Whale' and Dick Nicoll, two old `fossickers', are said to have discovered the Balfour Glacier in 1866, .. . <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,158</i>)

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> The system of fossicker Michael O'Flynn/ Got very much out of repair. <i>(1896,Poems (various authors),35</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Fossicker, ... one who fossicks, sc. works among the tailings of old gold-mines for what may be left. 1853. C. Rudston Read ... Australian Gold Fields, p. 150: - <i>(1898,Austral English,153</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Fossicker, fencer, rouseabout, and rabbiter he'd been,/ From Riversdale to Naseby every hamlet he had seen;/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,62</i>)

>  fossick v.i. to dig out crevices with a pick and knife; to work over waste heaps and abandoned claims in the hope of finding gold; to search about or rummage for profit.- foss'icker n. [E. dial.] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Many of the old station cooks, with the assistance of an `offsider' (mate) found no difficulty in cooking for fifty or sixty men. ... it has to be remembered that ... bread cost only 6d. or 7d. a 4lb. loaf, and tea (post and rails) could be bought for 1/- per lb. Some of these contract cooks were great fossickers and managed to find quite a lot of green stuff for vegetables ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,91,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Fossicker: A part-time miner - one who works creek-beds or other places but does not hold a registered claim. <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>) [NOTES: Glossary of Mining Terms]

>  v.int. to propect for gold, especially in abandoned claims and diggings; whence fossicker. fossicker n. searcher for gold or other minerals. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> FOSSICKER: `the fossicker is to the miner as the gleaner is to the reaper'. The fossicker picks out crevices or pockets but does not work the ground systematically. Often he did not hold a miner's right. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,527,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Of necessity our cattle became expert foragers and fossickers. <i>(1970,Way Up North,56</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The ghouls, the fossickers and the just plain inquisitive would be having a field day. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,111</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> The miners and fossickers did always agree/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,61</i>)

>  <b>TRAIL</b> During the early gold-rushes in Otago, a great many fossickers were lucky and found gold in abundance, ... <i>(1984,Child of the Arrow,49</i>)

>  The Waipori Flat has always been.. a sealed book to the "fossicker". <i>(Jun 5, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  I am happy to say that the fossickers and mica diggers have "cut it." <i>(Nov 7, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### four by two
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (a piece of) sawn timber four inches broad by two inches deep, or its metric equivalent: `I hit him with a (bit of) four-by-two'.

>  He had hit the guy over the head with a bit of four by two and also on the nose. <i>(Jul 22, 1970,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Four-by-two is a 2 x 4 inch piece of wood. Surprising how hard it is to accommodate to this one. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,42</i>)

>  four-by-two n. 4" x 2" piece of timber. <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  I'm not sure there's anywhere else in the world where conservators talk about four-by-twos." <i>((13/0/1991),Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  I'm not sure there's anywhere else in the world where conservators talk about four-by-twos." <i>(Feb 13, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> You can imagine that two layers of match-lining plonked on bits of four by two as studs really don't perform any soundproofing function at all. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,191</i>)

>  Wellington timber suppliers are planning to boost prices.. pushing the retail price of four by two framing timber to an all-time high... This means the retail price of four by two will rise to $5.23 a metre <i>(May 25, 1993,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  He hated Good Kiwi Blokes "The one that can take a bit of 4 X 2, a couple of rusty nails and an empty baked bean can and build an emergency weather balloon out of it when required. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Crikey dick if the load of four be twos and pinex didn't arrive. <i>(1980,Prisoner of Mother England,21</i>)

>  It appears that the Democrats have now completely adopted [Jim Anderton's] 'four by two' style of dealing with anyone who dares to voice a dissenting opinion. <i>(Nov 18, 1995,Dominion,2</i>)

>  "Mum and me, we rigged a sheerlegs in the garden with four-by-twos and took him [sc. the bull] to bits. <i>(Oct 15, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  [Caption] We had a little 'billy-cart' - a box with a couple of wheels on it and a shaft - and we used to go down to the wharf when the coal boats were coming in, and take a length of four-by-two with us. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,24</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ? small, insignificant person;(? a Jew, from rhyming sl.) -->(prob from 4"x2" timber=size, with play on a person 4ft high by 2ft breadth: cf Mr Five-by-five: but phs a variant of (P) four-by-three a. small; insignificant (rarely of persons)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> And look here, Mowat, any more like that and I'll be charging you down, you miserable little four-by-two. <i>(1949,Roads from Home,38</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a screw, warder, prison officer [(prison rhyming sl.)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> while somebody kept a lookout for four-by-twos, we'd boil up water or milk ... <i>Ibid.</i> 248 Four-by-two. (n) Prison officer. Rhyming slang from SCREW ... <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,218</i>)

>  <b>Looser</b> There are also terms specific to the prison situation such as.. four-by-two for a screw (prison guard, warden). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2 (2): 2</i>)

### four main centres
 
 <i>n.</i> the four original principal cities of New Zealand: Auckland, Wellington, Christchurch, and Dunedin.

>  <b>TURNER</b> New Zealand has rather more native names than Australia though the <i>Four Main Centres</i>, as the four cities which for a long time were of a quite different order of magnitude from other places in New Zealand are called, are named Auckland, Wellington, Christchurch and Dunedin. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,193</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Despite their difference in size, these four `main centres' have preserved a marked individuality ... <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),82</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> ~ In New Zealand there are traditionally four `main centres'. ... <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,61</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Our four main centres have fine railway stations and foul areas around. <i>(1985,G'day Country,162</i>)

>  Dunedin was the dryest [sic] of the four main centres, Auckland the warmest and Christchurch the sunniest. <i>(Jun 5, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### four o'clock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was the gay eschscholtzia, the "four o'clocks" or "Sleepy Joes" (as we called them as children, that furnished me with my pen name. <i>(Dec 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,90</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Taylor River [Marlborough] river-fan glowing with <i>eschscholtzia</i>, the orange-yellow four-o'-clock, or bright with blue borage ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,171</i>)

>  <b>TED REYNOLDS</b> Eschscholtzia was called `four o'clock' in Blenheim in the 1940s. <i>(Mar 30, 1988,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Ted Reynolds (NZ Herald)]

### four pillars
 
 <i>n.</i> The four seats in which all male Maori over the age of 21 could vote, created by the Maori Representation Act of 1867.

 <i>adj.</i> [used 1988 British medical researchers on Tapanui flu: a four star research with 5-star ...]

 <i>n.</i> (Sport) [Domain: cricket]

### four-figure man
 
 <i>n.</i> an early WW2 enlistment with a low Army number, a First Echelon man; hence, an experienced soldier; see also six-figure man, thirty-niner

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> We sent the wrong bloke. A four-figure man would have come back with the town clock. <i>Ibid.</i> 67 Hark at the four-figure man cracking hardy. Ibid. 103 That hardy four-figure myth - the regions of Bagush ... <i>(1944,Troop Target,63</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> His driver roared him away, away from the colonial rabble where an officer, a four-figure man who had shot paratroops hooked in the olive trees on Crete, could sit with his men in the last days of the war and be one of them. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,94</i>)

>  ... but there was no closer spirit than among the `four-figure' men, as the First Echelon members were called, on account of their regimental numbers ... see Echelon <i>(Sep 26, 1979,Evening Post,2/8</i>) [NOTES: [From an article First Echelon Reunion by Arthur Helm]]

>  The forgotten legion, the thirty-niners, the four-figure man ... all sound like mysterious titles <i>(Sep 18, 1982,Evening Post,12</i>) [NOTES: [an article on First Echelon].]

### four-lane
 
 <i>v.</i> widen a road to provide for four lanes for traffic, two in each direction [see notes at four-laning]

>  Transit's immediate concern should be to four-lane the Centennial Highway. <i>(Apr 30, 2004,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  A proposal to four lane Jervois Quay was considered by the City Council several years ago. <i>(Jul 22, 2004,Wellingtonian</i>)

 <i>n.</i> widening a road to provide for four lanes for traffic, two in each direction [Google threw up use in Canada (most) USA and India, also UK green laning and high laning.  <i>n.</i>

### four-stand
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Other buildings include a self-contained bach with an attached car shed, a four-stand woolshed with nightpen space for 350 ewes.. two stables and a track room. <i>(Nov 29, 2002,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

### four-tooth
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep of 22 to 30 months old.

>  Ewe lambs 32 two tooth ewes 402 four & six tooth 638. full mouthed 1322 broken mouthed 11, wedders 290 total 2695. <i>(Mar 14, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Has nobody a line of broken-mouthed ewes that could be palmed off to him as four-tooths; ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,26</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> He objected to to breeding halfbreds in order to sell them all as four-tooths; while older and older Merinos were retained ... <i>(1981,Early Northern Wairoa,48</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ages of sheep. Although there are many variations, the following names are usually used: A sheep is a lamb until weaned. After weaning a lamb is called a hogget until shorn. A male lamb is a ram lamb until castrated, then a wether. A female lamb is a ewe lamb. After shearing, a ewe or ram lamb is usually called a shorn hogget until cutting the first two teeth. It is then called a two-tooth. Some use the term two-tooth as soon as the lamb is shorn. Next year the sheep is a four-tooth, then a six-tooth and then an eight-tooth or full-mouthed sheep. It is full-mouthed as long as the teeth remain perfect but then becomes broken-mouthed and gummy. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  The sheep forwarded to the laboratory were one "two-tooth" and one "four-tooth." <i>(1898,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VI/71</i>)

>  The sheep were two, four, and six-tooth ewes. <i>(Feb 7, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A ewe that goes to the ram as a four-tooth is exactly three years old when it has its first lamb. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,174</i>)

>  The evidence for the defence is that a four-tooth ewe ceases to be a four-tooth as soon as the fifth tooth, or the fifth and sixth tooth, appear through the gums... The witnesses for the plaintiff... are unanimous in their opinion that a ewe two and a-half years old is still a four-tooth ewe, although the fifth and sixth teeth have appeared... I have accordingly to settle the matter as follows - a ewe is correctly defined as a four-tooth until the fifth and sixth tooth reach maturity. <i>(Jun 3, 1924,Otago Witness,13</i>)

 <i>v.</i> fracture [correct spelling of infinitive?]

### fragie
 
 <i>n.</i> a fragile parcel [(Postal workers')]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There was [postal] work [in the 1960s], hard satisfying work. Unloading parcel bags, huge cumbersome containers nicknamed pre-fabs, from a three-door Z. There was slots, there was lumps, there was packing fragies in bottlos, there was the tying down of the north line for a clearance, the scissors, lead seals and squeezers. The sorters on call-back flicking circulars with precision. The volcanoes of letters on the facing-up table, the checker chanting the reds onto the lorry, the checker calling the overseas in, London 45 ... The queue of men working at the parcel depot or the postal siding ... Tieing down the cargon. ... The sorters' school at the posties' pub known colloquially as Box 44 or the B.B.C., the Back Bar of the Cecil. The cheerful abuse of cobbers. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)

### frame pack
 
 <i>n.</i> a pack set on a frame which fits the contours of one's back [(trampers', mountaineers')]

Some become prejudiced about frame packs for instance ... <i>Ibid.</i>27 Frame packs of the Bergen or Canadian type are permitted by the Department, but they must be capable of being used without the frame to provide for emergencies. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,24</i>)

>  <i>(1958,Tararua,12/7</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> A pack may be a frame pack or frameless pack. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)

>  She took the backpack off, put the framepack on, staggered a little. The daypack she perched precariously on her shoulders, but she knew she'd need her hands free. <i>(Dec 28, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

Mr.David Garrick ... was the first private individual to select in Dunedin, and ... he chose what is now probably the most valuable site - that quarter acre upon which now stands the Bank of New Zealand ... Upon this he erected his frame house brought out on the John Wicliffe, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,99</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### frame-tent
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> We reached the township of Maori Point ... consisting of a few `frame-tents', as fixed tents usually lined with green baige or druggetting are called. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,134</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The order of architecture known as a `frame-tent' - that is, a frame consisting of posts let into the ground rising some six feet or a little more from its surface, and topped by a wall-plate from which sprang light rafters to a ridge pole. The whole was enclosed with stout calico ... <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,78</i>)

### frameless pack
 
 <i>n.</i> see cit. [1958 Tararua 12/27 ; 1966 Turner]

The frameless pack of course receives that name only on formal occasions, being more familiarly known by that elegant term, <i>kidney-rotter</i>, or less frequently as<i> kidney-buster, kidney-crusher, kidney-sweater or kidney-rider. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,27</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### franchise farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a property where stock of a particular breed are bought and bred on a franchise basis.

>  Mr Marshall says David should take the credit for the latest expansion, Kelso Ltd, in which the Kelson name has been franchised to three farms in Southland, Canterbury and Gisborne. In all, 11,000 Kelso ewes are spread across six farms - the three farm franchises, farm at Taihape and Colyton that run Kelso-owned ewes and hoggets, and a farm near Marton... For now, Kelso Ltd is concentrating on helping the franchise farms consolidate their flocks. <i>(Aug 30, 2002,Dominion,C5</i>)

>  ... back in the 70s, John Clarke sold an amazing 80,000 copies of his Fred Dagg album in three short weeks... Other evidence, besides that Daggmania: the huge popularity (still) of Billy T James... <i>(Apr 26, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  The Fred Daggish message - we don't know how lucky we are - seems a bit flat. <i>(Aug 10, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  "Shane's a leader in Fred Dagg clothing, a farmer with 342 cows to milk each day, " Mr Hawkins wrote in a newspaper column. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Duncum isn't the only culprit responsible for the egregious comedy about a lost rural Kiwi arcadia peopled by two Fred Daggs sans bollocks. <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  The Dagg persona was never much more than a convenient overcoat he slipped on before going to work. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  New Zealand humour is dry and cynical, that's why Fred Dagg became so popular. <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,118</i>)

>  Indeed John Clarke's Fred Dagg in gumboots and black singlet portrayed the old stereotype as an anachronism. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  We could .. get a smile out of John Clarke's rural Fred Dagg and Lyn of Tawa's suburban borax-poking... <i>(Apr 19, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <i>Willy Nilly</i> is quite edgy. You see shades of Fred Dagg. <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>)

>  After all, Fred Dagg's gumboots and singlet, a sort of local version of Dorothy's iconic ruby slippers, now reside at Te Papa. <i>(May 29, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),79</i>)

>  On April 1 1975, Stewart Island was left off the weather map. In the storm of outrage that followed, Carline received bundles of protest mail. TV1 immediately called in Fred Dagg to present the weather with gumboots, Stewart Island was reinstated and Carline and TV1 were forgiven their sin of omission. <i>(Jun 1, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

### Fred Daggish
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>TUCKER</b> I was also convinced that the method being used to brand the horses was, to say the least, `Fred Daggish'. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,51</i>)

### free
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of fleecewool) not cotted, without contamination by vegetable matter [(woolgrowers')]

>  Free. When fleece is either not cotted or without vegetable matter contamination. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

### free breakfast table
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> [Grey] ... put forward a policy which included `one man one vote', triennial assemblies, the compulsory government purchase of large estates and the `free breakfast table,' with a land tax to make up for the loss of customs revenues. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,162</i>)

### free face
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Blasting: ... [The fuses,] about five feet long, were each cut about half-an-inch longer than the one before it, thus ensuring that none of the holes would be over-burdened and `blow [313] out' in the wrong direction, as any explosion must follow the line of least resistance, which is the shortest distance to a `free face.' <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),312</i>)

### free grazier
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf long paddock]

>  <b>BOYD</b> Ratepayers, he complained, were being `heavily rated' for the formation of roads and water tables, daily damaged by the `free graziers'. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,84</i>)

### free kindergarten
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The under fives are now catered for in Carterton by an institution.. known as the Carterton Free Kindergarten Association. <i>(1961,Carterton District Centennial Celebration,16</i>)

### free lance pig
 
 <i>phr.</i> a wild pig ['effectually' sic in citation? 2001 Questionnaire respondents. wild pig - free lance pig.]

>  <b>BEST</b> Fences that effectually bar horses, cattle and sheep may be no protection against free lance pigs left to shift for themselves. <i>(1925,Tuhoe: The Children of the Mist,19</i>)

### free ride
 
 <i>v.</i> to get away without paying

>  Why not use our forestry sinks to free ride until 2012, instead of introducing carbon tax. <i>(Oct 2005,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: (NZ Climate Change Office) www.climatechange.govt.nz/policy-initiatives/carbon-tax-questions.html]

### free selection
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These limits apply to lands which are thrown open for `free selection', as it is termed, ... The rule is almost invariable, that land thrown open for so-called `free selection' is offered to the public under three different tenures, and the choice is left entirely to the would-be settler. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,202</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In Canterbury the Wakefield system of `free selection' of land at a uniform price (now 2 [pounds] per acre) placed little restraint on the aggregation of large holdings while prices were so high. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,154</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the opening of Crown land for purchase.

>  It is rumored - but we can't vouch for the accuracy of it - that as soon as road lines are cut through the bush country the land will be thrown open for free selection as was done in Canterbury. We are, however, disposed to receive the statement with caution, because the principle of free selection in bush country would be much more difficult to carry out than it would be in open land. <i>(Oct 6, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### free selector
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also selector](../A#also-selector))

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> [Aust] Hence it often happened that large runs in the most favourable situations were often cut up by small investors, `free selectors' as they are called in Australia. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),82 (et pass.)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [also used in Aus.] a farmer who buys land from the government with a deposit and pays an annual amount until purchase price is completed.

>  Both the aim and the object of those who frame laws in favor of special settlements, free selectors, deferred payment, and small farm associations are admirable ... If a portion of the progress payments is remitted by the state to the Southland free-selectors, the practical result will be that they will ultimately obtain their land for a less price than they originally agreed to pay for it. <i>(May 1, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### free wheat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wheat grown to sell on contract to a mill or agent.

>  There will be a number of farmers who will carry 'free' wheat and market it after harvest. 'Free' wheat is grown with a matching contract to supply a miller or dealer. <i>(Oct 25, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

### free-boarder
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an unthrifty cow.

>  Continuing improvement is leading to the poor, in-bred brute, commonly known as the 'free-boarder,' being absent from most herds. <i>(1902,Department of Agriculture Report,41</i>)

### free-fish
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  In the meantime, trailing baited lines without sinkers in the current, we free-fish. <i>(Aug 7, 2002,Dominion Post,A20</i>)

### free-graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to graze lightly but without controls as in break-grazing.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> When the crop in the forage paddock is ready for use it should be free-grazed. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,71</i>)

### free-grazing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a light and open grazing of a mature paddock or crop, as opposed to break-grazing.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The wastage resulting from free-grazing is more apparent than real. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,71</i>)

### free-milling
 
 <i>adj.</i> [adjective? (quartz mining)]

>  Although the veinstone was free-milling, the gold occurred in such a fine state of division that the treatment facilities were altogether inadequate. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 18 (NS)/10</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]

### free-mustering
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>WHEELER</b> `Why halfbreds?' He replied: `They are good economic units - strong, free-mustering sheep that suit our country.' <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,25</i>)

### free-running
 
 <i>adj.</i> [farmers' cf dogged]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the sheep were free-running. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,93 (et pass.)</i>)

### free-seater
 
 <i>n.</i> one who scales the fence to obtain a free-seat; or otherwise enters a sportsground in such a way as to avoid paying an entrance fee see also scaler

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Around the camp was a ten-foot hight corrugated iron fence, topped off with three strands of barbed wire, which had apparently been placed to stop the `free seaters' in the cricket and football seasons. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,108</i>)

### freehold
 
 <i>n.</i> [(station)]

>  Freehold. To make freehold, to buy the freehold of run country. The past is freeholded. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>SINCL,AIR</b> `Freehold' became the focus of a system of socio-political attitudes ... The cry of `Freehold' aroused the farmers to great fervour; ... To farmers the future seemed to lie between socialistic, loafing `townies' and hard-working, individualistic, freeholding countrymen. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,201</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to make a rural property freehold.

>  Freehold - to make f[reehold], to buy the f[reehold] of run country. <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Keith freeholded the farm in 1965 and he and Ina continued to milk cows until they sold it ... <i>(1982,Where it all Began,74</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Every settler in this block has freeholded his section. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,xii</i>)

>  <b>HAWKINS</b> Although much sheep country was freeholded during the seventies, the Canterbury wool clip did not decline in quantity or value. <i>(1957,Beyond the Waimakariri,309</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> By the end of the 1870s the best land in the central coastal plains and hill country from Napier to Takapau had been freeholded ... <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,99</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [931 ca1890-1910 Acland Sheep-station Glossary (Part freeholded) to make freehold; to buy the freehold of run country.]

>  A reader ... asks the question, `Does it pay to freehold?' He is on a 486-acre section, with a 33-year lease in perpetuity, and has the right to freehold the section at Land and Survey Department valuation if wishes. <i>(Jul 7, 1957,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

>  If we ever manage to freehold our farm, what would we pay in land tax? ... there would be no change just because you freeholded the property. <i>(Jun 26, 1958,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

### freeholding
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the providing of land for private ownership.

>  The Government must provide more funding for the Crown to buy out the lessee's interest in those lease with outstanding conservation, landscape and recreation values, where there is little land suitable for freeholding. <i>(May 2002,Conservation News,1</i>)

### freeloader
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a working dog that does not work effectively as part of a team.

>  <b>CURTIS</b> Make no mistake though, that yellow dog was no freeloader, he earned his way and then some. <i>(1997,Murphy's Law and the Hunter,96</i>)

### freepost
 
 <i>n.</i> when mail is not charged for

>  Post had to publicly humble itself by restoring full rural delivery and subsidies, and staging massive general PR apologies such as reducing the price of stamps and having a freepost day, to re-endear itself to the public and politicians. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to send by Freepost without stamps

>  3 December 2003. <i>NZ Herald</i> reports that businesses " will be able to freepost their payments of PAYE, GST and fringe benefits tax from the beginning of next year." Is this the first use of freepost as a verb? <i>(Feb 2004,Mail Coach, The,70</i>)

>  [Businesses] will be able to freepost their payments of PAYE, GST and fringe benefits tax from the beginning of next year. <i>(Dec 3, 2003,NZ Herald</i>)

### freeze (on)
 
 <i>v.</i> to refuse to share [(prisoners')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> FREEZE (ON) (v) Refuse to share <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

### freeze one's cursor
 
 <i>phr.</i> to irritate?

>  It's the loss of social poignancy that FREEZES MY CURSER. Detached, faceless and artless is a large part of technological encounters. <i>(Jul 23, 1997,Capital Times,2</i>)

### freeze-frame
 
 <i>v.</i> make no changes to

>  Science Minister Pete Hodgson said there was a very strong argument for creating new science links at Otago and Massey universities. "Freeze-framing Wallaceville doesn't make sense." <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### freeze-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> At the end of the evening someone suggested we finish up with a `freeze out' and this was agreed to. The rules of this form of sport were simple. Each player has to have three threepenny pieces and on the first round all put in one threepence. An ordinary poker hand was then dealt and cards were bought as required. There was no betting as all hands were tabled face up and the man with the worst hand was `frozen out'. All he had to do however, was to put in his second threepence and he was in again. After losing three threepences, however, he was frozen out for good. This went on until only two players remained and on the final game the winner took the whole kitty! <i>(1975,Men of the Milford Road,54</i>)

### freezer
 
 <i>n.</i> freezing works

>  <b>FARRELL</b> So Martin moved on, rousie for a gang, a spell at the freezer in Prestwick, a few months on a fishing boat round Stewart Island. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Yarding Sheep. Ready for the Freezer. <i>(1913,Country Work,caption facing 32</i>)

>  Probably the most common trouble is the so-called `store-' or `freezer-burn' which does not become apparent until the goods have been frozen for a considerable time. These comprise unsightly light-red patches ... `Burn' is widely held to be caused by the low temperatures to which these delicate tissues are subjected in the process of freezing and storage. <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,15/248</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal to be killed at freezing works and exported frozen in full carcase.

>  Breeding freezers: are we on right lines? <i>(Aug 1912,NZ Farmer,922</i>)

>  .. in a year or so we can re-ship these beasts back to Canterbury as heavy, fat and prime freezers. <i>(Nov 1891,NZ Farmer,472</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> In April 1892 stock sales were becoming more successful than previously, there being large entries and a steady supply of freezers ... <i>(1975,History of Fortrose, A,98</i>)

>  The opening of the new freezing company's works at Islington has imported a little life to the fat sheep market and really prime 'freezers' (crossbred wethers and maiden ewes) are worth 2 and a half d. per lb, shipping weight. <i>(1887,NZ Farmer,359</i>)

>  The New Zealand Shipping Company have been purchasing 'freezers' to ship of their own account, and this fact, coupled with the scarcity of fat sheep in Otago ... <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,290</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> .. Be they freezers or crawlers or wethers or ewes. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,21</i>)

>  We noticed that Romney flocks were still kept by owners, who yearly sold large numbers of freezers. <i>(Mar 1895,NZ Farmer,92</i>)

>  Mr Rowland's opinion is borne out by the fact that in a line of freezers which he had shipped through the National Mortgage Company, and which were killed at Longburn, the whole of the sheep were perfectly white, not a yellow one being amongst them. Lambs, prime freezers 11s to 12s 2d. <i>(May 1897,NZ Farmer,162</i>)

>  The high prices that have been ruling for freezers had inflated the sheep market. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,218</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> .. how to tell the 'freezers' from a line of 'butcher's prime.' <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,18</i>)

### freezer beast
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cattle beast to be killed for home consumption, i.e. on a property.

>  [advertisement] FREEZER beast, ready now, $3.20 per kg. Ph 323 8823. <i>(Nov 22, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9</i>)

>  Freezer beasts wanted (6) ready for mid-November, on site slaughter by home kill. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,42</i>)

>  .. the amount of milk is generally quite a small volume and dare I say it there are a lot of jobs that don't supply a freezer beast. <i>(Feb 23, 2001,Fencepost.com,Moozelee</i>)

### freezer ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe grazed for homekill and consumption.

>  .. there'll be more than that next year because I kept all my freezer ewes. <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,8</i>)

### freezer pig
 
 <i>n.</i> a pig suitable for meat rather than breeding

>  [advertisement] Large freezer pig for sale. Not suitable breeding. <i>(Oct 28, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,41</i>)

### freezing
 
 <i>n.</i> (employment in) the frozen meat industry

>  This industry gives employment to a large number of persons, both directly to those engaged in breeding, shearing, freezing, and shipping, and indirectly to those who labour at bushfelling, grass-seed sowing, fencing, and otherwise opening up new country. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,11</i>)

### freezing chamber
 
 <i>n.</i> [figurative use noted in the 1959 citation]

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> The sheep ... are then transferred to the first freezing-chamber, where the temperature is about 10 degrees below freezing-point ... <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,80</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>(1889,New Zealand After 50 Years</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In 1891 the [Legislative] Council passed the taxation bill and a bill, which both parties supported, to alter the tenure o[f] future appointments to the Legislative Council from life to seven years. It rejected every other important Liberal measure, thus earning the name of `the freezing chamber'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,167</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PERRIAM</b> Off the cooling floor was a bank of six freezing chambers. <i>(1989,Where it all Began,91</i>)

>  During his voyage out on the Kaikoura, Mr. Far, who was in charge of the salmon ova, was twice locked in the freezing chamber. <i>(Mar 8, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  Each of the six freezing chambers can accommodate 1000 sheep. <i>(Jan 18, 1900,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> Well, that's what Max used to call me - all full of self-righteousness after his vacation work in a freezing chamber or something. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,49</i>) [NOTES: 1978 edition]

>  The storing capacity of the freezing chambers is equal to 30,000 carcases. <i>(Oct 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### freezing company
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Within the last few years several of the freezing companies in New Zealand have erected machinery for freezing by the compression ... of ammonia ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,312</i>)

>  Under normal conditions the freezing season is from about the middle of November to May or June. The fat stock - i.e., cattle, sheep, and lambs - are ... bought outright from the farmer by the freezing company or meat-exporter ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/164</i>)

### freezing establishment
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> The flocks of sheep are consigned to the company owning the freezing establishment, which is erected in an open plain near the railway ... <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,78</i>)

### freezing ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe destined for slaughter and possibly export as mutton.

>  The extra large yarding in the fat sheep section was made up almost wholly of freezing ewes ... <i>(Mar 11, 1943,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

### freezing factory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  And, again, there are the highly cultivated properties within easy distances of the freezing-factories, where fat lambs are grown on rape, turnips, and clover, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,96</i>)

### freezing house
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a freezing works

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> These gentlemen [Messrs Nelson Brothers] have under their own control the many manufactures subsidiary to the slaughter and freezing-houses, and their works [at Napier] have increased enormously since they were first started a few years ago. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes</i>)

### freezing industry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> You squatted, as the saying was, with mighty few sheep, and as the freezing industry was unknown, waited patiently for the natural increase to pile your sheep figures to astronomical heights. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,56</i>)

### freezing sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or lamb grown for the export meat trade.

>  The market was well-supplied, and there was an improved demand for all classes, the advance being particularly noticeable in freezing sheep and best ewes. <i>(Apr 25, 1896,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  The buyers of the freezing sheep are not keen at present. <i>(Sep 1901,NZ Farmer,410</i>)

>  Prime freezing sorts [of fat lambs] sold about the same as last week. [freezing sort] <i>(Feb 10, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,7</i>)

### freezing stock
 
 <i>n.</i> sheep and cattle bred and grown for the export meat trade.

>  For freezing stock, buyers seemed to have formed a corner, and this class suffered slightly, but all the other sorts showed an improvement. <i>(May 15, 1897,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### freezing wether
 
 <i>n.</i> a wether sheep for slaughter and freezing

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In 1885, skins off freezing wethers were worth one shilling each. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,95</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wether bred and grown for the export meat trade.

>  A fairly large entry of fat sheep was made up mostly of crossbred ewes, with a few pens of prime freezing wethers and some very nice merinos. <i>(May 15, 1897,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Freezing wethers sold rather above late rates ... <i>(Feb 10, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,7</i>)

### freezing worker
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who works at a freezing works

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I'd like to see some of these people ... who criticise freezing workers for <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Presently a foreman appeared, one of those iron men who can manage freezing workers and to whom an enraged steer is a minor worry. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,63</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> One of my neighbours, a freezing-worker ..., owned a guitar. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,226</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>MASON</b> He's never been a doctor! Freezing worker. <i>(1969,Awatea,29,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> ... they said she had a mouth like a freezing worker, but even that wouldn't stop her. <i>(1978,Boy, The Bridge, The River, The,118</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> My name is Tu the freezing worker./ Ngati D.B. is my tribe./ The pub is my Marae./ My fist is my taiaha. <i>(1979,Eyes of the Ruru,15</i>) [NOTES: (Sad Joke on a Marae)]

>  In 1932, wages were cut, and the freezing workers struck throughout the country. The butchers didn't believe that they could be replaced, but when the works reopened it was found that the companies had installed the American chain system, with each worker responsible for only one aspect of the process. The butchers were replaced by the unemployed, who initially made a terrible mess of the carcasses. But in time they became competent process workers, as opposed to the tradesmen who had preceded them. <i>Ibid.</i> 86. The freezing workers sent in a short line-out to meet them: Kennedy and Scott from the New Zealand union, Evans from Auckland and Douglas and Knox from the FOL. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,76</i>)

>  <b>STEAD</b> Ljuba Panapa was a neighbour, almost a friend, Dalmatian-Catholic of course, but now a young war-widow, which somehow made her more acceptable than she'd seemed as the wife of a Maori freezing worker. <i>(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,42</i>)

>  If you were to ask 100 New Zealanders "What do you call the people who work on a slaughter chain?", the overwhelming answer would be "freezing workers" - not "meat processing plant workers", "steak manufacturers" or "fresh and wholesome table product converters"! <i>(Dec 1999,North & South,23</i>)

>  In reference to the series of agreements from the Southland Freezing Worker's [sic] Union, the men affected do various kinds of work under different conditions ... <i>(Feb 6, 1908,West Coast Times</i>)

### freezing works
 
 <i>n.</i> an abattoir or factory where livestock are slaughtered and their carcasses frozen [rare early use freezing work]

>  Freezing is going on energetically at both freezing works. <i>(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,V/10</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> From experience of companies now working (in other countries), it is found that the operations can best be carried on by having the freezing works away from the port of shipment but adjacent to a line of railway, ... and where paddocking can be got for holding the stock until slaughtered. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,77</i>) [NOTES: 1881 Dec 21 Report of Committee on Frozen Meat Trade in 1957 Macdonald Canterbury Frozen Meat Company 77]

>  Went to Mr Nelsons at To Moana; saw his freezing, boiling and tining[????] works and a wool drying machine ... one that pays. <i>(Jul 5, 1887,Chudleigh Diary,355</i>)

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> ... we visited the freezing works where the mutton and beef is frozen before [4] shipment to Britain. <i>(1888,Far South,3</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... having freezing-works on shore. <i>(1889,New Zealand After 50 Years,130</i>)

>  Also saw the manager of the Islington freezing works. <i>(Sep 28, 1891,Chudleigh Diary,378,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> ... at Gisborne, where Nelson Brothers have recently erected a freezing work [sic]. <i>Ibid.</i> 446 ... the author visited the freezing works of Nelson Brothers, 15 Downgate Hill, London,and found excellent New Zealand lamb stored there. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,445</i>)

>  The solution of all their difficulties ... was found to lie in having freezing-works on shore, near to the place of shipment ... In other cases ... the freezing-works consist of a large building with a [freezing-]chamber, and powerful engines constantly at work. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,138</i>)

>  The collateral industries which are carried on in connection with large freezing establishments are scarcely less important that the freezing-works themselves. Side by side with the latter are to be found tallow-works, ... fellmongeries, meat-tinning works, .... oil- and manure-works. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,313</i>)

>  Near the new freezing-works there is a pumiceous ... material ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/567</i>) [NOTES: (Hill)]

>  Messrs. Nelson Brothers, of the meat-freezing works at Tomoana, kindly offered to receive and paddock any black lambs forwarded ... to their works until such time as a drive of black lambs should be collected together <i>(1900,TrNZI,33/197</i>)

>  In September, 1896, a new work in connection with the dairying and meat industries was started in the shape of the Taranaki Freezing-works. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,9</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> The buyers from the freezing works were picking their sheep, and many were the pressings and pinches bestowed on each indignant animal before he was decided worthy of the honour of becoming a `freezer.' <i>(1907,Return of Joe,177</i>)

>  FREEZING-WORKS AT OWENGA. [Hdg] <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/51</i>)

>  The Wairoa Freezing Works and Bridge are gone. <i>(Mar 8, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/117</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>LILICO</b> A bitch pup I sold ... went direct to freezing works to pen up and developed into a first class forcer and backer ... The finest backing dogs I have ever seen are in the saleyards at Addington ... <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,26</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> [Thomas Sutcliffe] Mort established the first freezing works [115] in the world near Sydney in 1861. <i>(1938,High Country,114</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... the Gladston agents of overseas meat interests ... had him first repair freezing works then pull them down ... <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,11,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> We live on slaughter in this country, butchery buys the cars and the cake-mixers for those who know the freezing works only as a smell when the wind is in the south. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,13</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The cattle still had to be trucked to the freezing works. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,221</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Freezing works has been claimed as a New Zealandism, though it occurs in the index of a book published in England, J.T. Critchell and J.Raymond, A History of the Frozen Meat Trade (Constable, 1912). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,175</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> `If that's on the cards, let's get few flagons,' said Entwhistle quickly. `After all the eagle shits tomorrow.' This was freezing workers' slang for pay-day. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,29</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Freezing Works. This is the place where New Zealand's major exports are converted from meat on the hoof to fastidiously prepared sides or whole carcasses for overseas consumption. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,42</i>)

>  Freezing Works: A factory where livestock are slaughtered and their carcasses frozen. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  freezing works n. Austral. & N.Z. a slaughterhouse at which animal carcasses are frozen for export. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,444</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 301; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 197]]

>  <b>LELAND</b> ...like every other industry in the country the freezing works have been hit by 'restructuring' which means that fewer works, and workers, are doing what used to be done by many more firms and workers. <i>(1990,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,67,1994 ed.</i>)

>  Our factories are no longer "freezing works" that simply slaughter anbimals and freeze down the resultant products or carcasses. <i>(Oct 1999,North & South,18</i>)

>  [Sir Howard Morrison] went to a church college then on to what he calls the higher school of learning - the Whakatu Freezing Works. <i>(Oct 11, 2002,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  [Advt]. Formerly a Freezing Works - remodelled. <i>(Nov 27, 1926,NZ Observer,41</i>)

>  The big railway station is ghostly, the town has lost its freezing works and most of its industry. <i>(Nov 28, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  H L & R M were greatly interested with the boys at the freezing works wharf. <i>(Mar 19, 1904,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  I would arrive on the bus in the dark, with the freezing works looming out of the mist like a Victorian prison. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,134</i>)

>  The establishment of the Auckland Freezing Works.. is now accomplished. <i>(Feb 2, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 6</i>)

>  The power could not be switched off on account of the freezing works supply. <i>(Nov 17, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  His one ... companion was a freezing-works chain slaughter-man (with appropriate gestures he explained his job as a brisket-puncher) ... <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,4</i>) [NOTES: 19? Sargeson `A Final Cure' in 1975 NZ Short Stories III 4]

>  Payment is made on freezing works killing sheets. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,629</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> A second son wanted freezing works jobs also, but the father refused ... <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [check that this was not a (former) freezing works for meat]

[<i>Urtica ferox</i>]]] both which grow to the size of small trees; a shruby [sic] Speedwell [fnf Veronica. [ed. [<i>Hebe</i> sp.]]] found near all the Beeches, Sow thistles, Virgins Bower, Vanelloe, French willow, [ed. fn 1 `French willow' was one of the popular English names for the willow-herb, <i>Epilobium</i> sp. There are many species in New Zealand, most of them native and very widespread.] Euphorbia, [ed. fn 2 <i>Euphorbia glauca</i>.] and Cranes bill ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/804-805</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/804-805 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

### Frenchman
 
 <i>n.</i> the fish, <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> FLATHEAD <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> Haast ... Other names for the Flathead are Chinaman and Frenchman, but where these names originated and for what reason I do not know with certainty. One theory is that when Chinamen hawked fish about Dunedin they kept Flatheads for their own consumption, as they preferred them to all other fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,286,(2nd edn) ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FRENCHMAN: see Flathead <i>Ibid.</i> 100 [The Flathead, <i>Kathetostoma</i> sp. is sometimes called Frenchman or Chinaman (presumably because these fishermen sold them in the early Otago days). The Latin translation of the name means roughly `large, perpendicular mouth.' Also known as Spotted Stargazer. <i>Ibid.</i> 100 FRENCHMAN Another name for Flathead. Also known as Chinaman. The latter name apparently derives from the fact that Chinese fish merchants in Dunedin in the early days always kept these fish for their own consumption. Nowadays these fish are more accurately termed Spotted Stargazer. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,74</i>)

### fresh
 
 <i>n.</i> a rush of water in a river or creek, a flash flood

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... the river follows the western hills, and at its freshes overflows the left shore, which was covered with shrublike veronica. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/77</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> During the freshes, which occur after heavy rains, the natives come down the river in their canoes by hundreds to see their friends. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,26</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The hot Northwesters melt the Mountain snows, sending down freshes. A fresh was just beginning in the Rangitata as we got to it. <i>(1856,Journal of Henry Sewell,2/221,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I had to cross the Waimakiriri just after a fresh, ... and I assure you I did not like it. I crossed it first on the plains, where it flows between two very high terraces ... of which the most northern must be ... 300 feet high. <i>(Mar 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,v/62,1863 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ...having never crossed any of the rivers on horseback in a fresh, having never seen the Rangitata in a fresh ... it may be imagined that I felt a certain amount of caution to be necessary ... <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,38,1960 ed.</i>)

>  We are in a state of doubt about the Racia ... there is every chance of the river being bank high and not passable for a week, the first fresh is always the highest. <i>(Sep 26, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,60</i>)

>  The `freshets' may float the logs down from the forests to the sawing stations. ... [The trees] lie partly buried in the mud waiting until the `fresh' comes. [NB quotes - an English article?] <i>(1862,NZ Magazine,161</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> None of us had ever seen a river in what the settlers there [Takaka/ Nelson] call a `freshet' [i.e. water in the back watersheds coming down]. <i>Ibid.</i> 224 [In] a rainy season, during which the snow gets melted on the mountains, the river rises in what is termed a `fresh.' <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,156</i>)

>  <b>NUELLER</b> The weather has been frightful, and repeatedly pegs ... were swept away by heavy freshes ... <i>(Dec 1, 1865,My Dear Bannie,78,1958 ed.</i>)

>  ... a heavy fresh occurred [in the West Coast river]. <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/46/2</i>)

>  Fresh in river. <i>(Jan 20, 1866,Hokitika Signal Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... I have had one short experience with a summer `fresh' ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,61</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> For many years therefore the Waimumu Stream had to be forded at the tailings and on this occasion a large `fresh' had scoured out the bank. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,385</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> ... in consequence of a bridge having been carried away by a fresh in the river, had to cross the river at a temporary bridge ... <i>(Sep 5, 1868,Waikato Journals,152,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ..the river at our first crossing had been still in a state of fresh ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,52</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> [a rise in a stream; also ordinary text in later usages] that a `fresh' would soon be down <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,103</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  1907 19 Feb - ...[sic] creeks jammed [with logs] for want of a fresh to clear them ...[sic] <i>(Feb 19, 1907,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1907 Feb 19 in 1980 Rogers The Fowlds Papers - Health 2 [Letter to Fowlds from E. O'Brien]]

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> Heavy rain fell and a `fresh' was the result. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,14</i>)

>  Noun and verb--flood (in river). (Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p.104) [Recorded in English from the sixteenth century onwards - A.W.] <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> At that time there was no bridge over the Waipawa river between Onga and Tikokino, and the crossing, especially if there was a fresh in the river, was a tricky one. [1910's] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,119</i>)

>  fresh ... 3 n. NZ freshet. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 445, sense 17, & 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 197]]

>  <b>SANSOM</b> When a spirit was `fresh', that was for a few days following the burial, he wouldn't risk the track home [on Stewart Island] at all. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,120</i>)

>  A 'fresh' in the river had the effect of clearing the bar. <i>(Sep 16, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a freshman [(obs univ)]

 <i>n.</i> [1951 16-18 M 26 Marlborough C. 25 FRESHERS -->1951 16-18 M 32 St Bede's, Chch 25 FRESHERS -->1960 17C F B9 Wanganui Girls C.G 25 FRESHERS -->1951 15 F 9 Marlborough C. 25 FRESHERS (4)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Ever since he had climbed the wall to get grog for a dance, a gloomy and celibate fresher, it [the pub] had been his thieves' kitchen ... <i>(1985,Horse,44</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Quick and the Dead) 44]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Ever since he had climbed the wall to get grog for a dance, a gloomy and celibate fresher, it [the pub] had been his thieves' kitchen ... <i>(1985,Horse,44</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Quick and the Dead) 44]
