### freshwater crayfish
 
 <i>n.</i> Paranephrops spp., koura, crawler, crawlie [T. J. Parker, `Studies in Biology' (Colonial Museum and Geological Survey Department, New Zealand), p. 5: - `Paranephrops which is small and has to be specially collected in rivers, creeks or lakes.']

>  <b>TINNE</b> I had heard ... of the fresh-water cray-fish (goura) that are found in these lakes ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,17</i>)

>  The small <i>cray-fish</i> in myriads bred,/ With sunk fern-bundles lifted from the Lake:/ Next pounded<i> fern-root</i> pounded to a cake,/ ...; and the choicer roots,/ .../ The <i>kumara</i> [u-acute] and <i>taro.</i> [a-acute] <i>Ibid.</i> 1/305 Crayfish ..... three or four inches long are caught in abundance in the central lakes in manner described. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/184</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ The Australasian <i>cray-fish</i> belong to the family <i>Parastacida</i>, the members of which are confined to the southern hemisphere, whilst those of the family <i>Potamobiida</i> are found in the northern hemisphere. Victoria; <i>Paranephrops,</i> found in the fresh waters of New Zealand.The species are as follows:- (5) <i>New Zealand Fresh-water Cray-fish</i>. Name applied to <i>Paranephrops zealandicus</i>, white, which is confined to the fresh water of New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English,105</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The other principal species formerly taken were the inanga and kokopu <i>(Galaxias</i> and <i>Retropinna)</i>, upokororo <i>(Prototractes)</i>, and panoko <i>(Cheimarrichthys)</i>; while the lamprey (piharau and korokoro) and fresh water crayfish (koura) were taken in considerable numbers in some districts. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,71</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The newcomer's attention was never caught by the kakahi shellfish which is quite common in the Waikaka, but they noticed an occasional koura or freshwater crayfish. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,6</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 6]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> His introduction to the freshwater crayfish, for instance, was not without grief and sweat. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,44,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Freshwater Crayfish - kekewai, kewai, koura wai, <i>Parenephrops planifrons </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>HINZ</b> The trout will take the smaller freshwater crayfish whole, but even the smaller koura can be bigger than a man's thumb. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,67</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... Those little freshwater crayfish up the Cobb tasted sweet. I could go one now. I would love one now. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,114</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> CRAYFISH (freshwater) ... A successful method of catching freshwater crayfish or `koura' as he is known to the Maoris, is to use their method. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,42</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Sometimes on Sunday afternoons, before milking began around half past three, we'd go down to the creek at the back of the farm to catch koura, those small freshwater crayfish we called crawlies. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,5</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> In the creek that ran behind town young Maureen and Alice used to catch cockabullies and fresh-water crayfish, then tip them back and watch them swim away. <i>(1986,Then Again,108</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The creeks [up-country from Raglan c1914-1018] were full of eels, butterfish and crawlies (freshwater crayfish), so we never lacked in a variety of foods. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,27</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> ... boys of my age came from the stream with freshwater crayfish, ... <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,71</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

MATHEW</b> ... those channels [of inland inlets], especially where they are joined by freshwater creeks ... 1860 <i>Ibid.</i> 16 ... freshwater creek, which flows through a considerable extent of extent of forest, and affords facilities to float the timbers down. <i>(1840,Reports and Essays,4</i>)

>  From the extreme value of allotments with water frontages for commercial people, the [Auckland] waterside was subdivided into very small allotments ... but two of the best of these [allotments], bordering on a fresh-water creek, which runs down through the valley, have been selected by Government officers. <i>(Jul 19, 1841,GBPP,(No.569) 134</i>) [NOTES: 1841 July 19 D.Sinclair to Lord John Russell in GBPP 1842]

### freshwater flounder
 
 <i>n.</i> = black flounder

>  <i>Rhombosolea retiaria:</i> Black or fresh-water flounder. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> FLOUNDER (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia (ii) Rhombosolea retiaria (iii) Rhombosolea tapirina (iv) Rhombosolea leporina</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLOUNDER, BLACK More sole-like in shape than his cousin flounders this fellow also has small, brick-red spots on his back and has the ability - unlike the other flatfish - of being able to live in pure, fresh water. Other names are`River Flounder' and `Mud or Freshwater Flounder'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,72</i>)

>  [species name] black flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> .. [other name(s)] blacks, freshwater flounder, river flounder <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Black flounder <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Restricted to New Zealand. Alternative names include river flounder, freshwater flounder, or estuary flounder, derived from its habitat. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,144</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flounder, freshwater 2287 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### freshwater mussel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ten different species of mussel, including a freshwater one, inhabit New Zealand waters... <i>(Feb 19, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The fresh-water mussel and the crayfish are plentiful in some places. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/30</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> FRESH-WATER MUSSEL (Hyridella menziesi). This or related species or subspecies are found buried in mud in most of our lakes, rivers and streams. They grow from 2 to 4 inches in length, and are covered with a thick dark green horny epidermis.... This is the kakahi of the Maoris, formerly much favoured by them as a special food for young children and the sick. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... I found ... one of Mr. Brunner's old camps, near which were plenty of freshwater mussel-shells (unio), and some felled trees. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,9</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Freshwater Mussel - kaeo, kakahi, ngaeo, <i>Hyridella menziesi,</i> etc. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The freshwater bivalve <i>Hyridella</i> ... is found in many of our lakes, rivers and creeks, where it lives buried beneath the mud. In this freshwater mussel, as it is often called, the outer layer of the shell is very thick, acting as a protective coat ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,80</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Sean breakfasted on fresh-water mussels from the lake... <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,214</i>)

### Freyberg's Circus
 
 <i>n.</i> the 2NZEF Division [(WW2)]

>  `Freyberg's Circus' (as the troops often called it) was maintained as much by rank-and-file laughter, horseplay, mischief, and the assertive, rumbunctious, enterprising spirits of autonomous man as it was by any subservience to conventional military organisation or to aspirations of professionalism. <i>(Jun 7, 1986,Dominion,9</i>) [NOTES: (Les Cleveland)]

>  Rather than "Freyberg's Circus" (the troops' self-derisive term), I felt that here, in this well produced and illustrated book, was a thoughtful indication of a substantial stratum of RSA officials and their outlook. <i>(Mar 13, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),73</i>)

### fribby
 
 <i>adj.</i> Applies to small short locks of wool. Also as n (usu pl), such locks. Also frib (usually pl) short wool pieces and second cuts. 1900 Aust 1915 MacDonald NZ Sheepfarming 26/69 1929 H.B. Smith Sheep and Wool Industry in NZ (ed 3) 209 1951 Ac ECR 379] sc. of wool ?rarely @n [(woolbuyers')]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> When the fleece is placed on the table, outside uppermost, the stained and fribby pieces should be taken off the edges, <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,69</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The fribby bits that fall on the board, when shaken from the fleece, should be swept under the table and left there until there is sufficient to make a bale. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,122</i>)

>  Fribby: Perhaps more a trade term than a station one. The yolky locks round the points taken off by the roller from a decently skirted fleece. <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Fribby.-Perhaps more a wool trade term than a station one. The yolky locks round the points taken off by the roller from a decently skirted fleece. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  fribby a. NZ & Austral. (of locks of wool) small or short. [orig. unkn.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,302</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of wool] short and stained.

>  <b>PERRY</b> The fribby bits that fall off the board, when shaken from the fleece, should be swept under the table. <i>(1922,NZ Sheepfarming,122</i>)

>  <b>BISLEY</b> A Few Technical Terms Used in the Wool Trade .. Fribby.- Small, short. <i>(1897,Practical Treatise on Wool and Sheep Breeding,49</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> When the fleece is placed on the table, outside uppermost, the stained and fribby pieces should be taken off the edges. <i>(1915,NZ Farmer,69</i>)

>  At that line the 75mm fleece wool faded into wool which at its centre was no more than 12mm long and fribby rubbish at that. <i>(Aug 1993,NZ Sheep Farmer,4</i>)

### friendly
 
 <i>n.</i> [plural uses]

>  The "friendlies" have naturally been led to believe that they are indispensable. <i>(Feb 20, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  With one hand full of medals he called the "friendlies" to him to be decorated. <i>(Apr 3, 1926,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  Friendlies were sometimes not distinguishable. <i>(May 24, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 2</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> Some of the main tribes fought alongside the settlers during the Anglo-Maori wars, and became known as the kupapa or 'friendlies'. <i>(1978,Looking Back,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> friendly Maori; sc. those `friendly' or loyal to the Crown during the New Zealand Wars of the mid-19th century [(usu. pl)]

>  No natives about except the `friendlies' at Poutoko and Hauranga . <i>(May 18, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/707,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  From that day forward there was nothing going on except a repetition of false alarms and a circulation of Maori threats, through the Waiuku `friendlies'; ... <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,30</i>)

>  During all this, there were not more than half a dozen `friendlies' under fire [from the Hauhaus]. <i>(Jan 20, 1866,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Jan 20 Otago Witness (Lieut, St. George) in 1985 Bramby Eye Witness History 45]

>  <b>28 T.S.GRACE</b> Wherever we go now as Missionaries, whether amongst the Friendlies or Kingites, ... <i>(Nov 28, 1868,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Nov 28 T.S.GRACE letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 204]

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> Two hundred Friendlies met with Heta. <i>(1869,Letters & Journals,IV/554</i>)

>  <b>HAWTHORNE</b> The reason why they were allowed to escape the halter was this - in most cases the murderers were connected with friendly natives, and it was thought desirable not to offend those `friendlies', as they might prevent the settlement of the land question. <i>(1869,Dark Chapter from NZ History,25</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> This war is a terrible nuisance I vow, ... By foes and friendlies we have often been had, But to have to feed captives is rather too bad. <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,14</i>) [NOTES: Maori Captives]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> The service rendered by `friendlies' is commemorated by this monument [at Wanganui]. <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,194</i>)

>  There is much that is objectionable in the Kingites, but they are, at least, fifty per cent. better then the Friendlies. <i>(1877,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Aug T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 266]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Accompanied by a force of 500 `friendlies' he pursued the enemy from point to point and finally captured the whole of them ... [at] Te Teko. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/254</i>)

>  After the battle of Moutoa, in 1863, when so many `friendlies' fell fighting on our side against the hauhaus ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/150</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  One telegram stated that body of `friendlies' in the pay of the colonists, well-armed, well-drilled, and with plenty of ammunition had gone over to the enemy ... They belonged to the Arawa tribe. <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65/7/1</i>) [NOTES: [referring to the Anglo-Maori Wars, 1869]]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Instead, it urged, the Government should divide disputed land and buy whatever proportion belonged to the `friendlies' or `the progress party', as the settlers called the `land sellers'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,121</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> The Maori troops who fought for the British queen were called `friendlies', and the performance of the `friendlies' in the 1845 rebellion was appreciated sufficiently by the Government to encourage the passing of several Native Forces Acts. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,38</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> of a Maori, friendly towards the Pakeha side in the NZ Wars see friendly @n

>  <b>POWER</b> ... it being found impracticable to make the friendly Maoris take to the bush again; and without them our small force could do nothing.) <i>(1846,Sketches in NZ,34</i>)

>  Waka and other friendly chiefs were perfectly confounded as to the intention of the Government. <i>(Jul 12, 1847,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1847 July 12 H. Williams to Selwyn in 1874 Carleton Life of Henry Williams 2/157 in 1986 Belich NZ Wars 34]

>  The friendly party of natives (so it is stated) are sowing wheat and planting potatoes on the Waiwakaiho Block ... It is feared by them that Henry Epuni would crop the land if the friendly natives did not. <i>(Jul 28, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/151,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  `That one of the two parties into which the natives are divided (commonly styled the friendly party) claims a right to the support of the British Government. <i>(Aug 18, 1855,Australian & NZ Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Aug 18 Australian and NZ Gazette in 1856 Fitton NZ 161]

>  On my journey to and from Auckland, when I came in contact with both friendly and hostile Maoris, I found one opinion only, namely, that the war had begun hastily without the counsel of the Bishop or Missionaries having been sought. <i>(May 28, 1860,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1860 May 28 T.S.GRACE letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 91]

>  I intend to go to Waitara this evening to induce the friendly Natives to occupy the Mahoetahi Pa. <i>(Aug 31, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/631</i>) [NOTES: (D. McLean)]

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> At the outset of the war, fears were entertained that the present Government would show too uch leniency towards the rebels, and place too much confidence in what are called `friendly' natives. <i>(Aug 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Aug WILLIAM MORGAN Journal (1963) 67]

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> Today a lot of friendly natives came to see the Governor. <i>(May 14, 1864,Journal,222</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> There is a creek here where we bathe every morning and there is a settlement of Friendly natives at the other side of it ... and laying it down in English grass, thus making a permanent estate of it. <i>(Feb 24, 1865,Journal</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> An auxiliary force of 400 `friendly' Ngapuhi accompanied the Redcoats and provided them with intelligence of Hone Heke's movements ... <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,15</i>)

>  In the winter of 1846, troops of the 65th Regiment, abetted by Willie Swanson, who had been made one of the leaders of a group of 150 "friendly" Maori, drove marauding Maori from the Hutt Valley. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/95</i>)

### friendly Native
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [friendly](fly-fuz.md#friendly))

>  <b>ELDER</b> After Captain Graham returned to his vessel, my friendly natives being up the country insearch of potatoes, a strange party came over from the other side of the Bay and got upon the sawyer's house-top, and I went to desire them to come down. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,124</i>) [NOTES: 1816 Jan 26 William Hall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 124]

>  <b>POWER</b> [He recommended me] not to attempt to ascend the mountain till the following day, when he ... and Major Durie with the armed police, intended to join the friendly natives. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,25</i>)

>  They [the soldiers] found fires alight [etc] but no natives. It is said they were fore-warned by `Friendly Natives'. <i>(Sep 12, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/634,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAWTHORNE</b> The assassins, guided by three resident and professedly `friendly natives' of Poverty Bay, came to Captain Wilson's house ... <i>Ibid.</i> 25 The reason why they were allowed to escape the halter was this - in most cases the murderers were connected with friendly natives, and it was thought desirable not to offend those `friendlies', as they might prevent the settlement of the land question. <i>(1869,Dark Chapter from NZ History,21</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> The so-called friendly natives in the stockade, as soon as the hostile natives opened fire, ran away into the bush. <i>(1879,Waikato War,44</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Some of the friendly natives acompanied the white skirmishers; with them marched Jackey Marmon, the white cannibal warrior. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/150</i>)

>  That this Committee concurs in the report of the Joint Committee on the granting of the New Zealand War Medal to such of the Colonial forces and friendly Natives in all cases where the claimants have been actually under fire, or otherwise conspicuous for distinguished service in the field; and recommends that the medal be also given to the nearest relatives of any who have died of wounds or have been killed in action. <i>(1899,NZ Parliamentary Debates,VI/889</i>)

### Friendly Road, The
 
 <i>phr.</i> Scrimgeour's programme on radio in Auckland

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> During the depth of the depression Labour Party Meetings were revivalist gatherings where bitterness against evil times mingled with the most pure visions of tomorrow. In Auckland a radio station known as `The Friendly Road' run by the Reverend C.G. Scrimgeour (`Uncle Scrim'), broadcast the same evangelistic message in a programme called `The Man in the Street', ... <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,258</i>)

>  Travel the Friendly Road with Scrim. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Three years later Scrim exchanged the city theatres for a non-denominational 'radio church' on 1ZR (then 1ZB) spreading his philosophy on 'The Friendly Road' programme. <i>(Apr 2003,NZ Memories,No. 41/43</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> It was absorbing, exciting as the Depression cut deeper and deeper, the sugarbag and sandshoe years, Uncle Scrim and the Friendly Road. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,239</i>)

>  The meeting was held under the auspices of the Friendly Road... This meeting.. resolves that he be asked to advise the Friendly Road of his assurance that the hours and conditions of the Friendly Road Sessions will remain undisturbed. <i>(Oct 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

>  I am a "Friendly Rodent." Pardon, I mean "Friendly Roadian." Perhaps if I keep following the Friendly Road, I'll get a job worth GBP1500 a year some day. <i>(Oct 20, 1937,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  The Government had paid altogether GBP2100 for the Friendly Road Station, which, today, is 1ZB, a Government Commercial Station. <i>(Nov 24, 1937,NZ Truth,1</i>)

### frigate mackerel
 
 <i>n.</i> 'dark bluish above with a pattern like that of the blue mackerel' (Paul 128)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>FRIGATE TUNA </i>(Frigate mackerel) <i>Auxis thazard</i> ... The frigate tuna is a slender fish and is the smallest of the tunas found in the New Zealand region ... They are called bullets by tuna fishermen and can cause a great deal of trouble to purse seiners working skipjack schools [by gilling themselves in the net and taking time to remove]. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,293,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> COMMON MACKEREL ... <i>Other names: Scomber japonicus</i>; southern mackerel, English mackerel, frigate mackerel; tawatawa (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,267</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> COMMON MACKEREL [<i>Scomber australasicus</i>] ... The names southern mackerel, English mackerel, and sometimes frigate mackerel are in use. (The latter properly belongs to another species.) <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,48</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MACKEREL</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Scomber australasicus </i>(ii) <i>Trachurus declivis</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Blue Mackerel (ii) Jack mackerel. (c) Other common names: (i) Common Mackerel, Frigate mackerel, English mackerel (ii) Horse Mackerel. (d) Principal localities where taken: (both) Gisborne, Nelson, Picton .. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,34</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MACKEREL ... The common Mackerel is also known as the English Mackerel, Frigate Mackerel or Southern Mackerel. The Horse Mackerel is also known as the Yellowtail. The Koheru is also sometimes known as Yellowtail and is often misnamed Horse Mackerel and Scad. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,162</i>)

>  [species name] blue mackerel .. [scientific name] <i>Scomber australasicus</i> .. [other name(s)] English mackerel, common mackerel, frigate mackerel, pacific mackerel, southern mackerel <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>68/414</b> <i>Auxis thazard</i> Lacepede. Frigate mackerel. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 149. GRACULUS MELANOLEUCUS. Vieill. Frilled Shag. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,50</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PHALACROCORAX MELANOLEUCUS. (FRILLED SHAG.) <i>Phalacrocorax melanolencus,</i> Vieill. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,333,1888 2nd edn 2/173 ed.</i>)

>  There are thirteen species [of shag] in New Zealand <i>Phalacrocorax novae-hollandiae</i> (Black shag), <i>P. melanoleucus</i> (Frilled Shag), <i>P. brevirostris </i>(White-throated Shag), <i>P. varius </i> Pied shag, and <i>P. punctatus</i> (Spotted Shag), which I have often found in the inland bays, rivers, and lakes. The frilled shag is, owing to its cautious habits, called the `duckscarer' by the sportsmen living in the above - mentioned sound [Kenepuru]. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/100</i>)

>  <i>Little Pied Cormorant Phalacrocorax melanoleucos</i> (VIEILLOT, 1817) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Kawaupaka, white-throated shag, frilled shag, little river shag, little cormorant, little shag. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,117</i>)

### Fritz
 
 <i>exclam.</i> a child's call for truce, pax [(childrens')]

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... the way to ask for a truce in a game is to call Pax ... At the school I went to, the word was Fritz, and the truce was terminated by the call Fritz all over, but we knew that some schools used Fans (from south-west English dialect fain I or fainits?). Apparently some New Zealand schools use or have used baldy, which sounds like a variant of English barley. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,158</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

### Frodo economy
 
 <i>phr.</i> the benefits from the Lord of the Rings trilogy

>  As vast and impressive as the Alexanders' sheep farm is the unforeseen economic boom that followed the filming of Jackson's trilogy. Some locals call it the "Frodo economy" and it has rippled across New Zealand in unexpected ways... The Frodo economy first came calling in earnest on the Alexanders in the form of three young girls from overseas toting backpacks filled with drawings of Bag End, Frodo's home. <i>(Oct 31, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  [Hdg] Frodo economy grows stronger... The spinoffs from Peter Jackson's movie trilogy have created what has been dubbed the Frodo economy... <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  There have been no recent studies on what has been labelled by some as the "Frodo Economy", but an Institute of Economic Research report for the Film Commission, issued in April last year, estimated the filming of the $650 million project boosted the economy by $352 million the previous year. <i>(Nov 28, 2003,Dominion Post,AZ8</i>)

### frog
 
 <i>n.</i> [Gold Mining]

 <i>n.</i> [(two-up ?WW1)]

>  `Murder on the old sergeant-major.' `Ten frog half-way, cobber. Right, she's set.' `Come on, me lucky lads, you come 'ere in a pill-box and go away in a Rolls-Royce.' `The last spin, digs, so plank it down thick and heavy now; you pick 'em and I'll pay 'em.' <i>(Aug 10, 1921,Quick March,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a French letter, condom

>  <b>NORGROVE</b> `How do you ask a girl for a packet of frogs?' <i>(1987,Shoestring Sailors,50</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SCOTT</b> ... to `frog' a horse - which I will interpret for those of my readers who do not know the meaning of it. Take the horse's foot up and carefully make an incision in the frog half an inch deep, the horse then cannot walk for a week or two. <i>(1897,How I Stole 10,000 Sheep,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> road [ORIGIN: rhyming slang, 1961 Stage I University Student's essay on NZ Slang.]

 <i>n.</i> usu. boiled tapioca, or tapioca pudding; occas. sago or sago pudding (cf frog spawn tapioca or sago pudding) [(boarding-school slang) ca1935 St Patrick's College, Silverstream (fide Fr Alex McDonald) Opie Lang and Lore of Schoolchildren.1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 22B FROGS EYES-TAPIOCA]

 <i>phr.</i>

>  From Cape Reinga to the Bluff - we shall welcome them all. <i>(Aug 18, 1917,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

### from go to whoa
 
 <i>phr.</i> from start to finish [also (from) whoa to go?]

>  <b>McGILL</b> go to whoa, from from beginning to end; eg `I'd like to hear all the sordid details. Everthing - from go to whoa.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 26, 1958,Weekly News,45</i>)

>  `We were given only 12 months to organise the thing from go to whoa and trying to put in place significant sponsorship in less than 12 months was very difficult <i>(Aug 26, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,3</i>)

### from North Cape to the Bluff
 
 <i>phr.</i> from one end of New Zealand to the other; throughout New Zealand (See [also CAPE REINGA TO THE BLUFF; THREE KINGS TO THE SNARES (cf. British from Land's End to John O'Groats)](../A#also-cape-reinga-to-the-bluff;-three-kings-to-the-snares-(-british-from-land's-end-to-john-o'groats)))

>  Some of these introductions have largely displaced the original vegetation in many localities, from the North Cape to the Bluff. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2:132</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[eptospermum] ericioides</i>, A.Rich. ... Abundant from North Cape to the Bluff, ... Kanuka; Maru. ... Easily distinguished from [manuka] by its greater size, ... wood durable; much used for piles, house-blocks, posts and rails, &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,161</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> From the North Cape to the Bluff there is scarcely a spot ... in which the game is not played. Many of these outback townships are not connected by railway ... <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,163</i>)

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> From the North Cape to the Bluff there are thousands whose tongues become instantly garrulous in grateful reminiscence [of Selwyn]. <i>(1911,Selwyn,5</i>)

>  <b>MALFROY</b> [caption] TIMBER-JACK MADE IN NEW ZEALAND ... This is a ... well-tried-out jack which is extensively used in logging operations from North Cape to the Bluff <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,21</i>)

>  <b>CLOKE</b> When we see our far-flung Circuit,/ From the North Cape to the Bluff, <i>(1931,Songs of NZ,210</i>)

>  [Advertisement] From North Cape to the Bluff/only the best is good enough! Get Plume the best you can buy! <i>(Mar 6, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener),3</i>)

>  George Te Whaiti ... explained that Maoris `from North Cape to the Bluff' now came into the district for seasonal work. <i>(Jul 26, 1958,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> From North Cape to Bluff [title] <i>(1961,From North Cape to Bluff</i>)

>  From the North Cape to the Bluff the cash registers are tinkling. <i>(Nov 12, 1963,NZ Truth,37</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> We find so many interesting things to do ... just as our sisters do in the cities and towns and country places from North Cape to Bluff. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,131</i>)

>  INDUSTRIAL REACTION to the Government's wage restraints has been primed to burst countrywide in the middle of next month. -->`From North Cape to the Bluff,' Federation of Labour president Mr Jim Knox said yesterday. <i>(Sep 2, 1982,Evening Post,30</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> [He] cussed Seddonland from N.C. to Bluff. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,8</i>)

### from Stewart's Island to the North Cape
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... the coast climate of New Zealand from Stewart's Island to the North Cape may be described as most changeable <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/36</i>)

### from the Bluff to the Cape
 
 <i>phr.</i> from one end of New Zealand to the other

>  There ain't a dug-out.. nor a armed Hun from here to the Bluff, or from the Bluff to the Cape. <i>(Oct 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

### from the North Cape to Stewart Island
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Tennessee may stand for any place from the North Cape to Stewart Island ... <i>(Sep 10, 1919,Quick March,19</i>)

### from the North Cape to the Bluff Hill
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  From the North Cape to the Bluff Hill, in the extreme south of the Middle Island, the climate and soil are eminently adapted for the growth of a large variety of fruits. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,131</i>)

### from the Reinga to the Bluff
 
 <i>phr.</i> a variant of from Cape Reinga/North Cape to the Bluff

>  Mr Seddon was not only Premier of New Zealand; he was the friend of every man, woman, and child in it. His genial personality and kindliness of heart made him an always welcome guest in every corner of these islands from the Reinga to the Bluff. <i>(Jun 11, 1906,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  The apportionment of the revenue, and liabilities, above suggested, might be made by mutual consent between the Governor and the Assembly, and that once done for a fixed period, there seems no reason why the same representative institutions should not be maintained from the Reinga to the Bluff. <i>(Jun 12, 1862,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Harakeke or Korati [sic](Phormium tenax) is the justly celebrated New Zealand flax. It is plentiful everywhere, on bush-land and open-land, rich soil and poor soil, hill and dale, from the Reinga to the Bluff. a variant of North Cape to the Bluff q.v. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/204</i>)

>  Holding us in from the tip of Reinga/ To where the Bluff folds down a heavy paw. <i>(1985,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,140</i>) [NOTES: 19?? Eileen Duggan`Cloudy Bay' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 140]

### from the three Kings to the Snares
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [cf. from North Cape to the Bluff](fly-fuz.md#from-north-cape-to-the-bluff))

>  But he would venture to say that if they looked from the Three Kings to the Snares they would see a number of diverse interests which had sprung up under the provincial system - diverse but yet bearing close analogy - which could not have done so under any other system. <i>(Jul 16, 1870,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> If you searched the outback district, from the Three Kings to the Snares, I doubt if a worse built whare than that could have been found. <i>(1927,Follow the Call,112,1975 ed.</i>)

>  During the past five years we imported blankets, tweeds from Great Britain, and many thousand boxes of candles from Europe; yet from the Three Kings to the Snares everyone was wailing over the scarcity of money in the colony. <i>(Mar 21, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### front
 
 <i>n.</i> that place, area, border, etc., which is closer to the sea or other means of access. Compare BACK COUNTRY. (?farming) [1953 Dec R. Mason quotes Mrs Roberts of Bluff Station, Clarence Valley, Marlborough, using `I came in from the front; ... out to the front; ... on the front ...']



 
 <i>n.</i> administrative section of a prison [(prison slang)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> FRONT (n) Administrative section of a prison. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(axemen - woodchoppers)]

>  But, as most experienced axemen knew, Hewitt had turned too soon. Lamberton took out a good `front,' meaning he had cut through more than half-way. <i>(Oct 24, 1972,Press, The,3/6</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (?Canterbury) near(er) the coast: cf. front country [(?farming)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> This bush, though very beautiful to look at, is composed of nothing but the poorest black birch. We had no difficulty getting through it, for it had no under-growth, as the bushes on the front ranges have. <i>(Mar 24, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/57</i>)

>  On the first approach to the mountains they were met by great foothills ... In many cases along these front hills there were large areas of forest or `bush' ... <i>(1951,High Country,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> sell? [(drug slang)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> I began to sell to people I knew ..., and I soon began to find people to whom I could front my dope, according to a similar arangement under under which I had first entered the dealing circuit myself. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> of a farm, a block of land: that nearest the road, away from the back

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The owner of a run, whose centre was about twenty miles from our station ..., had fitted up a rough out-station at our front and his back boundary, and not more than three miles from our house. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,216</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> The Hunts successfully farmed the front of this block of land for over 20 years without the scratch of a pen to show they had any title to ownership of this block, ... <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,7</i>)

### front at
 
 <i>v.</i> [prison slang)]

>  The ice hockey gives me one, so I front at the nanny goat and see that it's paying a spot. to front at....to go to FRONT (AT) (prison slang) <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### front bum
 
 <i>n.</i> (coarse slang) the vagina

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>front bum</i> Vagina. A female informant was confronted one Queenstown morning with a cheery request to "show us ya front bum". NZA. <i>(1995,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

>  <b>PLOWMAN</b> front bum: vulgar term for vagina <i>(2002,Great Kiwi Slang</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (coarse slang) a woman (by extension from use for vagina)

>  ...he [John Tamihere] referred to women as "front-bums",lamenting their being promoted to New Zealand's top positions because of their gender. <i>(Apr 11, 2005,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### front country
 
 <i>n.</i> the country on the plains used for sheep-stations (as distinct from back country nearer the Southern Alps; ? the part of a station nearer the homestead [(mainly Canterbury)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Flax and cabbage trees belong more to the front country, they are not so characteristic of the back; the forests too upon the front side of the mountains are well worthy of a description but these things I will reserve for the present. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,54</i>)

>  Front country: See Country. <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>JONES</b> No gardeners were kept except on the front country. <i>(1933,Autobiography,72</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Black pine (matai) was used [for fencing] in the front country. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,88</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Mt. Somers station, a `front' country block of about 16,000 acres, lies between Barossa and Winterslow ... <i>(1952,High Country Journey,77</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> These [Canterbury] ranges form a definite barrier between the `front' and `back' country and the inhabitants of each see little of each other. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farmland consisting of foothills, downs and plains which front the east-side of the Southern Alps and high country land [usually relatively accessible to the station or a road.]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Black pine (matai) was used [for fencing] on the front country but it was not as good as broadleaf, because it rotted away in a few years. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,104</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> In the South Island that country beyond the river gorges is termed back country, as differing from front country which consists of those hills through whose gorges the same rivers wend their way. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,2</i>)

>  <b>Relph</b> To the east of the Main Divide is the 'front country', a series of ranges forming the boundary with the Canterbury Plains. <i>(2007,From Tussocks to Tourists,11</i>)

>  Some of the smaller flocks on front country have almost been wiped out ... <i>(Aug 15, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,5</i>)

>  In Otago also the front country has suffered severely, but some of the mountain stations in the interior must likewise have lost heavily. <i>(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,5</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The Waimate district has been a Border Leicester stronghold since the early days; these sheep seemed to suit the front country from the very first. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,112</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> In winter, ewes are put on the sunny front country facing the lake, where snow seldom lies. <i>(1971,Historic Sheep Stations SI II,34</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> "Front country" refers to the usually lower-altitude paddocks and blocks used during spring lambing and winter grazing , which are closer to the homestead ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,138</i>)

>  Also sold to dairy farmers were Ngatara Station, Mt Florida Station, and another big property on the front country, whose owners declined to allow it to be named. <i>(Jul 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

### front country run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pastoral property on the foothills and downs of high country.

>  <b>JONES</b> Most of the front country runs then taken were fully stocked. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,36</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The front-country runs south of the Rangitata were <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,133</i>)

### front hills
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the eastern foothills adjacent to South Island high country. (See [front country](fly-fuz.md#front-country))

>  <b>MARTIN</b> In 1854 a further ten large runs were established on the remainder of the plains between the Ashburton and Rakaia Rivers; this meant that the entire plains and 'front hills' were taken up. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,7</i>)

### front lawn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "We've fetished the front lawn," says Dann. "Where else could you call a group the Front Lawn and everyone knew what the joke was?" The front Lawn is now unfortunately concreted over, as it were, but in the garden the concept remains "quintessentially Kiwi; it speaks volumes about our culture"... In New Zealand the front lawn has always been an upwardly mowable status symbol.. but mostly.. front lawns are there to keep people away. <i>(Apr 10, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### front lead
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> Immediately north of the [Waikaka] township a rich layer in the fault-involved older quartz drifts, ... was worked from the surface ... This layer is locally known as the `back lead'. On or near it a shaft was sunk ... but was not utilised for driving-out. ... Somewhere about 1880 ... others drove an inclined drift from point to the southward of the back lead for a distance of 254 feet ... Then they drove horizontally for a short distance and intersected an auriferous ... layer of quartz wash, about 10 feet wide, known as the `front lead'. [fn quotes OED on drift as an excavated passage] <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,4</i>) [NOTES: 1914 P.G. Morgan report Geological Survey Branch 147 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 4]

### front paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> a paddock that is open to a road and view.

>  If you're going to have an animal in the front paddock, you might as well have a good-looking one. <i>(Oct 21, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Front paddock 80 acres/ Upper paddock 50 [do]/ Back paddock 10 [do]/ Middle paddock 20 [do] <i>(Aug 2, 1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 Aug 2 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 49]

>  <b>BELL</b> ... I will come over tomorrow and help to shift the ewes and lambs into the front paddock - they want a change now. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,149</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> He'd either be in the front paddock, or in the cowbail - or if the cows were all right, he'd be over at the Maize paddock fence. <i>(1978,Elver, The,45</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> As I drove up his drive, I noticed a very large mare in the front paddock. <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,20</i>)

### front porch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a landing or stage at the front of a dairy factory, on to which produce was loaded and unloaded.

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> The factory stage was the concrete platform, or 'front porch' of the building, only the front being open, with the floor at roughly the same height as most of the vehicles that brought milk from the farms. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,41</i>)

### front range
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the side of the South Island mountain range facing east.

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ... in a few years there was not an acre between the sea and the front ranges which was not taken up. <i>(1872,Erewhon,10</i>)

### front run
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ...to these five were added two shepherds and a stockman from one station ... come over from front runs to attend a general muster of our sheep and cattle. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,143</i>)

### front side
 
 <i>n.</i> in respect of the Southern Alps, the eastern or Canterbury side

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Flax and cabbage trees belong more to the front country, they are not so characteristic of the back; the forests too upon the front side of the mountains are well worthy of a description but these things I will reserve for the present. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,54,1960 ed.</i>)

### front station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a homestead block or main settlement on a station that is most accessible from a road.

>  <b>STACEY</b> We had shifted all our belongings to the front station and were enjoying the delights of civilisation at Hanmer Springs. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,119</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> With the horses we were able to go into the front station most weekends. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,104</i>)

### front up
 
 <i>v.</i> to appear, confront [(?prison slang)]

>  <b>MACKENZIE</b> front up meet or appear before the parole board, superintendent, etc. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>) [NOTES: (Lag's Lexicon)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> FRONT UP (v) e.g., - up. Appear, confront. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to come up with the money, the cash in a business operation, etc. [1983 Jack Columbus (Blenheim) They have the money in the bank, and could easily front up for the section.]

### front-end load
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [fig. use] to push before one, from the transporting of material on the front-end loader of a tractor.

>  Mrs King's inability to do so is a consequence of her Government's decision to "front-end load" its extra spending during the present three-year term. <i>(Apr 30, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

### front-end loader
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tractor or other vehicle with a shovelling or loading implement at the front.

>  Much of his silage is conserved single-handed, using a front-end loader and building stacks. <i>(Apr 2, 1956,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

### frontage
 
 <i>n.</i> [(station)]

>  Frontage: `Land along a river or creek, of great importance to a station. A common in Australia, not peculiar to it.' So [1898 Morris Austral-English], but a still more common use in Canterbury is road frontage. <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  frontage n. land on a river or creek. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

### frost
 
 <i>n.</i> frostfish, <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> FROSTFISH (a) Scientific name: <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Frostfish. (c) Other common names: Frosts or Frosties. (d) Principal localities where taken: Cook Strait and East Coast as far as Napier. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(school)]

>  what a frost: a disappointment <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### frost flat
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an extensive area of flat ground, subject to severe frost because of its situation. [ca1900 at least. Specific areas of frost flat are the Rangitaiki, Pureora, and Whirinaki areas of the Central North Island, and parts of Tasman District.]

>  The persistence of frost flat communities at Rangitaiki is being jeopardised by invasive weeds ... <i>(Jan 1990,NZ Journal of Botany,28/225</i>)

>  Threatened plant programmes include wetland, frost flat, forest and coastal species across the area. <i>(Jul 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.jobs.govt.nz/jobs/viewjob.aspx?OpportunityID=22636]

>  <b>Poole & Adams</b> ..<i> Monoao. Shrub reaching 2m .. Branchlets long, slender.</i> Shrub land; especially on so-called 'frost flats' of the volcanic plateau. <i>(1963,Trees and Shrubs,160</i>)

>  Like many unique and special places, frost flats are now firmly on the conservation radar screen. <i>(May 2007,NZ Geographic,44,85 ed.</i>)

### frost lift
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) needle ice, that occurs when damp or wet bare soil or sub-soil is exposed to alternate warming and cooling.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> On depleted tussock pastures of the MacKenzie country, frost lift followed by wind erosion became a major Source of soil loss. <i>(1973,Hold This Land,70</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> Following on frost lift, blowing by the nor'wester has been responsible for the complete removal of shallow soils down to the clay parent material. <i>(1973,Hold This Land,45</i>)

>  Frost lift pulls the seedlings right out of the ground where they subsequently die of dessication. <i>(May 13, 1982,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

### frost lifting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the forming of needle ice on exposed high country areas.

>  As a result of burning, tussocks are badly weakened on my country owing to removal of natural humus and the exposure of the surrounding soil to frost-lifting and subsequent erosion. <i>(May 3, 1945,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

### Frostbite
 
 <i>n.</i> class of racing yacht

>  <b>CARTER</b> These two classes, the Akatere and Frostbite, both owe their existence to the efforts of the designer, Mr. J. Brooke, of Devonport, Auckland. <i>Ibid.</i>131 Frostbite class dinghies have been raced in all parts of the North Island, having been transported by car and sailed in Lakes Rotorua, Rotoiti, Okataina, Taupo, Hamilton and Waikaremoana. <i>(1948,Little Ships: NZ's Yachting Fleet,129</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  <b>DYSON</b> The Frostbite is a clinker-built sailing dinghy with a single sail designed in 1938 by Mr. J. B. rooke. <i>(1966,Yachting the NZ Way,155</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> Because of the multi-purpose use of the Frostbite, the class has survived to the present day... The Club has now introduced a fibreglass Frostbite, the mould being taken off the wooden hull. <i>(1978,Little Ships of NZ,61</i>)

>  The Frostbite. In 1938 the Wakatere Boating Club decided to design and build a class similar to the American Frostbite Dinghy, a class in which very "keen" American yachties raced in the winter. <i>(1989,Kohimarama,115</i>)

### frostfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A fine specimen of the frost fish was picked up.. on the North Beach on Friday. <i>(Jun 15, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  Frost-fish have not been plentiful on the Otaki beach this season. <i>(Jun 30, 1944,Levin Daily Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Along this same stretch of coast John the Baptist, a part-Indian fish-hawker, would gather frostfish. <i>(Nov 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 54/73</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The Frost Fish, or Hiku of the Maoris (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>), is esteemed the most delicious fish in New Zealand. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,109</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [The frost-fish] is only known from from being in frosty weather thrown up by the ocean ... From this circumstance it is known amongst the colonists as the frost-fish. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,244</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Hiku, Frostfish .. [Scientific Name] <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> <i>Ibid.</i>486 Frostfish only find their way to market in winter time, and are very irregular in supply. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  ON LEPIDOPUS CAUDATUS, GUNTH (FROST-FISH). By Dr. R. v. Lendenfeld. <i>(Jan 1884,NZ Journal of Science,108</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> FROST FISH (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>). ... [30] The frost-fish, or hiku of the Maoris, is esteemed the most delicious fish in New Zealand. It is most commonly found cast up after cold frosty nights on sandy beaches that are exposed to the long roll of ocean swell, and is not in this country obtained by any kind of fishing. <i>Ibid.</i> 300 <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> Euphrasen Frost-fish, Hiku, or Para. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,29</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> Frost fish, `hiku' of the Maories (Lepidopus Candatus) is esteemed the most delicious fish in New Zealand: these are sometimes found, but only on frosty mornings. <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,177</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> The greater part of the spoil was butter-fish, ... and it was certainly the first fish in the Colony ... worth eating, for, excepting frost-fish, [most have little flavour]. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,148</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Jan 31 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 148]

>  Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish; while only the ling, hake and haddock ... are among those of more southern types ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <i>7. Portland Island</i>. - Tarakihi, moki, frost-fish, barracouta, king-fish, john-dory, warehou, maomao, parrot-fish, flying-fish, smooth-hound, soldier fish... <i>Ibid.</i> 10. Kaipara Heads. - Frost-fish. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Para, n. lepidopus caudatus; frost-fish. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,127</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ name given in Australia and New Zealand to the European <i>Scabbard-fish, Lepidopus caudatus</i>, White. The name is said to be derived from the circumstance that the fish is found alive on New Zealand sea-beaches on frosty nights. It is called the <i>Scabbard-fish</i> in Europe, because it is like the shining white metal sheath of a long sword. <i>Lepidopus</i> belongs to the family <i>Trichiurida</i>; it reaches a length of five or six feet, but is so thin that it hardly weighs as many pounds. It is considered a delicacy in New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English,155</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ` The frost-fish ... the most delicately flavoured of all New Zealand fishes, is an inhabitant of deep water, and on frosty sights, owing probably to its air-bladders becoming choked, it is cast up be the surf on the ocean-beach.' And in New Zealand to <i>Carpophaga nova-zealandia, </i> Gmel. (Maori name, <i>Kereru Kuku</i>, or <i>Kukupa.</i>) <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,ii./ 51</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 The frost fish, which has a long, silvery body, is usually picked up on the sea beach in the winter weather;... <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  Black cardinalfish, frostfish, ghost shark, ribaldo, rubyfish, sea perch, trumpeter, white warehou and yellow-eyed mullet have now been included... Other reductions to catch limits are to specific stocks of grey mullet, barracouta and oreo dory. <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Frost-fish</i>: The allusion is to the popular idea that the Frost-fish (which is rarely caught at sea, but sometimes is found lying upon the beach) comes ashore to die. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,186</i>)

>  <i>Lepidopus Caudatus </i>... Frost fish. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/238</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  LEPIDOPUS CAUDATUS (Euphrasen). <i>Frost-fish; Para</i>[a1-macron]. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (rarer south of Bay of Plenty). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>SPEIGHT et al.</b> The only true mackerels recorded from Canterbury are the Pacific Bonito (<i>Sarda chiliensis</i>) and the large-scaled Tunny (<i>Gasterochisma melampus</i>) which may reach a length of 6 feet; but a related family includes the well-known Barracouta (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), the King Barracouta, <i> Jordanidia solandrii</i> (often known locally as Kingfish, but not to be confused with the North Island Kingfish which is a form or Yellowtail) and the elongated, silvery-hued frost fish (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>) whose curious stranding habits can hardly yet be said to be satisfactorily explained. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 199]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> (Euphrasen). FROST-FISH; PARA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,47,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> 1. Para (Frost-fish) 2. Manga [a-macron] (barracouta) 3. Ngoiro (conger-eel) 4. Tuna (fresh-water-eel) <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,48</i>)

>  ... the Frost Fish (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>),... <i>(1929,TrNZI,60/139</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  39. <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> Euphrasen. Frost Fish. Maori: Hiku, Para. <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/147</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Frost-fish</i>. ... This is the para of most New Zealand Natives, and the tikati of Taranaki. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/232</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> (Euphrasen). Frostfish. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/414</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Paara or Para - the frostfish. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> FROST-FISH (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>), Para of the Maoris, is like a long narrow ribbon of burnished silver, from 3 to 5 feet in length. They derive their name from the fact that numbers of these fish come ashore around the coast, usually on calm, frosty nights. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,71</i>)

>  frost fish: edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Frostfish <i>Lepidopus lex</i> Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,410</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> FROSTFISH (PARA) <i>Lepidopus lex</i> Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,306,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> FROSTFISH (PARA) <i>Lepidopus lex</i> Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,306,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> FROSTFISH ... <i>Other names: Lepidopus caudatus</i>; para, hiku, taharangi (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,277</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Frostfish - hikau, para, taharangi, <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  ~ n. long, very thin edible Australian and New Zealand fish, the European scabbard fish. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> FROSTFISH [<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>] ... A number of old Maori names are known but are not in use today. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,46</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family TRICHIURIDAE. The frost fishes (<i>Lepidopus </i>...) are unrelated to the ribbonfish ... which they superficially resemble. They have no scales. The `silver' which rubs off like aluminium paint is the substance guanin, which gives many fishes their silvery sheen. Nobody can explain <i>Lepidopus</'>s habit of putting itself ashore on cold spring and winter evenings. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,225</i>)

>  <i>frost fish n.</i> long, very thin edible Australian and N.Z. fish, the European scabbard fish. Maori name <i>Para. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> FROSTFISH (a) Scientific name: <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Frostfish. (c) Other common names: Frosts or Frosties. (d) Principal localities where taken: Cook Strait and East Coast as far as Napier. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,25</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FROSTFISH <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>: The Maori name Para means `to spring' or `to leap' and refers to the last moments of the average specimen when it seems tp leave its own element to spring to its death. <i>Lepidopus</i> means `small scales' and <i>caudatus</i> refers to the small tail. It is sometimes called the `Suicide-fish.' <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,66</i>)

>  <b>AYLONG</b> FROSTFISH <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> ... Frostfish have a scaleless body and are a uniform bright silver colour. They spend the cold winter months in deep offshore water beyond the edge of the continental shelf and apparently come to the surface on clear still nights when the moon is full; although it maybe that they are on the surface at other times but are not seen because of the dull rough conditions. ... They are occasionally found stranded on beaches. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,289,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] frost fish ..[scientific name] <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> .. [other name(s)] cutlassfish, scabbardfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Frostfish <i>Lepidopus caudatus</i> ... A widespread species of temperate oceanic waters, generally simply called scabbard-fish, but known as frostfish in Australia and New Zealand (from the skin's colour which comes off when touched). <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,123</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] frostfish 2037-45 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  A much longer and more delicate fish is the hiku of the Maori, or frost fish. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  The landing beach.. seems to be a nursery for kingfish and frost-fish. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  I suppose you have seen plenty of frostfish... They usually come ashore at high tide on a frosty morning. <i>(Dec 7, 1920,Otago Witness,57</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### frostie
 
 <i>n.</i> a frostfish

### frostpot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small oil burner used to ''fight'' frost in orchards, particularly in Central Otago.

>  .. overhead sprinklers offer the cheapest method of frost control in frosty situations. On the other hand, frostpots are the more economical where frosts are few and light. In situations with light frosts, trickle irrigation combined with frostpots is usually the best alternative. <i>(Dec 1976,NZ Journal of Agriculture,38</i>)

### frosty
 
 <i>n.</i> an elderly person (white-/frosty-haired)

>  TOTALLY Wellington is developing strategies to attract frosties to the cultural capital. The 50-years-plus market is a key growth area, providing people have enough retirement savings to blow on entertainment. <i>(Nov 14, 2001,Capital Times,3</i>)

### frot one's bacons
 
 <i>phr.</i> rub one's hands

>  Time and again I have heard them simpering cautiously on the NBS - gutless, boring and insincere, frotting their bacons in pseudo-controversy. <i>(Apr 1952,Here & Now,16</i>)

### frothy bubbly
 
 <i>n.</i> beer

>  frothy bubbly: beer <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### froudacious
 
 <i>adj.</i> inaccurate

>  <b>McGILL</b> froudacious inaccurate; from historian Froude's comments on Australia and New Zealand, c.1880, coined from `Froude' and `audacious'; ref. Farmer and Henley's Slang and its Analogues. Obs. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)

 <i>n.</i> inaccuracy (See [froudacious](fly-fuz.md#froudacious))

### frown affairs
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) alteration of 'foreign affairs'

>  Mr Peters, who was dubbed "frown affairs" minister after his refusal to smile for media cameras during the ministerial swearing in ceremony last month, was all smiles before the media and joked that Mr McCormick should do the same. <i>(Nov 11, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### frozen
 
 <i>adj.</i> having nothing happening

 <i>adj.</i> [(goldminers')]

>  White crystalline quartz - this contains a considerable amount of gold in a very fine state of division, and is deposited directly on the wall-rock without any intervening selvage or parting - `frozen on', to use the expressive phrase of the miner. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.4 (NS) 123</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  Below the igneous rock and `frozen' to it is 3 in. of stonycoke, passing downward with apparently unaltered coal. <i>(1920,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,3/174</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan)]

 <i>n.</i> McDonald's menu item

>  One of McDonald's most requested burgers, the Kiwiburger, is joined in the Kiwiana-inspired menu by the new Kiwi Pav, Frozen L&P and the Kiwi Brekkie McMuffin. <i>(Aug 24, 2011,Wairarapa News,23</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> the Antarctic

 <i>abbr.</i> Fellow of the Royal Society of New Zealand.

### Fru Ju
 
 <i>n.</i> a fruit-flavoured ice-block

### fruit cheese/paste
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  All fruit cheeses and pastes are made in the wame way. Fleshy fruits, usually those with pips or stones, are cooked with their skins, pips and stones and a small amount of liquid in a pan in a low oven. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

### fruit loop
 
 <i>n.</i> an eccentric or crazy person

>  His funeral gave us a gospel choir, a lot of thin weeping women and a sanctimonious lecture on eternal love from a narcissistic fruit loop who couldn't maintain a relationship with anything other than a mirror for five minutes and who has sex with strangers at the carwash. <i>(Nov 4, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>Philippa Swan</b> 'Becky, don't you ever worry about the sort of people attracted to a life coach?' 'Fruitloops?' <i>(2006,Six Pack, The,52</i>)

>  ...as a reporter who's sat through lots of council meetings, it's safe to say that, on some committees of some councils, I've never seen as many fruitloops in one room as I have since visiting my grandmother in the psycho-geriatric ward of a mental hospital. <i>(Sep 20, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Many readers will presume [she] is a total fruit loop; others might wonder if she was hit by lightning during the storm. <i>(Mar 10, 1995,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  I'm told that someone who would be seen in politically correct circles as perspicacity-challenged is immediately identifiable by his peers if he is described as a fruitloop. The last time I rubbed shoulders with fruitloops they were repulsively sticky little things, in bright colours. When poured on to a plate they became a cereal breakfast fit to brighten the morning of any jaded five- year-old. <i>(Mar 11, 1995,Dominion,16</i>)

### fuchsia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i> native, New Zealand fuchsia =kotukutuku, kohutuhutu (incorrectly konini) [tree fuchsia 1882 POO, 1917 DDD. (A) New Zealand Fuchsia - 3 entries (mainly scientific) -->1905 MNZ, 1915 CKB -->M 1892] (See [tree fuchsia](tot-tyr.md#tree-fuchsia))

>  <b>MAHY</b> They left the track and scrambled down into a shadowy gully under native fuschia (sic) and beech trees. <i>(1985,Catalogue of the Universe,42</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> The fuschia berry, or, as it is called by the natives, konini, is also highly prized... The New Zealand fuschia grows to the height of 20 feet; and sometimes more. <i>(1849,Notes on NZ,31</i>)

>  The fushsia [sic] commemorates Leonard Fuchs, a sixteenth century botanist and it is well to remember this for it will help in getting the spelling right. It is all too easy to put the "s" in the wrong place. <i>(Jan 10, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>) [NOTES: Original in <i>Taranaki Herald</i> 19/11/1986.]

>  Ground covered with ... myrtles and Fuccer [sic] trees 40 foot hie [sic]. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,11/44/238</i>) [NOTES: (Henry Woulder)]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The hills are steep, covered with leptospermum, tall fuchsias with purple flowers, Edwardsias, and other shrubs. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/122 There are two fuchsias here: one, the <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>, forms a moderate-sized tree, and is very common; the other, however, the <i>Fuchsia procumbens</i>, is very rare, and at present is met with only in Wangaroa Bay, to the northward of the Bay of Islands. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/121</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The <i>fuchsia</i> is indigenous to the forest of New Zealand as well as to those of Chili and Patagonia, ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/54</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Tho' this is midwinter the only plant which I observed casting its leaves was the Fuchsia. <i>(Jun 16, 1853,Traveller under Concern,117,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i> New Philos. Journ.(NS) (And) <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal</i>) [NOTES: Tancred Notes on Natural History Canterbury]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Fuchsia cannot rival the ratas in beauty, it nevertheless gives a pleasing variety to the forest. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,146</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The fuchsia is indigenous in New Zealand, the woods in many places being adorned with an undergrowth of this elegant shrub. <i>(1866,Polynesia,51</i>)

>  Fuchsia, or Kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>S.MATHEW</b> . - then the `Rata' with its small red flowers, and the Fuchsias with varied flowers, red or white or purple - <i>(1873,Autobiography</i>) [NOTES: 1873 S.MATHEW Autobiography in 1940 Founding of NZ 206.]

>  [Native name] Kohutuhutu .. [Settlers' name] Fuchsia ..[Family] Onagraceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>KIRK</b> (And) <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  Kohutuhutu (fuchsia) .. <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i> .. Timber very strong, durable, and hard; used for house blocks and fencing posts; is almost indestructible by fire; grain prettily marked <i>(1890,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,11</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Two or three hundred yards beyond begins an irregular fringe of native bush, broadleaf, veronica, fuchsia, pittosporum, and tangled supplejack; beyond these rise the lofty pines and other forest giants. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  Report on the Mokau River <i>(1909,AJHR,C-6, Appendix D</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <b>McLEAN</b> (And) <i>(1911,Emu, The,xi (1911-1912)</i>)

>  (And) (A) Native Fuchsia - 6 entries (mainly scientific) <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  ~ A plant with handsome drooping flowers developed from the native fuchsia of New Zealand. [14 edn: native to New Zealand, South America and Mexico.] <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),174,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> During the warm months there was a long season for koninis from the kotukutuku trees (native Fuchsia) then ... the ramarama berries (Myrtaceae) were ripe. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,16</i>)

>  Amongst them were several varieties of fine fuchsias. <i>(Mar 29, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  Fuchsias were once called "ladies' eardrops". They symbolised "elegance and taste" in flower language. <i>(Jan 12, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  The scrambling fuchsia, one of the few native plants of New Zealand that are deciduous, seems to demand a great deal of moisture. <i>(Sep 28, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  Some big fuschias, by a looped bend in the river, embower a gurgling creek. <i>(Jan 29, 1924,Otago Witness,69</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Fuchsia excorticata </i> <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>HUDSON</b> (And) <i>Fuchsia excorticata </i> <i>(1898,NZ Moths & Butterflies</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Fuchsia, Native</i>, n. <i>The name is applied to several native plants.</i> ... (3) In New Zealand, to <i>Fuchsia excorticata </i>, Linn., N.O. <i>Onagrariae</i>. (Maori name, <i>Kotukutuku</i>, (q.v.) See also <i>Tooky-took</i> and <i>Konini. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,156</i>)

>  The native fuchsia [fn <i>Fuchsia excorticata.</i>] - an exile from South America - and the fierce bush-nettle [fn <i>Urtica ferox</i>.] are conspicuous amongst the undergrowth, together with the bush-lily [fn <i>Astelia nervosa</i>.], but we see but few species of the characteristic New Zealand daisy-trees [fn Species of <i>Olearia</i>.] or of the bushy Veronicas (koromiko), which are so conspicuous in other parts of these islands. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F[uchsia] excorticata, Linn. ... Native fuchsia; Kotukutuku</i>; the fruit <i>Konini</i>. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,186</i>)

### fuchsia berry
 
 <i>n.</i> konini

>  <b>LUSH</b> After dinner we all went into a small wood near our house to gather fuchsia berries ... The lads quickly dispensed themselves over the wood and climbed up the fuchsia trees, many of which were exceedingly lofty. <i>(Nov 11, 1850,Auckland Journals,36,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> The fuchsia berry, or as it is called by the natives, konini, is also highly prized. ... The New Zealand fuchsia grows to the height of 20 feet ... <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 130]

>  <b>AYSON</b> I remember the fuchsia berries were ripe and we had a good feed [in 1853]. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,22</i>)

>  There were ... wild pigs in the surrounding bush; fuchsia berries could be gathered for tarts ... For tea, a concoction of bidi-bidi was used, and I have heard it described by one of these early pioneer women as horrible stuff too! <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,30,1988 ed.</i>)

### fudging
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### full
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Mrs Dougal [a Dunedin Presbyterian] ... gave her lawful spouse a particularly bad time if and when he returned home anyway fou' ... <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,81,1935 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> construed with blast, bore, lick, out, split, swing, to form phrasal adverbs with the sense `at full speed'; `energetically'; `flat out', of speed, activity of any kind (cf OED FULL 9c) [Constr. omits prepos at or in: full speed can also be used adverbially this way. All have been in common coll use since at least the 1930s. (cf EDD drive 13. full drive w Yks, Nhp `he went along, full drive, to use a common express for walking or running' 11. full used as an intensive in phr -bang, -bet, -split [but not -nit])]

### full (of)
 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> fed up (with)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> Although, there were plenty of station horses, ... I invested in a couple for my own private use. ... [One] bucked me off more times than I can remember, and I began to get `full' of him. George, my mate, proposed Rarey-fying him. The process consisted in breathing up his nose ... One of Rarey's theories was that the breath of man inhaled into the horse's nostrils intimidated him. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,38</i>)

### full as a boot
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1920; also full as a bull etc. (see McGill below)]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A state of intoxication. .in 4;`Hardie was as full as a boot! We put him on the stretcher and threw some blankets over him. The first time Hard's been well truly drunk since he got married.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],367</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> `Gitting himself full as a boot so he couldn't see the road to the dunny if it had red flags on it,' she'd say. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,7</i>)

>  full as a boot/bull/Catholic school/goog (egg) very drunk. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,47</i>)

### full as a bull
 
 <i>phr.</i> roaring drunk

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... by the time the dance was under way I was as `full as a bull' and ripe for anything. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,103</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I spose ya'll get full as a bull at this reunion? <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,44</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> The old man had come home full as a bull one night ... all his money gone. The old woman had gone butcher's hook and they'd got stuck into one another. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,275</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> But I noticed he was full as a bull in no time. <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,11</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Everybody as full as a bull on the bus back from the game and they stopped for a leak ... and Fong Needham fell down the bank ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,174</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> As full as a bull they both got;/ And the owner's big loss was forgot. <i>(1974,Ballads of a Racegoer,21</i>)

>  full as a bull: drunk <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>HARVEY</b> They were nice and frothy [milkshakes] ... and it took a heck of a long time to suck that thick stuff off the top with a straw. When Nuts finished his he ... said, `Jesus. I'm full as a bull.' <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,47</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> full as a boot/bull/Catholic school/goog (egg) very drunk. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,47</i>)

### full as a butcher's dog
 
 <i>phr.</i> very full

>  I see the mother and father slug as full as a butcher's dog [with my plants and slug-killer]. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,2</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> see full as a boot (See [see also goog](../S#see-also-goog))

 <i>phr.</i>

### full as a tick
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... full as a tick. <i>(1949,Roads from Home,74</i>)

>  <b>COWLEY</b> `I'll shout you a Coke.' `No thanks,' she said. `I'm full as a tick.' <i>(1971,Man of Straw,108</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The old swagger was properly stunned when we left him; he was full as a tick with wine, ... <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,145</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> [[?petrol or is this fig, meaning hard to fly, like a drunk, woozy]]

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Young Bradshaw's the boy who is flying this plane,/ She is as full as a tick and it's pouring with rain./ Things are not going good - his donk's on the blink* -/ Then he puts up a blue and she dives in the drink./ [fn] *his donk's on the blink: his engine is giving trouble (airman's slang) [Charles had served in the RNZAF in WW2] <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,89</i>)

### full as the family po
 
 <i>phr.</i> elaboration of full as a po (P8: po 2. earlier C. 20) very drunk.

 <i>phr.</i>

### full board
 
 <i>n.</i> [(shearing)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> At one side [of the shearing shed], down the whole length of the building, the shearers work, thirteen of whom are called a `full board.' <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  full board n. fully staffed shearing shed. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a full number of shearers occupying every available stand in a woolshed.

>  <b>WILSON</b> At one side, down the whole length of the building, the shearers work, thirteen of whom are called a 'full board.' <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> When there is a full number of shearers required, a shed is said to have a 'full-board.' [full-board] <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

### full book
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Customers would probably be told that it was "a full book." <i>(Dec 4, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/180 : 6</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a removal of wool from the rear end, bellies and head of sheep.

 <i>n.</i>
