### full lick
 
 <i>phr.</i> full speed

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Clive ran .... full lick into the sea. <i>(1984,Cunninghams, The,249</i>)

### full mouth
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep's complete set of (eight) teeth

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Each year it [sc. a sheep] cuts two more until it has eight teeth, which is a 'full' mouth. <i>(1975,Mary-Lou,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or cattle beast with eight erupted incisor teeth.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> "Full-mouth" is, however, an elastic term. <i>(1921,Tutira,158</i>)

>  For sale 1500 Merino wethers, from 4-tooth to full mouth. <i>(Dec 3, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The usual Ngamatea policy is to keep all wether lambs and carry them until they are full-mouths, but this year for the first time they sent 2,000 wether lambs away fat off their mothers. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,153</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Full mouth: Five-year-old sheep, or cattle beast, for that matter, which has now erupted eight incisor teeth. [full mouth] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,33</i>)

### full of run
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [stock] frisky and fast-moving when mustered.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The ewes, with the characteristic of the Merino and in fine fettle after three weeks or so in the paddocks were full of run and for the first few miles Lofty had to keep the lead in constant check. <i>(1949,High Country,108</i>)

### full of sting
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [stock] very active, especially sheep after shearing.

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Taking the shorn mobs back from the shed was also a problem as they were full of sting. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,67</i>)

### full on
 
 <i>adj.</i> fed up (with); weary of (cf full (up) of)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `This is no go, and we must give it best! ... I think it's time to cry `Smoke oh' and then we'll go up, for I'm full on this shooting racket.' `You are adept at slang, Arthur, and no mistake', ... `And yet a fellow told me the other day, he could tell at once that I had not been long in the country, or, as he phrased it, `that I had not got the lime-juice out of me yet;' so it can't be colonial slang that I speak.' Well, I don't know that colonials are worse than other people for speaking slang, but they have, of course, many words and phrases peculiar to themselves.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,151</i>)

### full out
 
 <i>phr.</i> flat out (constr. of full speed and flat out (ca1928 now SE PJ) confused, cf. also, to go all out)

>  <b>DRESCHFELT</b> When he's shickered he drives full out the whole time, just lets her rip. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,130</i>)

### full rip
 
 <i>phr.</i> full speed

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As they drew near the hotel, Doc suddenly [53] dug his heels in, and despite The Bar's efforts to pull up as per custom, galloped right on full rip past the hotel. <i>Ibid.</i> 62 Before he could gain control, the team, still going full rip, made straight for the open door of the stables. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,52</i>)

### full split
 
 <i>phr.</i> at top, or high, speed

>  One man going full split after a bullock. <i>(Jul 4, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,47,1950 ed.</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> (informal) both arms akimbo to express disapproval

### full tear
 
 <i>phr.</i> full speed

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `On looking up,' said Jack, `to see what was making the row, we saw a coach coming full tear down. The driver was cracking his whip and roaring at the horses. ...' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,65</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As they were trying to force the crossing one bellowing old cow broke away, by passed the stockmen, and headed back full tear for the ranges. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,111</i>)

### full tit
 
 <i>phr.</i> top speed, flat out, full bore [ca1935 Havelock]

>  "...trees in a mile or so end of the straight better chance at the trees full tit along here no brakes some of them and no fat people inside sweatin' kilos on their seats. Car." <i>(Nov 1998,Landfall</i>)

### full up
 
 <i>adj.</i> tired (of), fed up (with)

>  <b>REEVES</b> I note the men have to do all the fielding and get tired, or as the colonial slang goes, `full up', soonest. <i>(1892,Homeward Bound,33</i>)

>  full a.-full up' a. sl. of. tired of. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Full up 2. (Constructed with of) sated; weary; disgusted: Australian and, later, New Zealand, from ca. 1890. Rolf Boldrewood in The Miner's Right. Variants full (if followed by of), full on (with object). Cf. fed up (with), q.v., the English counterpart. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,307</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> full up exasperated, weary, disgusted; eg `I'm full up with this shower, Netty. Let's split.' NZA. b. full up of <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,47</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Full up of, adj. (slang), sick and tired of. `Full on,' and `full of' are other forms.' [1890] <i>(1898,Austral English,156</i>)

### full-mouth pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen of sheep for sale at around the age of four to five years.

### full-mouthed
 
 <i>adj.</i> of sheep see Acland

>  <b>PEACHE</b> .. only about 2,500 being full-mouthed ... <i>(May 30, 1879,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1879 May 30 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 58 .]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ages of sheep. Although there are many variations, the following names are usually used: A sheep is a lamb until weaned. After weaning a lamb is called a hogget until shorn. A male lamb is a ram lamb until castrated, then a wether. A female lamb is a ewe lamb. After shearing, a ewe or ram lamb is usually called a shorn hogget until cutting the first two teeth. It is then called a two-tooth. Some use the term two-tooth as soon as the lamb is shorn. Next year the sheep is a four-tooth, then a six-tooth and then an eight-tooth or full-mouthed sheep. It is full-mouthed as long as the teeth remain perfect but then becomes broken-mouthed and gummy. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but earliest in NZ] [of sheep] of four to five years of age.

>  For sale 3000 Merino ewes, 6 tooth and full-mouthed. <i>(Dec 3, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  After four years a sheep is full-mouthed, failing or gummy. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,17</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> .. as yet he could not tell a hogget from a full-mouthed sheep, even by looking at their mouths ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,69</i>)

>  .. full-mouthed ewes 13s 10d ... <i>(Feb 3, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,7</i>)

>  This [rise in value] was more noticeable in values in the full-mouthed ewes. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,348</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> By its fifth year an adult sheep has erupted eight incisor teeth and is termed full-mouthed. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,11</i>)

>  Full-mouth woolly ewes $34 to $35. [full-mouth] <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

### full-tongued
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a huntaway dog] well-noised. (See [well-noised](../W#well-noised))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Here and there a dog .. would shoot out and slew shep down a clear spur; full-tongued it would rattle them helter-skelter down to the man below. <i>(1949,High Country Days,160</i>)

### full-wool
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] carrying twelve months growth of fleecewool. [(1984 cit. adverbial)] (See [in the wool](../I#in-the-wool))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> These were full-woolled sheep under ordinary station conditions. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,29</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> There were always little bits of wet ground and boggy places where there was a bit of good tucker and the heavy, fullwoolled sheep would get stuck in these places ... <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,36</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> We built it big enough to hold 150 full woolled sheep. <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,58</i>)

>  .. two extra heavy full-woolled lambs from K and R McKay, Onewhero, South Auckland , topped the sale at $80. <i>(Aug 22, 1996,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i> variations and varieties of fellow [1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 11 FULLA]

>  [corruption of fellow] fulla <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  [Maori girl speaks] `Howzat you fullas?' but the girls were already conferring and Charlotte was enraged. ... she was going to throw that Macky in the tutae for sure. <i>(1985,Women's Work,223</i>) [NOTES: (Grace Kepa) [Maori English]]

>  This sort of banter goes on all the time in shearing gangs; keeps things interesting, they reckon, and probably helps the young fullas from chucking it all in when they're learning to shear, ... <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,30</i>)

### Fuller's Earth
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand [(obs theatrical)]

>  Other miscellaneous historical (obsolete) names from the early 20th century include..<i>Fuller's Earth</i>, a predictable pun alluding to the prominence of Benjamin and John Fuller in the local theatre and cinema business. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fuller's earth. New Zealand: theatrical and cinematic: from ca. 1912. Punning on the Fuller brothers, who, ca. 1910-30, owned a great number of N.Z. theatres and cinemas. (The Daily Telegraph, July 23, 1934.) <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,307</i>)

>  ... `Captain Cooker' is well known, better known than `Fuller's Earth' as a synonym for New Zealand (from the theatrical family). <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> New Zealand was no longer merely a colony ... To theatricals it became Fuller's Earth, commemorating the noted Fuller brothers. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,49</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Fuller's Earth New Zealand; Fuller Brothers theatres and cinemas spanned the land 1910-30. - a Fuller's any vaudeville show. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,47</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw [in the Indian Ocean] <i>Procellaria glacialis</i> or Fulmar & the next day the <i>Procellaria aequinoctialis</i>, which is [189] very improperly thus called, as it is never seen near the Equinoxial line ... They are all Petrels ... [ed. fn] Respectively the Antarctic Fulmar and White-chinned Petrel (or Cape Hen). <i>(Dec 3, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/188</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Albatrosses, pintadas [<i>procellaria capensis</i>], and petrels of all kinds, amongst which was also the fulmar, (<i>procellaria glacialis</i>,) were now daily observed [in the South Atlantic]. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/52</i>)

### Fulton's blind half hundred
 
 <i>phr.</i>

### fumitory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FUMITORY (<i>Fumaria officinalis</i> and<i>F. muralis</i>) occurs in cultivated fields, although not very abundantly except in Wellington. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,86,1967 ed.</i>)

>  FUMITORY (<i>Fumaria officinalis</i>) ... native to Europe and Russia, is an important crop weed through the cooler temperate zones, and in New Zealand common in some districts, mainly between Christchurch and Oamaru.... SCRAMBLING FUMITORY (<i>Fumaria muralis</i>) ... native to Eurasia, is seldom listed as an important weed abroad, but is common in cultivated ground in New Zealand,. . . <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,47</i>)

>  <i>scrambling fumitory [s] Fumaria muralis</i> Sond. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,68</i>)

### Fun Ho
 
 <i>n.</i> A brand of metal toys, like Dinky Toys, but of initially cruder construction, made originally of lead and then aluminium in Wellington by Harold Underwood in the 1930. In 1945 production moved to New Plymouth and in 1949 to Inglewood where they were made until 1982.

>  A rugged brightly painted Fun Ho! toy in Santa's stocking on Christmas morning was a surprise bringing approving smiles to the faces of Kiwi kids for generations.... Harold (Jack) Underwood made lead slush tops and semiflat figures in his basement as a hobby, unaware that those experiments would become the success that enabled him to resign from the public service and enter the toy-making business... There is an absolutely positively Wellington origin about the distinctive Fun Ho! trademark, too. The design with exclamation nmark was by the late E Mervyn Taylor, a New Zealand arrist who worked in the city at the same time as an illustrator and commercial artist... The earliest Fun Ho! models were made from lead and appear to have been produced in Wellington. <i>(Dec 31, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  <b>CONEY</b> We studiously bought Fun Ho trucks for our daughters. <i>(1990,Out of the Frying Pan,165</i>)

### fun parlour
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Only the dairy and the take-away still open. Down the other end usual crowd round the fun parlour but where I was, nobody else. <i>(1983,Murder to Burn,94</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Internet search Jan 2006: global Eng.??]

### funder
 
 <i>n.</i> a funding organisation

>  ...producer Dave Gibson claimed that TVNZ was lobbying the government to have funder NZ On Air disestablished and for TVNZ to be funded directly. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### fungus
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The New Zealand fungus known to commerce is found upon various kinds of decayed timber in the North island, in what are called new bush settlements. ... IT is used by the Chinese for mixing with food, and for medicinal purposes. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,151</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [see also basket fungus, beech fungus, bracket fungus, brain fungus, bush-fungus, hakeke, strawberry fungus, tobacco pouch usu. Jew's ear fungus, formerly collected, dried and sold to Chinese merchants cf wood fungus]

>  <b>HAY</b> One fungus (<i>Hirneola</i>, sp.) is gathered here to a small extent for export to China. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/212</i>)

>  Piripi and Pene came to ask me to cart their fungus to the beach for them. ... [June 8] I shipped 4 bales of fungus for Piripi ... <i>(May 27, 1886,Chudleigh Diary,345,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The fungus belongs to the same genus as the European Jews'-Ear (<i>Hirneola Auricula-judae</i>), a tough but gelatinous fungus formerly in reputation as an ingredient of gargles. The New Zealand fungus now under notice (<i>Hirneola polytricha</i>), is well described ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/55</i>)

>  The exports for 1899 were: Wool, 4,819 bales; fungus, 107 sacks; <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,42</i>)

>  Often when household funds were low, a few shillings were obtained by selling fungus, otherwise known as `Taranaki Wool,' which was purchased by Chinese dealers ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,91,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> At one time Jacobs leased about 2000 acres of the usually useless [Chatham] Tobacco Country. He wanted it for the fungus that grew so abundantly on the kopi trees which formed the main portion of that forest. This he gathered to export to China. The very large, soft circular fungus could also be induced to grow on logs and roots split open and placed in an old tin shed. There copious watering soon covered everything with the huge white growth and all Jacobs had to do was to pull it off the logs and spread it out to dry. A pile a foot or so high would only yield a few handfuls of dry chips. When ready for packing it had to be damped once more to keep it from turning into powder. It never went mouldy because it was a mould to start with. When first sent abroad the Islanders got 4d. a pound for it, later only 2d. Even at that price they stripped the forests and killed the goose. Jacobs seems to have been the only one who attempted to cultivate it. The Chinese used it for soup (it makes a thick, sticky, rather tasteless fluid when boiled), dressing silk and varnish. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,145</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> Gum-digging and fungus-gathering went on at a furious rate; then there was the washing of the `nuts' (gum [138] too small to be scraped) and the sifting of the gum-dust (the scrapings), then the drying of the fungus - ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,137</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> For pocket money Stella and I collected fungus, the velvety grey fungi that grows generally on dead and rotting wood in the bush. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Aseroe rubra</i>

>  The flower fungus, <i>Aseroe rubra</i>.., belongs to a strange family of fungi called stinkhorns, named for their use of unpleasant odours to attract the flies which spread their spores. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/65</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Clathrus cibarius </i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> (And) Clathrus cibarius, <i>(1921,Tutira</i>)

>  <b>VAILE</b> <i> Shepherd's Basket</i> Clathrus cibarius. <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>ASEROE RUBRA</i>, stinkhorn fungus. Marie Taylor (1970) writes that this and other fungi advertise their presence chiefly by the horrible stench of the mucus containing the spores: .. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  "Chew Chong, based in Taranaki, exported Wood Ear fungus to China. In the mid-1800s there was a huge trade in fungus. It was at one stage larger than butter exports." <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Capital Times,19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Favolaschia calocera</i>

>  The attractive, orange pore fungus (<i>Favolaschia calocera</i>) is especially common on fallen dead twigs and branches in our native bush, and sometimes on dead wood in backyards. It does not cause a disease, but is rather a wood decay fungus. <i>(Mar 2003,Open Space,No. 60/23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> This latter [<i>Polyporus versicolor</i>] - the RAINBOW FUNGUS - derives its popular name from the concentric zones of various colours on the upper surface of each bracket. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,36,1961 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> basket fungus, fairies' closet <i>Clathrus cibarius </i>

>  <i>CLATHRUS CIBARIUS</i>, THE `BIRD-CAGE FUNGUS'. ... This fungus, known variously as `basket fungus,'`lattice fungus,', `Devil's purse,' <i> `tutae whetu,' `tutae kehua</i>,' belongs to the Phallaceae, a family included in the Basidiomycetes. It is found commonly throughout New Zealand during the late autumn and early spring. [Distribution.- Australia; Chile; ... New Zealand] <i>(Dec 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/427</i>) [NOTES: (Cunningham)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>CRUCIBULUM VULGARE</i>. Bird's nest fungus is found on dead twigs and old tree-fern debris on the forest floor. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Nidula candida</i> BIRDSNEST FUNGUS ... Tiny cups on dead wood or twigs ... with brown pellets of spore-bearing tissue nestling within, like eggs in a nest. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,490</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Little has been recorded of the bracket fungus [<i>Polyporus</i>] and its importance in the generation of fire. ... Dr G.B. Cone has spoken to an old Pakeha bushman who has used it in the Thames district where it was known as `punk' to the early settlers, who learned of its use from the Maoris. Punk burns very slowly and persistently. It can be put out only when smothered. ... Punk has now been recognised as the dried flesh of the <i>Polyporus hosularus </i>which may be found in many parts of the North Island. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,61</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>GYROMITRA ESCULENTA</i>. In spite of its name the brain fungus is reported to be poisonous, sometimes even deadly. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> basket fungus, fairies' closet <i>Clathrus cibarius </i>

>  <i>CLATHRUS CIBARIUS</i>, THE `BIRD-CAGE FUNGUS'. ... This fungus, known variously as `basket fungus,'`lattice fungus,', `Devil's purse,' <i> `tutae whetu,' `tutae kehua</i>,' belongs to the Phallaceae, a family included in the Basidiomycetes. It is found commonly throughout New Zealand during the late autumn and early spring. [Distribution.- Australia; Chile; ... New Zealand] <i>(Dec 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/247</i>)

>  [photo captions] (1) Honey mushroom (2) Red tobacco pouch fungus (3) Violet tobacco pouch fungi ... (5) Basket or lattice fungus. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Clathrus cibarius </i> [[a1927] Ver. = Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) NET FUNGUS =basket fungus ca1935 Marlborough]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Matakupenga ... 3. Clathrus cibarium</i>, net or basket fungus. = pukurau. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,188</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>SCUTELLINIA</i> (?). An orange-peel fungus. Any child's eye would be caught by such tiny, brightly-coloured cups set out against dark soil; in Britain they have the fanciful name `fairy bath'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,10</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(Northland)]

### funk
 
 <i>v.</i> funking vbl n -->(in marbles) childrens for encroaching your given mark, or not shooting with knuckles on the ground, or otherwise breaking a set rule; to cheat -->ca1930 NZ [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> [Waitara, Taranaki, 1899: reference, game of Little Ring] Many heated arguments arose as to who said `everythings' or `nothings' first. If a player's `taw' touched a marble and lay alongside it, the others would shout `knuckle down' just before his turn. ... `Knuckle down' meant that the player had to hold his taw close to the marble attacked and fire it in that very inconvenient position. ... When a boy lost all his marbles at play he was `scat' [?related to skint ?HWO] . ... A `ringer' was a taw which, when fired at the commencement of the game, lodged in the track of the ring itself; that, and knocking a marble out when he `fired' were the perfect shots. ... To fire a shot while holding the hand nearer to the ring than where the taw had lain, was to `funk', an unforgiveable crime. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,176</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Reverend Mother talks of when she used to give you marbles for being a good little boy - pee-wees and agates, and stonies and glassies and a big tor. And once she tried to play with you, and you said she funked because her knuckle was inside the ring.' <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,153</i>)

### funking
 
 <i>n.</i> [(marble term)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### funks
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  Action terms which refer to the shooting of the marbles are: Knuckle down Stiff, Knee High, Funks [Flunks sic] or Funnicks, Slips, Firsts and Lasts and Forces. ... The term `Knuckle down Stiff' (Taranaki [18]80, Gisborne [18]85, Waitara [18]95) is mentioned by [Lady A.B. Gomme Vo. 1, p.113]. ... As Taranaki [18]80 says, `The term was called out in order to prevent too easy a success against a marble near the ring'. It was less advantageous to fire with the hand on the ground than with the hand elevated, perhaps at `Knee High'. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### funs
 
 <i>n.</i> a game (esp. marbles) not played seriously, `for keeps', or to strictly agreed rules

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> <i>(1950,Confidence Corner,58</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> [Photo caption] Marbles became `in' or `out' capriciously but someome always knew the rules of the various games - `holey,' `liney' and, seen here, `ringey' - which could be played for `funs' or `keeps.' <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>exclam.</i> a cry when playing marbles for keeps, to indicate that the shot in progress is not for `keeps', or that after the game the marbles won have to be handed back; the expressions are `funs or keeps.' Sometimes pron funds

>  <b>FRAME</b> `Treasure' I played ringie and holie. I played funs and keeps. I had pee-wees and bully taws and changers that weren't made of glass mind you. And I won nearly every game because the kids were so dazzled by the look of my stinkers. ... They came for miles to play marbles with me ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116,1961 ed.</i>)

### fur seal
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [seal](sa-sho.md#seal))

>  Fur seals have been protected since 1894, but many perish each year after becoming entangled in fishing nets. <i>(Nov 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 54/46</i>)

>  .. the New Zealand coast was the haunt of the fur seal, which was regarded by seamen with little less favour than the sperm whale itself. <i>(May 24, 1867,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  Mr P. Thompson read a paper by Dr Hector "On the Fur Seal of New Zealand" and Mr J. S. Webb read one by himself on the same subject. <i>(Nov 4, 1871,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  This was the only fur seal we saw throughout the cruise. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  The bewhiskered New Zealand fur seal and Hector's sea lion were almost exterminated and have taken 150 years to come back. <i>Ibid.</i> 27 The Bountys are just a pile of lichened rocks, yellow tide-line bull kept their only vegetation. Discovered by Bligh in 1788 and ignored for 30 years, their New Zealand fur seals were then exploited mercilessly. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  The issue revolves around the continuing protection of cute, brown-eyed fur seals. <i>(Jul 26, 2000,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  It is pointed out that the monopoly protested against would curtail the means of employment of a number of residents of Stewart's Island, Riverton, and the Bluff, who for years past have been accustomed to join in fur seal expeditions. <i>(Apr 15, 1886,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  Provided certain rituals were observed at kills, and no blood was spilt, nor carcase flensed nor left upon the camping rocks, the fur-seal took no heed of man. <i>(Oct 19, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  On the rocks.. were the fur-seals - those of the Aucklands being famous for their thick fur. <i>(Mar 30, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,96</i>)

>  'Some assert,' said Mr Eitel, the secretary to Dr. Mawson's expedition, to the interviewer last week, 'that all the fur seals have been killed by man's cupidity' <i>(May 22, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,8</i>)

>  The seal bones present at the [Chatham Islands] site are largely Fur Seal, but contain some of Leopard and Elephant Seals, and he thinks it possible that the King Penguin may have arrived as the remains of a meal of one of these. <i>(1973,Notornis,XXIII/355</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> Seals of the Hair kind are numerous here [on Stewart Island]. ... The Furr seals are here also but not so plentiful as the other ... The Hare seal is a singular looking animal of the brownish colour. <i>(1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 357]

>  The hair seal, generally resort to places where there is plenty of fern growing and here they breed; ... they grow to larger size than the fur seal, which generally live on the barren rocks on the seaside. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,105,1986 ed.</i>)

>  FUR-SEALS, OR SEA-BEARS. This is in the southern seas `the seal' of commerce, and it is much to be regretted that so little accurate information was collected in former years about its life-history. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/257</i>)

### futtah
 
 <i>n.</i> alteration of Maori whata q.v.

>  Several of them.. have recorded details of their daily lives.. rats that necessitated the erection of futtahs (whatas). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,299</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station store [alteration of Maori whata.]

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Acland has defined the term "futtah" as a "station store built on high posts to keep rats and mice out. Both name and design are borrowed from the Maori." In the South Island especially this name, a corruption from "whata" was quite common. ..E S Elwell, who refers to the structure at Papakaio near Oamaru as "the futtah, or store, weatherboarded, and with a shingle roof...." <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,245</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Futtah: A food store built on high posts so as to deny entry to rats and mice. This system was learned from the Maoris and the word is a corruption of their whata. Pronunciation has also corrupted into watty. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,34</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> One of the very few survivors of the true futtah raised high on piles is that of Orari Gorge station.. The futtah is clad in weatherboards.. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,246</i>)

### futuparu
 
 <i>n.</i> rugby

>  futubaru cod-Maori for rugby, Maori being `hutuporo' for football. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,47</i>)

### future
 
 <i>n.</i> pot(-belly); corporation; pukunui [(? occas. use) ca1938 Auckland (? and Wellington) (R. Mason) (Presumably because it sticks out in front, or is what goes in front)]

>  <b>R. MASON</b> Future: (-1938 a `corporation', a `pukunui). The last time I heard this used was by someone who had lived in the Rangi-tikei, Auckland, and Wellington. It was quite familiar to me, but I could not recollect definitely any occasion on which I had heard it. Might have been in Auckland, or possibly from friends in the Rangitikei district. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Letter</i>)

### Fuzzrag
 
 <i>n.</i> a police newsletter

>  It's "Fuzzrag" - police newsletters that not only name offenders, but vilify and shop them for their previous list of convictions. <i>(Jan 16, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

### fuzzweed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> FUZZWEED (<i>Vittadinia</i> spp.) ... The Australian plant, purples fuzzweed (<i>V. triloba</i>) has flowers purplish turning greyish. The native (<i>V. australis</i>) has flowers a dirty white. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,59</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>purple fuzzweed [s] Vittadinia triloba</i> (Gaud.) DC. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,62</i>)
